Every generator but DBML, AML and Mermaid, which write types as is, reads it: Swift, Zod, JSON Schema, TypeScript, Java, Kotlin, Scala, Go, GraphQL and JPA for their types, C#, SQLAlchemy, TypeORM, Sequelize and Drizzle by name before their own tables and the vendor tables, PHP and Doctrine for its name sets and `enumMembers`, and Rust and SeaORM through `rustTypes.ts`, the Rust layer on top: sqlx's own choices (MariaDB's `UUID`, PostgreSQL's `oid`, `cid`, `xid`, `xid8`, `timetz` and intervals, the semi-structured types and SQLite's `BOOL`, `TIME` and `TIMESTAMP` as a `String`), the declared widths, SIGNED kept in the name and the vendor categories from before the tables were refiled (`rustPrimitiveType`), so Rust and SeaORM output stayed byte for byte what it was as the classifier moved (an owner decision of 2026-10-09), but for PostgreSQL's and SQLite's `dec`, which their vendor lists gained (below) and `rustPrimitiveType` now reads as a decimal (`rust.ts`'s Types, an owner decision of 2026-10-10). The vendor tables were refiled with it (an owner decision of 2026-10-09): SQL Server's `bit` a boolean and its `numeric`, `money` and `smallmoney` decimals, PostgreSQL's bit strings, `pg_lsn` and `money` strings, Oracle's `DATE` a date and time and its `REAL` a double, SQL Server's `binary` and Oracle's `RAW` filed as `varbinary` is; `getPrimitiveType` (`utils.ts`) reads an Oracle or Snowflake `NUMBER(p,s)` with a scale above 0 as a decimal and, on MySQL, MariaDB, PostgreSQL and SQL Server, `FLOAT(1)` to `FLOAT(24)` as a float and `FLOAT(25)` to `FLOAT(53)` as a double. SQLite's list gained `BOOL`, `DEC`, `TIME` and `TIMESTAMP` and PostgreSQL's `dec`, each in step with `schema-sql-parser`'s, so the column cell suggests them too. `utils.ts` holds the helpers the generators share: `splitLines` at `LINE_TERMINATOR` (CR, LF, CRLF, U+2028 and U+2029, where an ECMAScript line comment ends; C# adds U+0085), `fractionalNumber`, `baseTypeName` and `findDataTypeHint`. The SQL importer keeps MySQL's UNSIGNED and ZEROFILL in a column's data type (an owner decision of 2026-10-09), so an imported schema reaches every unsigned rule and its DDL keeps them, and drops SIGNED, the default, which MySQL and MariaDB store as the bare type and which, kept, hid the type's name from Rust, SeaORM and Doctrine, which read the whole name (a `TINYINT SIGNED` an `i32` where the bare type is an `i8`, and an INTEGER where it is a SMALLINT) and made DBML, AML and Mermaid quote the type (`packages/schema-sql-parser/AGENTS.md`); a type with UNSIGNED or ZEROFILL goes into every database's DDL as written, which PostgreSQL and SQL Server refuse, as they refused one typed by hand. A primary key column without the NN flag is NOT NULL in every generator (an owner decision of 2026-10-09) but DBML, AML and Mermaid, which transcribe the document, and DBML and AML import back (no importer reads Mermaid), so they keep a key's NN flag as the document has it (`"id" int [pk]`, `id int nullable pk`, `int? id PK`; an owner decision of 2026-10-10).
  - `sqlalchemy.ts` (`class Base(DeclarativeBase)`), `drizzle.ts`, `zod.ts` (`import * as z from "zod";`) and `swift.ts` (`import Foundation`) emit an import header, per table too, and `jsonSchema.ts` its `$schema`; `typeorm.ts`, `jpa.ts`, `sequelize.ts` and the plain generators of Java, Kotlin, Scala, Go and C# leave names unimported.
  - sqlalchemy, typeorm, sequelize, drizzle render faithfully: references by class name, duplicate table names kept (class names dedupe), an unnamed column as `""`, a keyless table as is.
  - **No TypeScript directive in a comment** (`escapeTypeScriptDirective`, `utils.ts`, an owner decision of 2026-10-10): every TypeScript-family generator writes a comment line TypeScript would read as a directive as plain text. `typescript.ts`, `zod.ts` and `drizzle.ts` write a comment as `//` lines, and each line whose text opens with `@ts-`, after any white space and in any letter case, takes a backslash before its at sign (`// \@ts-ignore`, `//   \@TS-NOCHECK`), so it reads as written and is no directive; `typeorm.ts` and `sequelize.ts` write a comment inside a string literal alone (`comment: "@ts-ignore"`), where TypeScript reads none, and need nothing (`typeorm.test.ts`, `sequelize.test.ts`). What the compilers read: `@ts-ignore` and `@ts-expect-error` in lower case on any line, with any text after them (`@ts-ignored`, `@ts-expect-errors`), hiding the error of the next line that is no comment or failing with TS2578 where it holds none, and `@ts-nocheck` and `@ts-check` in any letter case in the comments a file opens with, where `typescript.ts` puts the first table's comment, so one left as is would turn off checking of the whole file, followed by white space, a colon or the line's end on 5.9 and 6.0 and by anything but an ASCII letter or `-` on 7.0 (`@ts-nocheck1`, `@ts-nocheckß`); 5.9 and 6.0 skip any white space JavaScript's `\s` matches before the at sign (U+00A0, U+FEFF), 7.0 spaces and tabs alone. A line where `@ts-` follows other text (`see @ts-ignore`) or a `/` stays as it is. Measured on 2026-10-10 with TypeScript 5.9.3, 6.0.3 and 7.0.2 (`strict`, `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`, `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`), zod 4.6.5, drizzle-orm 0.45.4, TypeORM 1.1.2 and Sequelize 6.37.8: a table and two column comments holding each of 13 directive spellings (each case, each kind of white space, a suffix) under the eight databases, the whole text and each table alone, 600 files across the five generators, compiled with no error, where TypeScript's and Zod's had failed with 144 TS2578 each; an unknown name put after each of the 1,968 comment blocks holding such a line in the TypeScript, Zod and Drizzle files failed on all three compilers, none hidden; and every language's output for the 211 names of the vendor lists and the five `data/*.sql` fixtures under each database, 3,696 texts with the comment corpus, matched the output before byte for byte but for 1,344 comment lines of TypeScript, Zod and Drizzle, each opening with `@ts-` and given its backslash.
  - `sqlalchemy.ts` writes SQLAlchemy 2.0 declarative classes (`Mapped[...] = mapped_column(...)`) held to the editor's DDL on MySQL, MariaDB and PostgreSQL, and on SQL Server and Oracle for the types the rules below name: there a column takes the type that database's DDL names, from the core where the core spells it and otherwise from one `sqlalchemy.dialects.<mysql|postgresql|mssql|oracle>` module, so that `create_all` writes the editor's column and Alembic's `compare_metadata` reports nothing outside the limits listed below. Any other type, and every type on SQLite, Snowflake and Databricks, takes the portable type of its category. Types come from the shared classifier (`columnTypes.ts`: `getColumnType`, `BINARY_TYPES`, `JSON_TYPES`, `UUID_TYPES`, the time-zone sets) and, for the rest, from the shared vendor tables with the misclassifications the owner had fixed (SQL Server's `bit` a `Boolean`, its `numeric` and money types a `Numeric` or `MONEY` / `SMALLMONEY`, an Oracle or Snowflake NUMBER with a scale a `Numeric`, PostgreSQL's bit strings, `pg_lsn` and `money` no numbers); the UNSIGNED and ZEROFILL the importer keeps (an owner decision of 2026-10-09) reach the unsigned rules.
    - **Types.** MySQL and MariaDB never get a `String` without a length where the database takes the column, the spatial types aside (below): `ENUM(...)` is the core `Enum(...)` and `SET(...)` the dialect's `SET(...)` with `Set[str]`, their members read as MySQL reads a literal (`enumMembers`); a signed integer is the core `Integer` / `BigInteger` / `SmallInteger`, `TINYINT` and `MEDIUMINT` the dialect's, and UNSIGNED or ZEROFILL the dialect's type with `unsigned=True` (and `zerofill=True`), a display width kept only on `TINYINT(1)` and a ZEROFILL column, where MySQL still prints it; `SERIAL` is `BIGINT(unsigned=True)` with `unique=True`, since MySQL's SERIAL adds a unique index wherever the column stands (written once where the document flags UQ too); `DECIMAL ... UNSIGNED` the dialect's `DECIMAL`, `FLOAT` / `DOUBLE UNSIGNED` the dialect's with `asdecimal=False` on DOUBLE, which reads a Decimal without it; `TEXT(n)` and `BLOB(n)` the core `Text(n)` / `LargeBinary(n)`, so that MySQL picks the class for create_all as it did for the editor's DDL (`TEXT(70000)` a MEDIUMTEXT, `BLOB(100)` a TINYBLOB); `BIT(n)`, `YEAR`, `TIMESTAMP`, `DATETIME(n)` / `TIME(n)` (`fsp=`) and the sized TEXT and BLOB types the dialect's, `LONG` and `LONG VARCHAR` a `MEDIUMTEXT`, `LONG VARBINARY` a `MEDIUMBLOB`; `BINARY(n)`, `CHAR BYTE(n)` and `VARBINARY(n)` the core `BINARY` / `VARBINARY`, `CHAR` / `NCHAR` the core types, a national varying name with a length (`NVARCHAR(n)`, `NATIONAL VARCHAR(n)`, `NCHAR VARCHAR(n)`, `NATIONAL CHAR VARYING(n)`, MariaDB's `NCHAR VARYING(n)` and the rest) the dialect's `VARCHAR(n, charset="utf8mb3")`, the character set MySQL stores them in; MariaDB's `INET4` / `INET6` the dialect's (they need a `mariadb+` engine URL), its `UUID` the core `UUID`, and its Oracle mode types as MariaDB stores them: `CLOB` a `LONGTEXT`, `RAW(n)` a `VARBINARY(n)`, a bare `NUMBER` a `Double`. PostgreSQL: the smallint names a `SmallInteger`, `real`, `float4` and `float(1)` to `float(24)` a `REAL`, `char(n)` / `bpchar(n)` a `CHAR`, bit strings the dialect's `BIT` (a `str`), `money` its `MONEY` (a `str`), `xid`, `cid` and `xid8` a `String` (psycopg2 reads them as text and PostgreSQL casts no integer to them), `inet`, `cidr`, `macaddr`, `macaddr8`, `jsonpath`, `oid`, `regclass`, `regconfig`, `tsquery` and `tsvector` the dialect's, the ranges and multiranges the dialect's with `Range[...]` / `List[Range[...]]`, `jsonb` a `JSONB`, `uuid` a `UUID(as_uuid=True)`, `time(n)` / `timestamp(n)` a `TIME` / `TIMESTAMP(precision=n)` (with `timezone=True` on the zoned ones), and an interval an `Interval`, or `INTERVAL(fields=..., precision=n)` where it names them; an array is `ARRAY(element, dimensions=n)` with one `List[...]` a dimension, but only of the element types psycopg2, SQLAlchemy's default PostgreSQL driver, parses into a list (`postgresArrayTypes`, `dec` among them, and every interval): it hands any other array over as one string, which an `ARRAY` would split into its characters, so that one stays a `String`. SQL Server: `rowversion` and `timestamp` its `TIMESTAMP` (a `bytes`), the name Alembic reflects them by (the dialect's `ROWVERSION` it reports), `hierarchyid`, `geography`, `geometry` and `sql_variant` a `String` annotated `bytes`, the binary form pymssql reads them in, `money` / `smallmoney` its `MONEY` / `SMALLMONEY` (a `Decimal`), `nvarchar(n)` and its synonyms an `NVARCHAR(n)` (`NVARCHAR` for max), `nchar` an `NCHAR`, `ntext` an `NTEXT`, `binary(n)` a `BINARY(n)`, `varbinary(n)` and `binary varying(n)` a `VARBINARY(n)`. Oracle: `DATE` the dialect's `DATE` (a `datetime`; the core `Date` drops the time and `DateTime` compiles to DATE), every `TIMESTAMP(n)` the dialect's `TIMESTAMP` with no argument, since SQLAlchemy 2.0 takes a positional one as `timezone` (create_all writes TIMESTAMP(6)), `timezone=True` / `local_timezone=True` on the zoned ones, `NVARCHAR2(n)` and its synonyms, `NCLOB` and `RAW(n)` the dialect's, `INTERVAL DAY TO SECOND` an `Interval(day_precision=..., second_precision=...)`, `NUMBER(*,s)` a `Numeric(38, s)`; `ANYDATA`, `URIType` and `VECTOR` a `String` and `BFILE` a `LargeBinary`, each annotated `Any`, since python-oracledb hands them over as a `DbObject`, an `array.array` and a `LOB`. The portable types: the text types a `Text`, the binary ones a `LargeBinary`, JSON a `JSON` (`Any`), a UUID a `Uuid`, a time-zone timestamp `DateTime(timezone=True)`, an interval an `Interval` on PostgreSQL, Oracle and Databricks only, a string its `String(n)`, a decimal its `Numeric(p, s)`, and any other category its core type (`Integer`, `BigInteger`, `Float`, `Double`, `Boolean`, `Date`, `DateTime`, `Time`).
    - **Keys.** A table's only key column of Integer or Numeric affinity (`NUMERIC_NAMES`) without the auto increment flag gets `autoincrement=False`, since SQLAlchemy's `"auto"` would make it a SERIAL, an AUTO_INCREMENT or an IDENTITY the document never asked for, or a CREATE TABLE that MySQL (1063) and SQL Server (2749) refuse for a decimal or float key; a serial type on that only key column (PostgreSQL's serials, MySQL's `SERIAL`) gets `autoincrement=True`. The auto increment flag gets `autoincrement=True` only where the database numbers the column the editor's DDL flags, on a type of Integer or Numeric affinity, the only ones SQLAlchemy takes it on: PostgreSQL the names it takes an identity on (`smallint`, `int`, `integer`, `bigint`, `int2`, `int4`, `int8`) and the serials, SQLite a table's only key of an integer name (`isIntegerFamily`, which the DDL writes `INTEGER` with `AUTOINCREMENT`, so the model types it an `Integer` whatever name it has: SQLite numbers a key declared `INTEGER` alone, and the `BIGINT`, `INT8` or `UNSIGNED BIG INT` key a `BigInteger` makes create_all write takes no insert without a key), Databricks `BIGINT` and `LONG`, MySQL an integer, MariaDB an integer or a float, SQL Server an integer or a decimal of scale 0, Oracle (its sequence and trigger) and Snowflake (not measured) any number; of a composite key only the first such column, since SQLAlchemy refuses a second. Any other flagged column gets none, and a lone numeric key `autoincrement=False`. A flagged column gets no `server_default`, as the editor's DDL writes no DEFAULT on it, numbered or not. SQLite's only key column declared exactly `INTEGER` keeps `"auto"`: it is the rowid, which SQLite numbers itself. A key column without the NOT NULL flag is still not `Optional` (an owner decision, above).
    - **Names** follow the name cases, then become ASCII identifiers: any other character is `_`, a name not opening on an ASCII letter gets `x` in front (a leading underscore belongs to name mangling and SQLAlchemy's instrumentation), and a Python keyword, `metadata`, which `DeclarativeBase` owns, or `self`, the first parameter of the constructor it writes, which no keyword can pass, gets `_` after it. Class names are numbered (`_2`) against each other and against every name the header can import, `Base` and the builtins the annotations use (`int`, `str`, ...), since a class body sees module scope; attribute names, relationship attributes included, against the same names within their class. A column whose attribute differs from its name writes the name first, a dotted column name also writes `key=` (a `ForeignKey` splits its target on the dot), and a second column of one name folds into the first.
    - **Strings.** Every name, comment and default is a Python literal in the quote black keeps (single quotes only where the value holds more `"` than `'`), a backslash, line break, tab and every other control character written as its escape, so that a comment or a default keeps its line breaks and Alembic compares it equal. The class docstring alone puts the table comment on one line, each line break a space, stripped at both ends as black strips a docstring (one stripped to nothing is a space).
    - **Layout** is black's at 88 columns, counted as black counts them, two columns for each character of the East Asian scripts (Hangul, kana, the CJK ideographs, fullwidth forms): a call past the line puts its arguments one to a line with the magic trailing comma, and a `server_default=text(...)` or an `Enum(...)` or `SET(...)` too long for its line its own arguments likewise; an attribute whose call would open past the line puts the call on a line of its own inside parentheses where the target with `= (` fits, and otherwise opens the brackets of `Mapped[...]` onto lines of their own with the call after the closing one, which black writes otherwise (Written as is); a lone `__table_args__` entry stays on one line where it fits, since the comma of a one-element tuple keeps nothing open; the header's names are sorted as isort's black profile sorts them (the all-capital names first). Black still removes the blank line every whole text opens with, as it does from every generator's. Emoji and combining marks, which black counts as two columns and none, count one here, and a class name so long that `List["..."]` passes the line inside the opened `Mapped[` is not broken further (neither measured).
    - **Written as is, though create_all, Alembic or black shows it:** Black 25.11.0 rewrites the lines a very long name pushes past 88 columns, which the layout above leaves whole or breaks otherwise (measured on texts built for them; the measured corpus holds none): `__tablename__ = "..."` (a table name of 67 characters or more), which it wraps in parentheses; `class X(Base):` (a class name of 76 or more), which it writes `class X(` / `Base` / `):`; an attribute whose `name: Mapped[...] = (` passes the line, where the layout opens `Mapped[` / `str` / `] = mapped_column(...)` and black writes `name: (` / `Mapped[str]` / `) = mapped_column(...)` (column names of 76 to 81 characters on a `Mapped[str]`), and splits the call after `] = mapped_column(` from 82; a `ForeignKey("...")` past the line (a parent table name of 64 or more), whose argument it moves to a line of its own; and a `ForeignKeyConstraint` whose column lists pass the line, which it writes one name to a line. MySQL and MariaDB spatial types (`GEOMETRY`, `POINT`, `LINESTRING`, `POLYGON`, the `MULTI` types, `GEOMETRYCOLLECTION` / `GEOMCOLLECTION`) stay a `String` without a length, which create_all refuses (`VARCHAR requires a length`); the owner has yet to choose between `LargeBinary` (create_all writes a BLOB, values read as WKB bytes) and GeoAlchemy2. MariaDB's `VECTOR(n)` is a `String(n)`, so create_all writes a VARCHAR(n) and Alembic stops on it. An `ENUM` or `SET` member holding a backslash: SQLAlchemy writes no escape for it in the DDL, so create_all fails on a member ending in one and drops it from any other (`back\slash` becomes `backslash`), and reflection doubles it, so Alembic reports a modify_type for every such member, `\%` included, though rows the model writes and reads on the editor's table keep their values. MySQL and MariaDB `NCHAR` and `NATIONAL CHAR` stay the core `NCHAR`, the column create_all writes, but Alembic reports `CHAR(charset='utf8mb3')` against `NCHAR()`, which the dialect's `CHAR(n, charset="utf8mb3")` would not (an option left to the owner). A `TEXT(n)` or `BLOB(n)` MySQL stores in another class gets that class from create_all, but Alembic reports it (`MEDIUMTEXT()` against `Text(length=70000)`). `FLOAT(M,D)` and `DOUBLE(M,D)` lose their (M,D) and `CHAR(0)` becomes a `CHAR(1)` in create_all (Alembic quiet). A serial column that is not its table's only key (MySQL's `SERIAL` beside another key or in a composite key, PostgreSQL's serials likewise) gets no AUTO_INCREMENT or sequence from create_all, which Alembic does not report, and a PostgreSQL serial without the NOT NULL flag, which PostgreSQL makes NOT NULL, is `Optional`, so Alembic reports a modify_nullable. A PostgreSQL key flagged auto increment, which the editor writes `GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY`, is `autoincrement=True`, which create_all writes as a SERIAL, and Alembic reports a modify_default; the second of two such keys, which the model leaves unmarked, takes no value the model writes on the editor's table (GENERATED ALWAYS), where MySQL refuses the editor's DDL with two (1075). SQLite's flagged integer key is `PRIMARY KEY (id AUTOINCREMENT)` in the editor's DDL and `PRIMARY KEY (id)` in create_all, which writes no `sqlite_autoincrement`: that table keeps no `sqlite_sequence` and hands a deleted last id out again (1, 2, then 2 where the editor's table gives 3), and Alembic is quiet. Oracle's flagged column is numbered by a sequence and a BEFORE INSERT trigger, neither of which create_all writes, so an insert without the key fails on the model's own table (ORA-01400) and succeeds on the editor's; a flagged `VARCHAR2` key, a `String` SQLAlchemy takes no `autoincrement=True` on, has the key the model writes replaced by the trigger, and the ORM loses the row it added (`Instance ... has been deleted`). A comment holding a backslash on MySQL or MariaDB, whose DDL lets the database read it as an escape (`\n` a line break, `\d` a `d`), keeps the backslash in the model, so Alembic reports a modify_comment or add_table_comment; MariaDB keeps an integer's display width (`int(10)`), which the model drops (create_all writes `int(11)`; Alembic is quiet). The PostgreSQL types SQLAlchemy has no class for keep the portable type, which create_all writes in their place while Alembic stays quiet: `xml` a `Text`; `name`, `xid`, `xid8`, `cid`, a `bpchar` without a length, `pg_lsn`, `tid`, the geometric types, the `reg*` types but `regclass` and `regconfig`, `txid_snapshot` and `pg_snapshot` a `String`; psycopg 3 refuses any insert into such a column, NULL included, as it does into any array kept a `String`, while psycopg2 reads a multirange as text and refuses to write one. An array kept a `String` Alembic reports as a modify_type for most element types (`bit(8)[]` as `ARRAY(BIT(length=8))`, `money[]`, `macaddr8[]`, `tsvector[]`, `int4multirange[]`), though not for `xml[]`. SQL Server, where only the types the rules above name follow its DDL: `tinyint` and `smallint` are an `Integer` (create_all writes `int`), `real` and `float(1)` to `float(24)` a `Float` (create_all writes `float(53)`), `datetime2`, `datetime2(n)` and `smalldatetime` a `DateTime` (create_all writes `datetime`), `image` a `LargeBinary` (`varbinary(max)`), `xml` and `text` a `Text` (`varchar(max)`), and a bare `varchar`, `nvarchar`, `varbinary` or a synonym of one, one character long in SQL Server, a type without a length (max), all of which Alembic reports; `float` and `double precision` are a `Double`, which Alembic reports as `FLOAT(precision=53)` though the column is the same; `time(n)` and `datetimeoffset(n)` lose their precision (create_all writes 7; Alembic quiet); `sql_variant` (which Alembic reports), `hierarchyid`, `geography` and `geometry` a `String`, which create_all writes as VARCHAR(max) and refuses as a key (1919); a `hierarchyid` takes its string form (`'/2/'`) through the model but refuses the bytes it reads back (6522). A rowversion without the NOT NULL flag is `Optional`, though SQL Server makes the editor's column NOT NULL, so Alembic reports a modify_nullable, and a table holding two, or a default on one (a MySQL document's `TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` carried to SQL Server, as in sakila and Magento2-sales), is refused by the editor's DDL and create_all alike (2738, 1755). Oracle, likewise: `ROWID`, `UROWID`, `XMLType`, `ANYDATA`, `URIType`, the `SDO_` types and `VECTOR` are a `String` without a length, which Oracle refuses (ORA-00906) though the editor's DDL creates the column, and Alembic reports `VECTOR`; a `DbObject` or `LOB` the model read (`ANYDATA`, `URIType`, `BFILE`) cannot be written back through it (ORA-00932); `BOOLEAN` is a `Boolean`, which create_all writes as a SMALLINT, an integer `NUMBER` (bare, `NUMBER(p)`, `NUMBER(*)`) a `BigInteger` (`NUMBER(19)`), `FLOAT(p)`, `REAL`, `BINARY_FLOAT` and `BINARY_DOUBLE` a `Float` or `Double` (`FLOAT(126)`; Alembic reports `REAL`, `FLOAT(63)` and the binary ones), `BFILE` a `LargeBinary` (a BLOB), on whose table Alembic stops (`UnsupportedCompilationError`: it cannot render `BFILE`), a `VARCHAR2(n)` a `String(n)`, which create_all writes in CHAR rather than BYTE units, `INTERVAL YEAR TO MONTH` an `Interval`, which create_all writes as DAY TO SECOND, and `JSON` a `JSON`, which SQLAlchemy 2.0's Oracle dialect cannot compile. SQLite: Alembic reports the declared names SQLite reflects as another type, `FLOAT`, `REAL` and `DOUBLE PRECISION` against `Double`, `SMALLINT` against `Integer`, `INT8`, `UNSIGNED BIG INT` and a `BIGINT` or `INT8` with UNSIGNED or ZEROFILL (a MySQL document carried to SQLite) against `BigInteger`, `TIMESTAMP` against `DateTime`, and `NCHAR`, `NVARCHAR`, `CHARACTER`, `NATIVE CHARACTER` and `VARYING CHARACTER` against `String`, and a modify_nullable on every key column without the NOT NULL flag, which create_all writes NOT NULL and the editor's DDL does not. A flagged key the database numbers nothing on (a string or a uuid, a PostgreSQL `numeric`, a MySQL decimal or float, a SQL Server decimal with a scale or float, and sakila's MySQL integers on PostgreSQL, SQL Server or Oracle, which are a `String` there) has no `autoincrement=True`, so create_all makes its table where the database refuses the editor's (MySQL 1063, SQL Server 2749). A keyless table (the mapper refuses it) and a one-table view of a table with relationships, whose related classes are not in the text, configure only beside the rest of the module.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-10 with SQLAlchemy 2.0.54, Alembic 1.16.5, psycopg2 2.9.12, psycopg 3.2.13, PyMySQL 1.2.3, pymssql 2.4.3 and oracledb 4.0.2 on Python 3.9.6, against PostgreSQL 17.11, MySQL 8.4.11, MariaDB 11.4.13, SQL Server 2022 (16.0.4295.3), Oracle AI Database 26ai Free (23.26.3) and SQLite 3.54.0, comparing what create_all and the editor's DDL made column by column and running `compare_metadata` against the editor's tables, one table at a time: every vendor type of the eight lists as NOT NULL, nullable and only key, and the argument, UNSIGNED and ZEROFILL variants of each database, showed nothing outside the limits above and the spellings the databases themselves refuse; PostgreSQL `dec(10,2)[]` and `dec[]` read and wrote lists of `Decimal` through psycopg2 and psycopg 3 (Alembic quiet); the model's own MySQL and MariaDB tables took a 70,000-character `TEXT(70000)` and a 70,000-byte `BLOB(70000)`; comments with CR, LF, CRLF and U+2028 breaks, quotes and Hangul came back equal on PostgreSQL, MySQL, MariaDB and Oracle (on SQL Server the editor's DDL writes a comment as a varchar literal, so a character outside the code page arrives as `?` and Alembic reports it). sakila showed nothing on MySQL but its FULLTEXT index, which the editor's DDL writes as a plain one both sides refuse, its customer comment's `\n` and a numeric `server_default`, which create_all writes as the expression `(4.99)` where the editor's DDL leaves the literal `'4.99'` (Alembic quiet); Magento2-sales nothing but MariaDB's display widths on the tables both sides created (both stop on the `FULLTEXT` its import reads as a column and on MySQL 8.4's refusal of a foreign key to part of a key); GNUBOARD5, YOUNGCART5 and OKKY (keyless tables set aside) nothing but the zero-date defaults MySQL 8.4's strict mode refuses on both sides. MariaDB's `VECTOR`, a type of 11.7 and later, was measured on MariaDB 11.8.9 in the review of 2026-10-09 and 10. The auto increment, `xid`, rowversion, object-type and `self` rules were measured on 2026-10-10 with the versions above: two rows inserted through the model into the editor's tables and into create_all's on all six databases for each flagged key shape named above, on SQLite under every integer name `isIntegerFamily` takes (`TINYINT`, `SMALLINT`, `MEDIUMINT`, `INT`, `INTEGER`, `BIGINT`, `INT2`, `INT8`, `UNSIGNED BIG INT`, `INT(10)`, `BIGINT(20)`, a `BIGINT` without the NOT NULL flag), Alembic quiet on each but the documented modify_nullable; `xid`, `cid` and `xid8` read and written as a `str` through psycopg2, which psycopg 3 refuses to write as it does for every type kept a `String`; `rowversion` read as `bytes`, inserted without a value and compared quiet by Alembic, where the `String` it replaced failed create_all as a key and showed a modify_type; `hierarchyid`, `geography`, `geometry` and `sql_variant` read as `bytes` through pymssql; `ANYDATA`, `URIType`, `VECTOR` and `BFILE` read as a `DbObject`, an `array.array` and a `LOB`; a `self` column written through `self_` on PostgreSQL, and every class of every whole text under the name cases constructed with each column attribute passed by keyword. Over 4,806 texts (the eight vendor lists, the variants, names under the four name cases, comment and layout cases and the five fixtures under every database, whole and per table) black 25.11.0 changed nothing but the opening blank line of the 104 whole texts, isort 6.1.0 (black profile) nothing, pyflakes 3.4.0 found nothing in a whole text, and every text but those the limits above name ran, configured its mappers and compiled CREATE TABLE and CREATE INDEX on its database's dialect.
  - `typeorm.ts` writes one class per table: `@Index` lines above `@Entity("<table>", { comment })`, then each column as `@Column`, `@PrimaryColumn` or `@PrimaryGeneratedColumn` with its TypeORM type as the first argument, or as `type:` in the options object where the typed overload refuses an option the column needs (`unsigned` on a fractional type, a PostgreSQL bit string's length, an interval's precision), and an annotation of what the driver hands the entity. A primary key, a serial, an auto-increment column or a NOT NULL column is `T`, any other `T | null` with `nullable: true`: a key without the NN flag reads as NOT NULL (an owner decision, above), and an auto-increment column as the identity or AUTO_INCREMENT column every database makes NOT NULL, but on SQLite, which numbers its key alone and leaves the flag on another column unwritten; `nullable: false` is never written. A key with the auto-increment flag is `@PrimaryGeneratedColumn`, naming its numeric type (`{ type: "int", unsigned: true }`), `"uuid"` for a uuid and nothing for another type, which then reads as `number`. It is `@PrimaryColumn` with the type and options any column of it takes and `generated: "increment"` where `@PrimaryGeneratedColumn` would lose what the column is: a numeric key that states a precision or scale (`decimal(10,0)`, Oracle's `NUMBER(10)`), which it has no option for, and on PostgreSQL a key of any type but an integer, a decimal or a uuid, or an array (`varchar(10)`, `timestamp`, `real`, `money`, `int[]`), which TypeORM would type as an integer and its sync drop and re-add as a serial, losing the key's data. An auto-increment column outside the key is `generated: "increment"`, and MySQL's and MariaDB's `SERIAL` is that and `unique: true`, the key its AUTO_INCREMENT needs; an auto-increment column, key or not, drops its default, as the editor's DDL does on every database but Oracle. A default is `default: () => "<expression>"` as typed, a comment `comment`, its line breaks written `\n`, a string literal in which TypeScript reads no directive (above). Relations: the owning side, on the child, is `@ManyToOne` or `@OneToOne` named after the parent table (`parent_<table>` on a self relationship) with `@JoinColumn([{ name, referencedColumnName }])` a column, typed `Relation<T>` and `| null` unless every foreign key column is NOT NULL or a key, its `onDelete` / `onUpdate` those `referentialActionSupport` keeps for the database but SET DEFAULT; the parent's side is `<child>List: Child[]` (`@OneToMany`) or `<child>: Relation<Child> | null`. An index is `@Index("<name>", ["<property>", …])`, with `{ unique: true }` where unique, an unnamed one `IDX_<table>` numbered around the names the document gives; a decorator longer than 80 columns breaks its options, then its arguments, one a line.
    - **Types** are read through `columnTypes.ts` and the shared vendor tables, by name first. ENUM and SET members are the type, `"enum"` or `"set"` with `enum: [...]`, read as MySQL reads a string literal (a parenthesis or a comma inside a member included), a control character written as `\n`, `\r`, `\t` or `\xNN`, annotated as the union of the members or, for a SET, an array of it. On MySQL and MariaDB UNSIGNED, ZEROFILL and SERIAL write `unsigned: true` on an integer or fractional type, and a synonym is written as the type the database stores (`INT1` to `INT8`, `MIDDLEINT`, `FLOAT4`, `FLOAT8`, `CHARACTER` and `CHARACTER VARYING`, the `LONG` forms as `mediumtext`, `LONG VARBINARY` as `mediumblob`, `NATIONAL CHARACTER` as `national char`, `GEOMCOLLECTION`, `SQL_TSI_YEAR` as `year`, `CHAR(n) BYTE` as `binary`); likewise SQL Server's standard spellings (`varchar`, `char`, `nchar`, `nvarchar`, `ntext`), Oracle's (`varchar2`, `char`, `nchar`, `nvarchar2`, `long`), SQLite's `BOOL`, `TIMESTAMP` and `DEC` (`boolean`, `datetime`, `decimal`) and PostgreSQL's `dec` (`decimal`), which TypeORM's drivers refuse or create as another type. `FLOAT(p)` is the type its width stores, with no precision: `real` or `double precision` on PostgreSQL, `real` or `float` on SQL Server, `float` or `double` on MySQL and MariaDB (`FLOAT(0)` and `FLOAT4(p)` too). A length is `length: n`, SQL Server's `max` `length: "MAX"`, an Oracle length in `BYTE` or `CHAR` units its number; a precision and scale `precision` and `scale`, `NUMBER(*,s)` precision 38 at that scale, a negative scale kept, `NUMBER(*)` a bare `NUMBER`. On PostgreSQL every interval is `interval` (its precision from `interval(p)` or `second(p)`), a time, timestamp or interval precision above 6 is the 6 PostgreSQL stores it at (with a warning), since TypeORM compares the precision it reads back, `timestamptz(p)` and `timetz(p)` are the long names the typed overload takes, a serial is its integer generated by increment, and an array is its element type with `array: true`, a date, time or interval array stating no precision. A type outside TypeORM's names takes one by its category (`varchar`, `int`, `bigint`, `decimal`, `double precision`, `text`, `timestamp`; a binary name `varbinary`), and a column with no type name, or with arguments alone, `varchar` (`nvarchar` on SQL Server), the type TypeORM reflects a `string` property as, since a nullable one's `string | null` reflects as an `Object` it refuses.
    - **Annotations** follow what each driver hands TypeORM: `string` for a `"date"` column (Oracle's DATE too), a PostgreSQL, MySQL or MariaDB decimal, a 64-bit integer (`bigint`, `int8`, `bigserial`, `serial8`, MySQL's `SERIAL` and `BIGINT UNSIGNED`) but on SQLite, PostgreSQL's `xid`, `cid` and `xid8`, and a time; `number` for a narrower integer, a float, PostgreSQL's `oid`, and a decimal on SQL Server, Oracle and SQLite, whose drivers hand over numbers; `boolean` for a boolean and SQL Server's `bit`; `Date` for a date and time; `Buffer` for a binary type, SQL Server's `rowversion`, `timestamp` and `hierarchyid`, MySQL's and MariaDB's `BIT` at any width and MariaDB's `VECTOR`; `object` for JSON, PostgreSQL's `point`, `circle` and intervals, and Oracle's intervals (an `IntervalDS` or `IntervalYM`) and `BFILE` (an oracledb `Lob`); `number[]` for a vector on PostgreSQL (`halfvec` too), MySQL and SQL Server. A PostgreSQL array is its element's annotation with `[]` a dimension where node-postgres parses the element (a numeric's as numbers, a date's as `Date`s), else the array's text, `string`.
    - **Names** follow the name cases. A character no identifier holds is `_` (letters beyond ASCII too), a leading digit takes `x`; a reserved word, and a class name TypeScript refuses or reads as a type operator (`number`, `object`, `readonly`, `keyof` …), take `_`. A class that would shadow an imported decorator, a global the emitted module reads (`Array`, `Boolean`, `Buffer`, `Date`, `Number`, `Object`, `Reflect`, `String`), a helper tsc emits (`__decorate`, `__metadata`), a CommonJS name (`__dirname`, `__filename`, `__esModule`, `exports`, `module`, `require`), `then` (a module that exports it is a thenable, which `import()` fails to load, TypeORM's loader of an `entities` glob in an ES module package included) or a member every object inherits (`__proto__`, `hasOwnProperty`, `toString`, `valueOf`, `toLocaleString`, `isPrototypeOf`, `propertyIsEnumerable`, `__defineGetter__`, `__defineSetter__`, `__lookupGetter__`, `__lookupSetter__`) is numbered (`Date2`, `hasOwnProperty2`), as is a repeated class: TypeORM keys plain objects by class name, so such a class fails its dependency graph at initialization (`hasOwnProperty`) or its grouping of the rows it saves. A property named `__proto__`, `hasOwnProperty`, `isPrototypeOf`, `propertyIsEnumerable`, `toLocaleString`, `toString` or `valueOf` takes `_` (`toString_`), keeping the column through `name:`, which is written wherever the property differs from the column; properties are numbered against the relation properties, and a second column of one name declares no property, its foreign keys resolving onto the first.
    - **Written as is, though TypeORM cannot use it:** a keyless table (`MissingPrimaryColumnError`), and Oracle's `BOOLEAN`, `BOOL` and `VECTOR`, SQL Server's `json` (which SQL Server 2022 refuses too; SQL Server 2025 and Azure SQL take it, not measured), MariaDB's Oracle-mode names (`NUMBER`, `VARCHAR2`, `CLOB`, `RAW`) and a type of another vendor (a MySQL dump's `tinyint`, `datetime` or `set` under PostgreSQL), which TypeORM refuses at initialization; columns its schema sync drops and re-adds, losing their data: PostgreSQL's `bpchar`, `name`, `oid`, `xid`, `cid`, `xid8`, `pg_lsn`, `pg_snapshot`, `txid_snapshot`, `tid` and `reg*` types (written `varchar` or `int`; with the auto-increment flag, key or not, all but `oid`, `xid`, `cid` and `xid8` fail the sync instead, and an array of one of those four is re-added as a scalar serial on every run), MySQL's and MariaDB's `TEXT(n)` and `BLOB(n)`, SQL Server's `varchar`, `nvarchar`, `char varying` and `(national) character varying` with no length (one character there, 255 in TypeORM) and `double precision(p)`, Oracle's `BINARY_FLOAT` and `BINARY_DOUBLE` (written `float` and `double precision`, both read back as `float`) and `ANYDATA` (written `varchar`), an Oracle `INTERVAL DAY TO SECOND` on every run (TypeORM strips only the first of its argument lists), an Oracle length in `CHAR` units (TypeORM reads the byte length, four a character in AL32UTF8) and every Oracle auto-increment column, key or not, which the editor's DDL fills from a sequence and a trigger and TypeORM re-adds as an identity, a key of another type as a number (a column outside the key of another type fails instead, ORA-30675); columns it alters on every run: Oracle's `NUMBER(p)`, `DECIMAL(p)` and `NUMERIC(p)` (a scale of 0 it reads as stated), a PostgreSQL negative scale (`numeric(10,-2)`), an ENUM member holding a comma, a quote or a backslash escape, a default it reads back in another spelling (`NULL`, `TRUE`); what it alters once: Oracle's `INT`, `INTEGER`, `SMALLINT`, `REAL` and bare `DEC`, `DECIMAL` and `NUMERIC` (an integer `NUMBER` made a bare `NUMBER`, which takes fractions), `NUMBER(*,s)` (made the `NUMBER(38,s)` it equals), unique and foreign key constraints (renamed to TypeORM's names), a comment holding a CR or CRLF line break (made `\n`), a SQLite type it spells otherwise (`INT`, `BOOL`, `TIMESTAMP`, `DEC`, a typeless column, a type with arguments: the table rebuilt, its rows copied), and the auto-increment flag, key or not, on a PostgreSQL type that takes no IDENTITY but a sequence's bigint default fits (a decimal, a float, `money`, a character type), which the editor's DDL leaves out (TypeORM adds the sequence, and NOT NULL outside the key); where synchronize fails: that flag, key or not, on a PostgreSQL type the sequence's bigint default does not fit (an array, a bit string, a boolean, `bytea`, JSON, a uuid outside the key, a network, geometric or text-search type, a date, time or interval, a range: `default expression is of type bigint`, the sync rolled back with the column and its rows), and into an empty PostgreSQL database an auto-increment column, key or not, whose type, or array's element type, is not an integer (`int`, `integer`, `smallint`, `bigint` and their aliases, the `int` that `oid`, `xid`, `cid` and `xid8` are written as included), a uuid or an ENUM (a character, decimal, float, `money`, boolean, `bytea`, JSON, bit string, network, geometric, text-search, date, time, interval or range type and its array: TypeORM writes it with no type, `syntax error at or near "NOT"`), MySQL's `SERIAL` outside the key (its UNIQUE index dropped first, which MySQL refuses on an AUTO_INCREMENT column), a SQLite generated key of any numeric type but `INT` and `INTEGER` (`AUTOINCREMENT is only allowed on an INTEGER PRIMARY KEY`; for a `BIGINT` one a saved entity's id is a string, a read one a number), a SQLite auto-increment column outside the key (TypeORM writes `AUTOINCREMENT` there, a syntax error), SQL Server's nullable `rowversion` (altered on every run, refused), Oracle's `URIType` (hidden columns it tries to drop), an index on a MySQL `TEXT` or `BLOB` column (sakila's FULLTEXT index, which the editor's DDL writes as a plain one and MySQL refuses without a key length); Oracle's `XMLType`, whose hidden columns stop the schema log (`typeorm_metadata` does not exist); what synchronize into an empty database cannot carry: a PostgreSQL auto-increment integer array, key or not (created as a scalar serial, which every later sync drops and re-adds), and its auto-increment uuid array or ENUM, key or not, or uuid column outside the key (created with no default, so an insert that leaves it out fails, and every later sync fails: `default expression is of type bigint`), a MySQL `BIT(n)` width (`bit(1)`), MySQL's and MariaDB's `ZEROFILL` (TypeORM has no option for it, so the column is only `UNSIGNED`), a PostgreSQL interval's fields and a date or time array's precision, an Oracle length's `CHAR` unit; reads that differ from the annotation: Oracle's `DATE`, whose time of day TypeORM's `"date"` drops, and SQL Server's `sql_variant`, annotated `string`, which reads as the stored value's own type (a `Date`, a `number`); on Oracle a table whose DDL did not quote its lowercase name (TypeORM quotes every name, so its sync creates a second table); Oracle's `SDO_` types, written `varchar` and not measured (the Oracle Free image has no Spatial types); Snowflake and Databricks, which TypeORM has no driver for, written by the same rules.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-10 with TypeORM 1.1.2 and reflect-metadata 0.2.2, pg 8.23.1, mysql2 3.24.5, mssql 12.7.4 (tedious 20.3.3), oracledb 7.0.1 thin and better-sqlite3 12.11.1: tsc 5.9.3, 6.0.3 and 7.0.2 (`strict` but `strictPropertyInitialization`, `experimentalDecorators`, `emitDecoratorMetadata`, `nodenext`) compiled 27,201 outputs with no error, where a file of two deliberate errors failed on each: 10,156 type modules, each vendor list type and its `(10)` and `(10,2)` in all eight databases, with PostgreSQL's `[]` and `[][]`, MySQL's and MariaDB's UNSIGNED and ZEROFILL, ENUM escapes and other spellings, as a NOT NULL, a nullable and a unique column, a key without the NN flag, a generated key and an auto-increment column outside the key, whole and a table at a time; 320 fixture modules, the five `data/*.sql` fixtures in all eight databases and the four name cases, whole and their tables' views together; 16,725 name modules, 207 names (keywords, globals, `Object.prototype` members, `then`, names no identifier holds) as table and column names in all sixteen pairs of name cases on PostgreSQL, MySQL, SQL Server, Oracle and SQLite, with relations, a self relation and comments of every line terminator. Every one of them loaded as CommonJS and as an ES module, and TypeORM's `entities` glob loaded the `then2` class in an ES module package. Against the editor's DDL on PostgreSQL 17.11, MySQL 8.4.11, MariaDB 11.4.13, SQL Server 2022 CU27, Oracle 23.26.3 Free (names quoted) and SQLite 3.53.2, TypeORM initialized every type the database accepted but those refused above, and its schema log was empty for 282 of 346 on PostgreSQL, 180 of 185 on MySQL, 212 of 215 on MariaDB, 73 of 82 on SQL Server, 48 of 77 on Oracle and 47 of 95 on SQLite, the rest under Written as is; a generated key and an auto-increment column outside the key left it empty for all 22 tables SQL Server accepted and for PostgreSQL's integer types; on PostgreSQL a generated key and an auto-increment column outside the key of each of 235 types and spellings (the vendor list, its arrays and sized forms), each with a row in it, came out alike but a uuid, whose key takes the uuid strategy: TypeORM's log dropped the column only where the type is written as another (`oid`, `xid8`, `cid` and `xid`, the last two refused as a key, and their arrays on every run), a sync that failed rolled back keeping the column and its row, and a character, float, decimal or `money` key converged with a sequence and saved and read back the value its annotation names (`"1"` for `varchar(10)`, `1` for `real`, `"$1.00"` for `money`); into an empty PostgreSQL database a generated key and an auto-increment column outside the key of each of 39 types and spellings (each category, arrays of an integer, a uuid and three others, an ENUM) failed, was created as a scalar serial or without a default, or came out stable, as Written as is says, a uuid key stable with `uuid_generate_v4()`; a generated key for 62 of the 65 MySQL accepted (`BOOL`, `BOOLEAN` and `SERIAL` altered once), and on MySQL and MariaDB the tables TypeORM itself made for an auto-increment column outside the key of each integer or boolean type (on MariaDB each float type too) stayed as made after one synchronize; a row read through a repository matched its annotation for each probed type (61 on PostgreSQL, 38 on MySQL, 23 on MariaDB, 41 on SQL Server, 36 on Oracle, 21 on SQLite, and a sized generated key on PostgreSQL and SQL Server) but `sql_variant`, while the vector annotations rest on the drivers' sources, none of these servers having a vector type; a column with no type initialized, was created by synchronize and read back on all six; a class named after each `Object.prototype` member saved and loaded its relation on SQLite; the fixtures on MySQL and MariaDB initialized, keyless tables left out, with a schema log that changed no column's type, only defaults and constraint names, and OKKY, GNUBOARD5 and YOUNGCART5 synchronized to an empty log on MariaDB.
  - `sequelize.ts` writes Sequelize 6 models, `class X extends Model<InferAttributes<X>, InferCreationAttributes<X>>` with a `declare` per column and relation and `X.init(attributes, { sequelize, tableName, timestamps: false })`, then the associations, run after every `init`, a composite foreign key getting none; `DataTypes`, `Model`, `Range` and the other names it uses stay unimported and `sequelize` is the application's instance. A column's `type` is the `DataTypes` member that makes Sequelize create the column's own type on the document's database, unless that member cannot write the value a read hands over, and its `declare` is what the driver hands over on a read under Sequelize, both measured per type against the real databases. A primary key without the NN flag is NOT NULL, and the importer keeps UNSIGNED and ZEROFILL in the data type (owner decisions of 2026-10-09).
    - **Types.** A type is read by name through the shared classifier (`columnTypes.ts`): its base name with MySQL's UNSIGNED, ZEROFILL and SIGNED set apart, its numeric arguments, the PostgreSQL array depth, the ENUM and SET members, and whether it is a serial or a SQL Server rowversion. On PostgreSQL, MySQL, MariaDB and SQL Server a name the vendor list holds keeps a member only where that member's DDL is the type itself (`POSTGRES_MEMBER_TYPES`, `MYSQL_MEMBER_TYPES`, `MSSQL_MEMBER_TYPES`), with its arguments (`STRING(20)`, `DECIMAL(10, 2)`, `DATE(6)` for MySQL's `DATETIME(6)`, `FLOAT(7, 4)` and `DOUBLE(10, 2)` for a MySQL or MariaDB float of a precision and scale, `DOUBLE` writing `DOUBLE PRECISION(10,2)`, which MySQL reads as `double(10,2)`; a lone argument there picks `FLOAT` or `DOUBLE` alone) and on MySQL and MariaDB `.UNSIGNED` and `.ZEROFILL` on a numeric member (`SMALLINT.UNSIGNED`, `DECIMAL(10, 2).UNSIGNED`; ZEROFILL implies UNSIGNED); every other listed name goes in as the string the document holds (`type: "TIMESTAMP(3)"`, `"timetz"`, `"BIT(8)"`, `"YEAR"`, `"SET('a','b')"`, `"BINARY(16)"`, `"BLOB(100)"`, `"varchar(max)"`, `"datetime"`, `"money"`), which Sequelize writes into its DDL unchanged, since the nearest member would create another type (a TIMESTAMP as DATETIME, a BINARY(16) as a BLOB no key takes, a money as a float, a `time(3)` or `timestamptz(3)` without its precision, a MySQL `BLOB(100)` or `TEXT(50)` as a BLOB or TEXT where MySQL picks the TINY type by the length). The members kept beyond the plain ones: `REAL` for a single-precision float on PostgreSQL and SQL Server; on PostgreSQL `RANGE(INTEGER)`, `RANGE(BIGINT)`, `RANGE(DECIMAL)`, `RANGE(DATEONLY)` and `RANGE(DATE)` for `int4range`, `int8range`, `numrange`, `daterange` and `tstzrange` (a `tsrange` is written as is, `RANGE(DATE)` being a tstzrange), and `ARRAY(member)` for an array of one dimension whose element keeps a member but one pg hands over as one string (`tsvector[]`, the value `ARRAY` cannot write back: `values.map is not a function`), any deeper array or one of an element written as is being written as is (Sequelize writes a nested element in generic SQL, `datetime[]`); on MySQL and MariaDB `GEOMETRY`, `GEOMETRY("POINT")`, `GEOMETRY("LINESTRING")` and `GEOMETRY("POLYGON")`; on SQL Server `TEXT` for `nvarchar(max)`, `BOOLEAN` for `bit`, and `DATE` for `datetime2`, `datetimeoffset` and `time` at any precision, the one member through which tedious writes a `Date` (as text with an offset, which these types convert); on every database `ENUM(...)` for an ENUM list, each member read as MySQL reads the literal. An interval outside PostgreSQL is `STRING`: Sequelize has no interval member, and `TIME` makes a type Oracle lacks. Oracle always takes a member, since Sequelize builds an Oracle insert's RETURNING binds from it and a type string fails every `create()`; there a `VECTOR` is `STRING`, its arguments being dimensions and format, never a length. SQLite keeps `TIMESTAMP` as written, Sequelize reading a SQLite value into a Date only where its column is declared DATETIME. A name the database's vendor list does not hold takes, in order, the member of its name (`jsonb`, `inet`, `tsvector`, `uniqueidentifier`), what the classifier reads it as (bytes `BLOB`, an interval `STRING`), its list category with a first argument as its length (MySQL's `datetimeoffset(7)` `STRING(7)`), and `STRING` (`mood`).
    - **Generated columns.** A column with the AI flag, and a serial without it (PostgreSQL's `serial`, `serial2` to `serial8`, `smallserial` and `bigserial`, MySQL's and MariaDB's `SERIAL`), is `autoIncrement: true` and `CreationOptional<T>`; a serial is NOT NULL on PostgreSQL whatever the flags say, and on MySQL and MariaDB `unique: true` outside the key, the key AUTO_INCREMENT needs, which SERIAL implies. Sequelize allows one auto-increment attribute in a model, so a serial past the first, or beside a column with the AI flag, takes `defaultValue: () => Sequelize.literal("DEFAULT")` instead, as SQL Server's `rowversion` and `timestamp` do: Sequelize's NOT NULL check refuses an insert that leaves such a column out, SQL Server takes no value for a rowversion but DEFAULT, and a function default never reaches the DDL sync writes. Both are `CreationOptional<T>`, and a document default on them is dropped, as on an auto-increment column. Any other column with a default is `CreationOptional<T>`; a nullable column adds `| null` instead.
    - **Annotations** are what each driver hands over: a BIGINT `number | string` on MySQL and MariaDB, whose drivers send a safe integer as a number, `number` on SQLite, `string` elsewhere; a DECIMAL, numeric or money `number` on SQL Server and SQLite, `number | string` on MariaDB, `string` on PostgreSQL, MySQL and Oracle; JSON `unknown`; a date `string`, a timestamp a `Date`, a time of day a `string` but on SQL Server a `Date` on 1970-01-01; PostgreSQL's interval, `point` and `circle` an `object`, a range `Range<T>` with its bounds' type (`string` for `int8range` and `numrange`), `xid`, `cid`, `xid8`, `money` and the bit strings a `string`, and an array of a type pg parses no further one `string` (`POSTGRES_TEXT_ARRAY_TYPES`: the bit strings, the geometric types but `point`, the multiranges, the reg types but `regproc`, `tsvector`, `tsquery`, `jsonpath`, `macaddr8`, `name`, `tid`, `xid`, `cid`, `xid8`, `txid_snapshot`, `pg_snapshot`, `pg_lsn` and `xml`); MySQL's and MariaDB's spatial types an `object` (GeoJSON), BIT a `Buffer` (MariaDB's BIT(1) a `boolean`), a SET one `string` on MySQL and an array of its members on MariaDB, MariaDB's VECTOR a `Buffer`; SQL Server's CLR types, `rowversion` and `timestamp` a `Buffer`, `sql_variant` `unknown`, `json` and `vector` a `string`; Oracle's intervals an `object` (oracledb's IntervalDS and IntervalYM), BFILE an `object` (a Lob), a VECTOR the typed array of its format (`Float32Array`, `Float64Array`, `Int8Array`, `Uint8Array` for BINARY), any of the four where the format is `*` or not given, and an `object` (a SparseVector) where it is SPARSE; an ENUM the union of its members. Snowflake's and Databricks' annotations follow the shared classifier and were never measured, both being cloud-only: on Snowflake `BIGINT`, `NUMBER`, `NUMBER(38,0)` and a DECIMAL are `string` while `INT`, `SMALLINT`, `TINYINT` and `BYTEINT` are `number`, though Snowflake stores all of them as NUMBER(38,0) and Sequelize 6's snowflake dialect sets no `fetchAsString`, so snowflake-sdk most likely hands each over as a JS number (inferred from its documentation and Sequelize's source).
    - **Names** follow the name cases, a character outside `$`, ASCII letters, digits and `_` becoming `_` and a leading digit taking `x` before it. A class name gets `_` after it (`RESERVED_CLASS_NAMES`) where TypeScript refuses it for a class or reads it as a type operator in the class's own `InferAttributes` (`any`, `bigint`, `boolean`, `never`, `number`, `object`, `string`, `symbol`, `undefined`, `unknown`, `Object`, `infer`, `keyof`, `readonly`, `unique`), where a CommonJS module binds it already (`require`, `exports`, `module`, `__filename`, `__dirname`: TypeScript's CommonJS emit refuses the first two with TS2441, which `--noEmit` never reports, and Node all five with a SyntaxError), and for `__proto__`, which would replace the prototype of Sequelize's model registry (`sequelize.models`), after which a class named `prototype` fails at `init`; a property keeps such a name (`declare number: number`, `declare require: number`). Class names are numbered against each other and against the names the output uses (`DataTypes`, `Model`, `Range`, `Sequelize`, `sequelize`, the `Infer…`, `CreationOptional` and `NonAttribute` helpers, `Buffer`, `Date`, and the typed arrays an Oracle vector names, `Float32Array`, `Float64Array`, `Int8Array`, `Uint8Array`). A property named after a reserved word gets `_` (`class_`, `constructor_`), and one named after a member of `Model` or its `Hooks`, a field Sequelize sets on an instance or a model's prototype, a name every object inherits, or `prototype`, a key lodash's `baseSet` drops from the copies Sequelize makes, so that sync leaves the column out and every update leaves it unchanged (`_model`, `_options`, `_hasCustomGetters`, `_hasCustomSetters`, `dataValues`, `rawAttributes`, `toString`, `__proto__`, `prototype`; `MODEL_MEMBER_NAMES`), is numbered (`_model2`, `prototype2`), keeping `field`; a property keeping `_hasCustomGetters` or `_hasCustomSetters` would fail the module's load once a relationship refreshes the model, `refreshAttributes` assigning that prototype field through the attribute's setter. Relation properties are numbered in the same set, so an alias never reads as a prototype key, and an alias is numbered too where one of its accessors, a verb and the alias with its first letter upper case (`get`, `set`, `create` for `belongsTo` and `hasOne`, and `add`, `count`, `has`, `remove` too for `hasMany`), would hide a `Model` method or an attribute (`dataValue2` beside `getDataValue`, `attributes2` beside `setAttributes`, `team2` beside an attribute `getTeam`); where the alias or one of its accessors is an accessor of an alias named before it on the model, since Sequelize keeps the first of two accessors of one name and an included alias hides an accessor of its name (`hasRoleList2` beside `roleList`, `team2` beside `Team` under the `none` column case); and where it equals its class name ignoring case, the alias Sequelize gives the model's own table in a query, which an include of the alias repeats (`employee2` for the inverse end of a self-referencing one-to-one on `Employee`, `userList2` on a class `UserList`). Each alias's accessors are taken once it is named, so the result follows the relationships' order (`roleList` and `hasRoleList2`, or `hasRoleList` and `roleList2`).
    - **Strings** Sequelize hands the database, a type written as is, an ENUM member, a table, column or index name, a foreign key, an alias and a default, keep each CR and LF as `\r` and `\n`, since the database compares them; a comment writes CR, LF and CRLF as one `\n`, inside a string literal, where TypeScript reads no directive (above).
    - **Written as is, though Sequelize cannot use it:** a bare `char`, `character` or `bpchar`, and outside SQL Server a bare `nchar`, `national char` or `national character` (one character), a bare PostgreSQL `varchar`, `character varying` or `char varying` (no limit) and a bare SQL Server `nvarchar`, `national char varying` or `national character varying` (one character) as `STRING`, which sync creates as `varchar(255)`, `nvarchar(255)` on SQL Server and `NVARCHAR2(255)` on Oracle (SQL Server's bare `nchar`, `national char`, `national character`, `varchar`, `char varying` and `character varying`, which no member writes, are written as is, `"nchar"`); MySQL's and MariaDB's display widths, `TINYINT(1)` synced as `tinyint`, `INT(11) ZEROFILL` as `int(10) unsigned zerofill`, which pads to ten digits, and on MariaDB an `INT(10)` as `int(11)`; a value read as an object that Sequelize writes back as JSON text the database refuses, PostgreSQL's `point`, `circle` and `tsrange`, arrays of `point` and `tsrange` and a range array of two or more dimensions (`invalid input syntax for type point`, `malformed range literal`), and MySQL's `MULTIPOINT`, `MULTILINESTRING`, `MULTIPOLYGON` and `GEOMETRYCOLLECTION`, whose text a write takes only past the declared type; on SQL Server `datetime` and `smalldatetime`, written as is and read as a `Date`, which no Sequelize 6 member writes a `Date` to (tedious refuses one through a type written as is, `Invalid string`, and `DataTypes.DATE`'s text carries an offset these types refuse, `Conversion failed`), so they take the value's text, which the declared `Date` refuses at compile time (TS2322); the `DATE` member creating a `datetime2`, a `time` or a `datetimeoffset(n)` as `datetimeoffset(7)` at sync, and `numeric` synced as its synonym `decimal`; a value given for a rowversion, which SQL Server refuses (`Cannot insert an explicit value into a timestamp column`), though `CreationOptional<Buffer>` allows one; a serial past the first in a table, synced without its sequence (`bigint NOT NULL`); on MySQL a SERIAL without the NN flag, which the editor's DDL writes `SERIAL NULL`, a column MySQL leaves NULL on every insert and sync creates NOT NULL (MariaDB refuses `SERIAL NULL`); a table with the AI flag on two columns, which Sequelize refuses at `init` (`Only one autoincrement field allowed`); on Oracle an interval or a vector, read as an object that `STRING` refuses on a write, so a write takes its text, again only past the declared type (TS2322), the members creating other types at sync (`VARCHAR2` as `NVARCHAR2`, `DATE` and `TIMESTAMP` as `TIMESTAMP WITH LOCAL TIME ZONE`, `FLOAT` and `REAL` as `BINARY_DOUBLE`, `BOOLEAN` as `CHAR(1)`, `RAW`, `LONG RAW`, `BFILE` and `JSON` as `BLOB`, a bare `DECIMAL` as a NUMBER of no scale, an interval or vector as `NVARCHAR2(255)`), a native `BOOLEAN` failing every write (`NJS-016`), a `RAW` or `LONG RAW` every write through `BLOB` (`ORA-00932`), `LONG` and `XMLType` every write (`ORA-22816`, the RETURNING clause), and a native `JSON` column every read through `DataTypes.JSON`, oracledb handing over an object that Sequelize parses again; a column named `__proto__`, which Sequelize leaves out of sync, inserts and updates and pg cannot hand over in a row object, though its property compiles; a keyless table, to which sync adds an `id`; on SQLite a type name its list does not hold, stored by its affinity (`money` reads back as a number, `timestamp with time zone` as a string); on MariaDB under `sql_mode=ORACLE` a `DATE`, which that mode makes a DATETIME, read as a `Date`. MariaDB's annotations follow the mariadb 3.x connector; under 2.x a BIGINT past a safe integer is a `Long` and BIT(1) a `Buffer`.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-10 with Sequelize 6.37.8, pg 8.16.3, mysql2 3.24.5, mariadb 3.5.4, tedious 20.3.3, sqlite3 5.1.7 (SQLite 3.44.2) and oracledb 6.10.0. TypeScript 5.9.3, 6.0.3 and 7.0.2 with `--strict` (`nodenext`, no emit) compiled 25,859 files per version with no error: 3,387 type tables across the eight databases, every name of the eight vendor lists and other spellings (PostgreSQL's every listed name as `[]`, `[][]` and ` ARRAY` too), each type in one table as a key without the NN flag, NOT NULL, nullable, unique, with a default and with the AI flag; the five `data/*.sql` fixtures; 156 names (keywords, TypeScript's type names, `Model`, `Hooks` and `Object.prototype` members, the output's own names, what a CommonJS module binds, the typed arrays, a name opening on a digit, Hangul, an empty name, CR and LF) as tables and columns under the 16 pairs of name cases with relationships, and alias documents under the same 16 pairs; comments with every line terminator, 24 defaults, empty tables, an empty document, repeated names and a composite foreign key; each as the whole text and one table at a time, every table's text found in the whole text. The 128 whole names documents of 113 of those names also compiled with emit to CommonJS, the only mode that reports TS2441, with no error on all three versions, and Node loaded each of the 16 SQLite ones, running every `init` and association: of their 45,200 association accessors none was missing or hid a `Model` method. Against PostgreSQL 17.11, MySQL 8.4.11, MariaDB 11.4.13 and SQL Server 2022 CU27, each type's table was created by the editor's DDL, a value inserted, read back through the generated model and written back, every value's JS type matched its annotation (140 on PostgreSQL, 127 on MySQL, 132 on MariaDB, 72 on SQL Server), and the model's sync created the editor's type but for the shapes under Written as is (6, 8, 8 and 14 types); on SQL Server a create and an update of a `Date` passed for every date and time type but `datetime` and `smalldatetime`, and a create, a bulkCreate, a save and an update on a NOT NULL rowversion; on PostgreSQL, MySQL and MariaDB a create without the serial passed and sync created the editor's serial. Oracle 23ai Free 23.26.3 (73 types), SQLite (all 31 names its list holds), MariaDB 11.8.9 (VECTOR and `sql_mode=ORACLE`) and SQL Server 2025 CU9 (`json`, `vector`) passed the same read check, and on MySQL and MariaDB the five fixtures synced with the editor's column types but for display widths and the keyless tables' `id`. PostgreSQL 17.11 read, wrote back and synced `tsvector[]` and the `dec` spellings (`numeric`, `numeric(5,1)`, `numeric[]`); on SQLite 3.44.2 and PostgreSQL 17.11 every include of a self-referencing one-to-one and of `userList2` passed, `hasRoleList` stayed callable after including `hasRoleList2`, `prototype2` wrote its `prototype` column, and on PostgreSQL `getTeam()` and `getTeam2()`, the accessors of a child of tables `Team` and `team`, each read its own parent; the 16 SQLite names documents loaded, synced and included every association with no alias equal to its class name and no accessor shared, their remaining failures being the empty names, a table named `$x` (a SQLite bind parameter) and the `__proto__` column.
  - `drizzle.ts` maps PostgreSQL, MySQL and MariaDB, and SQLite to their cores and borrows `pg-core` for SQL Server, Oracle, Snowflake and Databricks, whose DDL drizzle-kit then writes as PostgreSQL's; `relationName` only where one table pair has several relationships or a relationship returns to its own table.
    - **Owner decisions of 2026-10-09:** PostgreSQL's `bytea`, bit strings, `money`, `xml`, `tsvector`, range types and `box`, and MySQL's and MariaDB's BLOBs, `BIT`, `SET` and spatial types are declared as their own types rather than approximated; a PostgreSQL array is an array; SQL Server's `bit` is `boolean()` and PostgreSQL's `pg_lsn` `varchar()`; every `bigint` and `bigserial` stays `mode: "number"`, an unsigned one too (a JS bigint, which `JSON.stringify` refuses, would set one integer type apart); a primary key without the NN flag is NOT NULL (here through `.primaryKey()`); the importer keeps MySQL's sign words in the data type, which it does for UNSIGNED and ZEROFILL (SIGNED it reads and drops).
    - **Types.** A type is read by name: the shared classifier (`columnTypes.ts`) gives the base name with MySQL's UNSIGNED, ZEROFILL and SIGNED set apart on MySQL and MariaDB (on the other databases the generator drops the three words itself, so `INT UNSIGNED` is `integer()` and `SMALLINT(5) ZEROFILL` `smallint()` on PostgreSQL), the PostgreSQL array depth, the ENUM and SET members, the integer width, the scalar and the shared name sets (`BINARY_TYPES`, `UUID_TYPES`, `JSON_TYPES`, `MONEY_TYPES`, the zoned time and timestamp sets, the PostgreSQL bit strings); then come the names one database reads apart from the rest of its core (PostgreSQL's declared types; MariaDB's `SERIAL`, `UUID`, `INET4` and `INET6`), the generator's table for its core, and the shared vendor tables' primitive. A type's arguments are read only where it has one argument list and each number is a safe integer, so Oracle's `INTERVAL DAY(3) TO SECOND(2)` passes no precision and a length past 2^53 none. An INTERVAL's number passes as `precision` only after `SECOND` or the `INTERVAL` keyword itself, the one place PostgreSQL reads a seconds precision (`INTERVAL DAY TO SECOND(3)` gives `interval({ fields: "day to second", precision: 3 })`, `INTERVAL(3)` `interval({ precision: 3 })`); after a leading field it is that field's digits, as in Oracle's `INTERVAL YEAR(2) TO MONTH` and `INTERVAL DAY(3) TO SECOND`, and is dropped (`interval({ fields: "year to month" })`), where drizzle-kit would write `interval year to month(2)`, which PostgreSQL refuses, or a seconds precision the document never gave. A declared type is a `customType` written once after the imports, sorted by name, a one-table view declaring only the ones its columns use: on pg-core `bytea` for every name in `BINARY_TYPES`, under the borrowed pg-core too; on PostgreSQL alone `bit` (a bare one `bit(1)`, as PostgreSQL stores it), `varbit` (its declaration names no global, since a table const may be `undefined`), and, each under its own name with `data: string`, `money`, `xml`, `tsvector`, `tsquery`, `box`, `lseg`, `path`, `polygon` and the range and multirange types; on MySQL and MariaDB `tinyblob`, `blob`, `mediumblob` and `longblob` (`BLOB(n)` the smallest that holds n bytes, `LONG VARBINARY` a `mediumblob`, a VARBINARY with no length a `blob`), `bit` (`data: Buffer`), `set` with its `values`, whose declaration doubles a member's backslash and quote, and the spatial types (`data: unknown`, mysql2 parsing them into points it writes no value back from); on MariaDB alone `uuid`, `inet4` and `inet6` (`data: string`, which mysql2 reads and writes). A PostgreSQL array chains `.array()` once per dimension (`integer().array()`, an enum's const too), takes a default only through `sql` and never an identity. Integers keep their width (`smallint`, `integer`, `bigint`; MySQL's `tinyint` to `bigint`), but the borrowed pg-core follows what the classifier says the database stores: Oracle's `INT`, `INTEGER` and `SMALLINT` and Snowflake's integer names are `bigint`, Oracle's `REAL` and Snowflake's `REAL` and `FLOAT4` `doublePrecision()`, Oracle's `DATE`, which holds a time, `timestamp()`, and Databricks' `TIMESTAMP`, an instant like `TIMESTAMP_LTZ`, `timestamp({ withTimezone: true })`. On MySQL and MariaDB a character set, a collation, `BINARY`, `ASCII` or `UNICODE` written after a type is read past, so the type keeps its builder (`CHAR(36) BINARY` and `CHAR(2) CHARACTER SET latin1 COLLATE latin1_bin` give `char({ length: 36 })` and `char({ length: 2 })`, `TINYTEXT BINARY` `tinytext()`). MariaDB's `CLOB` is `longtext()` and a `NUMBER` the classifier reads as a double (one with no numeric precision) `double()`, as its Oracle mode (`sql_mode=ORACLE`), the one mode that takes either name, stores them; `NUMBER(10,2)` stays `decimal({ precision: 10, scale: 2 })`, and MySQL, which refuses both names, keeps `text()` and `decimal()`. On MySQL and MariaDB UNSIGNED or ZEROFILL gives `unsigned: true` to an integer, a DECIMAL, a FLOAT or a DOUBLE; MySQL's SERIAL is `serial()`, unsigned by itself, and MariaDB's, which refuses the `AUTO_INCREMENT` drizzle-kit writes after `serial`, the `BIGINT UNSIGNED NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT UNIQUE` it stands for: `bigint({ mode: "number", unsigned: true }).autoincrement()`, then `.primaryKey()` on a single key and `.notNull().unique()` anywhere else, with no default. MySQL's `FLOAT(p)` is `float()` up to p = 24 and `double()` past it, as MySQL stores it, `FLOAT(p,s)` and `DOUBLE(p,s)` keep both, `DOUBLE(p)` neither. MySQL's and MariaDB's `LONG` and `LONG VARCHAR` spellings are `mediumtext()`, as they store them, and a zero length passes as `length: 0` (`char({ length: 0 })`, `varbinary({ length: 0 })`), which pg-core and SQLite, whose databases refuse it, drop. Under the borrowed pg-core `money` and `xml` stay `numeric()` and `text()`, and SQL Server's `rowversion` and `timestamp`, 8-byte binaries outside `BINARY_TYPES` that the shared tables file as strings, stay `varchar()`. A zoned name in any spelling (`TIMESTAMP_TZ`, `TIMESTAMPLTZ`, `datetimeoffset`) is `timestamp({ withTimezone: true })`, a precision from 0 to 6 passing as `precision` (pg-core) or `fsp` (mysql-core). MySQL's VARCHAR with no length is `text()`, and an INTERVAL is `text()` on MySQL and SQLite.
    - **ENUM** is a `pgEnum` on PostgreSQL and the borrowed pg-core, its const named from `<table>_<column>_enum` in the table name case and its type `<table>_<column>`, numbered past every other enum type and every table name of the document, since PostgreSQL gives each table a row type of its name (`user_role2` beside a table `user_role`); a `mysqlEnum` on MySQL and MariaDB, always naming the column; and `text({ enum: [...] })` on SQLite; each member read as MySQL reads a string literal. On MySQL and MariaDB a member is written with each backslash doubled (`ENUM('a\\')` gives `mysqlEnum("e", ["a\\\\"])`), since drizzle-kit writes a member into its DDL with only its quotes doubled, so a single backslash would escape the closing quote (MySQL error 1064) or turn `\b` into a backspace. Its DDL then stores `a\` as the editor's does, while the member's literal type says `a\\`, as a quoted MySQL default already does: a value read back (`a\`) lies outside the union, and inserting the typed `a\\` fails with `Data truncated for column` under strict mode. drizzle-kit pull writes the stored `a\`, from which drizzle-kit's own DDL is refused.
    - **Defaults and strings.** A default that is an ENUM member passes as a string (`.default("a\\\\")` on MySQL), a number literal as a number to a number builder and as a string to a decimal, a quoted literal unquoted to a string builder, and `true`, `false`, `1` or `0` as `true` or `false` to a boolean, PostgreSQL refusing an integer as a boolean's default (SQL Server's `bit DEFAULT 1` under the borrowed pg-core); any other default goes through `sql`. Apart from a MySQL ENUM member, a string the output writes holds the document's value, its backslash and quote escaped and each line terminator written as its own escape (`\n`, `\r`, `\u2028`, `\u2029`). A comment is one `//` line per line of the document's comment, a line TypeScript would read as a directive taking a backslash before its at sign (`// \@ts-expect-error`; No TypeScript directive in a comment, above).
    - **Names.** A table const follows the table name case; a character outside `$`, ASCII letters, digits and `_` becomes `_`, a name that cannot open an identifier takes `x` before it (`1st` gives `x1st`), a reserved word takes `_` after it (`class_`), and the const is numbered past every name the module holds: the helpers and builders it can import, `Buffer`, `Date`, `table`, `one`, `many`, its core's `Any…Column` and `…TableExtraConfigValue` type names, the custom types the document declares (a table `blob` beside a `blob` declaration is `blob2`, a MariaDB table `uuid` beside a `uuid` one `uuid2`), earlier table and enum consts, and each `<const>Relations`. Any other name is kept, globals such as `undefined`, `NaN` or `Map` included. A column property follows the column name case through the same repair, numbered within its table past `$inferInsert`, `$inferSelect`, `_`, `__proto__`, `enableRLS` and `getSQL`, and a relation property past `__proto__`, which an object literal reads as its prototype, dropping the column or relation (`__proto__2: integer("__proto__")`); the builder takes the column's own name as its first argument wherever the two differ.
    - **Written as is, though drizzle-kit alters the column or refuses the DDL:** on MySQL and MariaDB ZEROFILL as plain `unsigned`, which drizzle has no option for; display widths (`INT(11)` and `TINYINT(1)` give `int()` and `tinyint()`), which MySQL 8.4 keeps on `tinyint(1)` and ZEROFILL columns and MariaDB on every integer; `TEXT(n)` as `text()`, where MySQL and MariaDB pick `tinytext` to `longtext` by n characters of the column's character set (under utf8mb4 `TEXT(63)` is a `tinytext` and `TEXT(64)` a `text`, under latin1 `TEXT(255)` a `tinytext`); `BLOB(0)` as `blob()`, MariaDB's choice, where MySQL stores `tinyblob`; MariaDB's `YEAR(2)` as `year()`, stored `year(4)`, mysql-core's `year` taking no width; a SERIAL without the NN flag NOT NULL, as SERIAL stands for, where the editor's DDL writes `NULL` after it, which MySQL takes as a nullable column and MariaDB refuses; a key on a zero-length column, written NOT NULL, which neither database indexes, though MariaDB keys the editor's `CHAR(0) NULL`; an ENUM member holding a comma or an opening parenthesis, at which drizzle-kit's snapshot splits the type, so `ENUM('x,y','z')` comes out `enum('','','z')`, which MySQL refuses, and `ENUM('x(y','z')` comes out `enum('''')`; a composite key or foreign key name drizzle-kit builds from table and column names, past MySQL's 64 characters in `data/Magento2-sales.sql`; the character set and collation written after a type, which no mysql-core builder takes, so drizzle-kit's column takes the database's default (`CHAR(2) CHARACTER SET latin1` comes out `utf8mb4`, `CHAR(36) BINARY` without its `utf8mb4_bin`); a `BIT(0)` as `bit({ length: 0 })`, which MySQL refuses (error 3013, Invalid size) as it refuses the editor's `BIT(0)`, and MariaDB stores as `bit(1)`, as it stores the editor's. On PostgreSQL `"char"`, `name`, `tid`, `jsonpath`, `pg_snapshot`, `txid_snapshot`, the `reg*` types and `circle` (which node-postgres parses into an object) as `varchar()`, `oid`, `xid`, `cid` and `xid8` as `bigint`, and a bare `bpchar` as `char()`, stored `character(1)`. Under the borrowed pg-core a length past PostgreSQL's 10,485,760 (Snowflake's `VARCHAR(16777216)`). On pg-core and SQLite an index name repeated across tables (`data/sakila.sql`'s `idx_fk_film_id`), which both hold schema-wide, so drizzle-kit refuses the pg-core snapshot and SQLite the second `CREATE INDEX`, as the editor's DDL repeats the name too. On MySQL, MariaDB and SQLite a table named after an `Object.prototype` member (`constructor`, `toString`, `valueOf`, `hasOwnProperty`, `isPrototypeOf`, `propertyIsEnumerable`, `toLocaleString`, `__defineGetter__`, `__lookupSetter__`), whatever the name case, which drizzle-kit's snapshot leaves out with no error, so its migration creates no such table, and an index of such a name, at which drizzle-kit throws (`Cannot read properties of undefined`); pg-core keeps both, and a column of such a name is kept on every core. On every core an empty table as `{}`, whose `CREATE TABLE x ()` MySQL, MariaDB and SQLite refuse; a default as the document gives it, a zero date included; a column named `__proto__`, or on MySQL, MariaDB and SQLite a table of that name, at which drizzle-kit's snapshot throws a ZodError, though drizzle-orm reads and writes the column and its relation; and a relation that takes a column's property name, a relation being named after the other table (`<table>List` for a many side) and numbered only past `__proto__`: the object literal keeps both entries, so `post` with a column `author` referencing table `author` gets `author: integer()` and `author: one(author, …)`, TypeScript types `post.author` as the column's number, and drizzle-orm's `findMany({ with: { author: true } })` returns the author row in its place.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-10 with drizzle-orm 0.45.4 and drizzle-kit 0.31.11: every name of the eight vendor lists and 315 more spellings, each as a NOT NULL and a nullable column, a key without the NN flag and an auto-increment key; 24 ENUM and 6 SET member lists; 130 awkward names under the four name cases with relationships and indexes, again with `__proto__` left out; comments with CR, LF, CRLF, U+2028 and U+2029, and lines TypeScript reads as directives after each kind of whitespace; defaults; empty tables; the five `data/*.sql` fixtures; and per database an enum beside a table of its type's name, SERIAL keys and columns, zero lengths and a `__proto__` column, for the eight databases: 208 whole texts and 15,519 one-table views. Under TypeScript 5.9.3, 6.0.3 and 7.0.2 strict the whole texts compiled with no error, and the one-table views' only errors name a sibling table const the view does not declare (a sibling named like a global, such as `File`, `Blob`, `Array` or a PascalCase table `set`'s `Set`, resolving to that global). drizzle-kit's DDL ran beside the editor's on PostgreSQL 17.11, MySQL 8.4.11, MariaDB 11.4.13 (`CLOB` and `NUMBER` under `sql_mode=ORACLE` too) and SQLite 3.53.0 (`node:sqlite`, Node 24.16.0): it created every table the editor's DDL created but, on MySQL and MariaDB, the ENUM with a comma, Magento's key name and a UNIQUE on a zero-length column, which the editor's DDL adds in a statement of its own that fails too, on MariaDB a key on a zero-length column, and on MySQL, MariaDB and SQLite the tables named after `Object.prototype` members, besides the `__proto__` column and table (the names corpus, whose editor DDL under no bracket stops at reserved words, was compared through drizzle-kit's statements); its column types differed from the editor's only for the names under Written as is (29 on PostgreSQL, `"char"`, `pg_lsn` and the `TIME(7)`, `TIME(9)` and `TIMESTAMP(9)` PostgreSQL caps at 6 among them;
