15 on MySQL; 20 on MariaDB), the ENUM with a parenthesis and the fixtures' display widths, and in character set and collation where the type named one; its NOT NULL and defaults differed nowhere but at a SERIAL without the NN flag on MySQL, and on SQLite, which keeps a default's text, `true` where the editor writes `1`, both storing 1. drizzle-orm read and wrote a `__proto__2` column and its relation on PostgreSQL through node-postgres 8.23.1, and MariaDB's `uuid`, `inet6` and `inet4` as strings and a spelt-out SERIAL through mysql2 3.24.5, where on MySQL 8.4 and MariaDB 11.4 a member written `"a\\\\"` (the value `a\\`) stored and read back `a\`, and the typed value was refused on insert; through node-postgres it returned a post's `author` column as 7 from `findMany()` and as the author row from `findMany({ with: { author: true } })`, which TypeScript 5.9.3 still typed as a number.
  - `graphql.ts` writes one `type` per table, sorted by name, and before them a `scalar` line for each custom scalar a field uses, declared once and only when used (an owner decision of 2026-10-09): `BigInt`, `Byte`, `Date`, `DateTime`, `Decimal` and `JSON`, the names graphql-scalars gives them, though graphql-scalars has no `Decimal`, so a server serves one through the default identity serializer unless it writes its own. The one-table view (`formatTable`) is that type's block of the whole text, after the scalars its own fields use. A primary key or a foreign key column is `ID`, and a primary key is `ID!` with or without the NN flag (an owner decision of 2026-10-09), any other NOT NULL column `T!`; a foreign key outside the key leaves its field to the relationship. A table or column comment is a description in a block string, laid over several lines where it holds a line break or ends in a quote or a backslash, with `"""` written `\"""` and a lone surrogate written U+FFFD, which GraphQL's source text cannot hold; GraphQL's own block-string rules then drop a description's leading and trailing blank lines and common indentation and read a CR or a CRLF as an LF, so those do not round trip. A table with no field is a braceless `type X`, which parses and builds, though a server refuses an object type with no field until the table gets one, and the importer skips it.
    - **Names.** Every character a GraphQL Name cannot hold becomes `_`, a leading digit gets `_` before it, an empty name is `_`, and a leading run of underscores becomes one, since GraphQL reserves `__` for introspection (`__typename` gives `_typename`); where that leaves no ASCII letter or digit (a Hangul name), the description starts with the original name. A type name a built-in scalar (`String`, `Int`, `Float`, `Boolean`, `ID`) or a custom scalar the output declares already takes, or another table took after the name cases fold them together, is numbered (`String2`, `Date2` where the document uses `Date`, `User2`); a table named after a custom scalar the output does not declare keeps its name. A table whose type name is `Query`, `Mutation` or `Subscription` keeps it and so becomes the schema's root operation type: a server adds its own root fields with `extend type Query`, since a second `type Query` fails (`There can be only one type named "Query".`), and the importer, which skips root types, `PageInfo`, a name ending in `Connection` or `Edge` and Apollo Federation's and Hasura's helper names (`isNoiseTypeName` in `schema-graphql-parser/parser.ts`), leaves such a table out. A column whose field name an earlier column of the table took is dropped.
    - **Relation fields.** A relation field is named after the table it points at, except where one table pair has several relationships or a relationship returns to its own table, the condition `drizzle.ts` writes `relationName` under: there the single foreign key column names both sides without a last word `id`, words split as `isSingleWord` (`fkColumns.ts`) splits them (`buyer_id` gives `buyer` and the parent's `orderListByBuyer`), and a composite key, a name with no such word, or a stem that in the column name case keeps no ASCII letter or digit or sanitizes to a name opening with `__` (`회원No_id`; `__buyer_id` under NameCase none) keeps the table name (`parentEmployee` on a self relationship). A relation field whose name is taken is numbered (`user2`), except that a relationship outside that rule whose name another one outside it took is dropped. The importer pairs a singular field with the one reciprocal named `by` and its name before its other rules, the longer name first, then once more with the digits a numbered name ends in dropped (`pairByName` in `convert.ts`), so these documents import back with as many relationships, except where numbering leaves names alike: several relationships of one pair that no foreign key names, two keys giving one name on a one and a many side (`buyer_id` beside `BuyerID`), or a stem ending in a digit that numbering repeats (`buyer2_id` beside `buyer_id` where the parent holds a column `orderListByBuyer`).
    - **Types** come from the shared classifier (`columnTypes.ts`, an owner decision of 2026-10-09), by name before the shared vendor tables: an integer of up to 32 signed bits, or 16 unsigned, and MySQL's `MEDIUMINT UNSIGNED` are `Int`, since GraphQL's `Int` is 32 bits; `BIGINT`, `INT UNSIGNED` and wider, MySQL's `SERIAL`, PostgreSQL's `oid`, `xid`, `cid` and `xid8`, and every integer type of SQLite and every Oracle and Snowflake integer type but a `NUMBER` of stated precision, since those store any integer in eight bytes or a NUMBER(38), are `BigInt`, and so is a MySQL or MariaDB `BIT(2)` to `BIT(64)`, which the classifier reads as an unsigned 64-bit integer whatever its width. An Oracle or Snowflake `NUMBER` with no fraction is `Int` up to 9 digits and `BigInt` past them, a negative scale counting the zeros it adds (`NUMBER(7,-2)` `Int`, `NUMBER(8,-2)` `BigInt`); Oracle's bare `NUMBER` and `NUMBER(*)`, which keep any scale, are `Decimal`; Snowflake's bare `NUMBER`, a `NUMBER(38,0)`, is `BigInt`, while its bare `DECIMAL`, `NUMERIC` and `DEC`, the same type in Snowflake, follow the vendor table and are `Decimal`. `numeric`, `decimal`, `dec` and MySQL's and MariaDB's `fixed`, SQL Server's `money` and `smallmoney` and a NUMBER with a scale above 0 are `Decimal`, but PostgreSQL's `money`, which node-postgres hands over as the session's currency text (`$1,234.56`), is a `String`. Floats are `Float`. A boolean is `Boolean`, but a MySQL or MariaDB `BIT` or `BIT(1)` stays an `Int`, as `TINYINT(1)` does: mysql2 hands it over as a Buffer, which `Boolean` refuses as `Int` does, and the mariadb connector's `true` is served by `Int` as 1. Binary types and SQL Server's `rowversion` and `timestamp` are `Byte`; `json` and `jsonb` on every database and the Snowflake and Databricks `VARIANT`, `OBJECT`, `ARRAY`, `MAP` and `STRUCT` are `JSON`; `date` is `Date`; every timestamp and datetime type, Oracle's `DATE` and SQL Server's `datetimeoffset` among them, is `DateTime`. A time of day, `timetz` and an interval stay `String` (an owner decision of 2026-10-09), since graphql-scalars' `Time` refuses the `12:34:56` the drivers hand over and its `Duration` the object node-postgres builds; so do a PostgreSQL bit string, `pg_lsn`, `uuid`, `uniqueidentifier`, the spatial types and any type no rule names. A PostgreSQL array is a list per dimension with nullable items (`int[][]` gives `[[Int]]`, `[[Int]]!` when NOT NULL), but only where node-postgres parses that array (`PARSED_ARRAY_TYPES`, every name PostgreSQL accepts for its element, `dec` among them, and an interval of any fields); any other array, a bit string's, an `xid`'s, an enum's or a range's but `numrange[]`'s, is a `String`, the text node-postgres hands over, while `numrange[]` and `money[]` are `[String]`, the lists of text it parses them into.
    - **Importer read-back** (`schema-graphql-parser/dataType.ts`): `BigInt` imports as `bigint` (Oracle `NUMBER(19)`), `DateTime` as the naive timestamp (`datetime2`, `TIMESTAMP_NTZ`) but on Databricks as `TIMESTAMP`, which Databricks documents as its session-time-zone type (`TIMESTAMP_NTZ` being its naive one; from the documentation, not run) and the classifier reads as an instant, `Date` as `date`, `JSON` as `jsonb`, `JSON` or `VARIANT` (SQLite `TEXT`), `Byte` as `bytea`, `BLOB`, `varbinary(max)` or `BINARY`, and `Decimal` as `DECIMAL(38,18)` (SQL Server `decimal(38,18)`) on MySQL, MariaDB, SQL Server, Oracle, Databricks and Snowflake, whose bare DECIMAL holds no fraction (MySQL's is a DECIMAL(10,0) and rounds 12.5 to 13), `numeric` on PostgreSQL and `DECIMAL` on SQLite, which keep any scale. 38 digits is the most SQL Server, Oracle, Databricks and Snowflake hold, and 18 after the point leave 20 before it. A generated document imports back with the type of every field outside the key, but where a database has no column for the scalar: `Int` comes back `BigInt` on Oracle, Snowflake and SQLite, `Date` comes back `DateTime` on Oracle, `JSON` comes back `String` on SQLite, a list comes back as its item with a `list of X` comment, and a zoned timestamp comes back naive (`timestamptz` a `timestamp`, `datetimeoffset` a `datetime2`, Snowflake's `TIMESTAMP_TZ` and `TIMESTAMP_LTZ` a `TIMESTAMP_NTZ`), since `DateTime` imports as one, while a Databricks `TIMESTAMP_NTZ` comes back a `TIMESTAMP`. The importer keys a type on its first `ID` field alone (`resolvePrimaryKeyFields` in `convert.ts`), so a composite key's fields after the first come back as plain columns: `String!`, or no field at all where one is also a foreign key, which then serves its relationship alone (sakila's `FilmActor.filmId`). It adds a relationship's foreign key column under the field's name and the parent's key (`language` toward `language_id` gives `languageLanguageId`), so a relation field the foreign key names (Relation fields above) comes back with the parent key's name in it (`languageLanguage`), keeps its name where the parent's key is `id`, and takes the table's name where that key has no word `id` (OKKY's self relationship `parent`, toward a key `code`, comes back `parentCategory`).
    - **Written as is, though a server refuses it or serves another value:** a table with no field; a PostgreSQL interval, `point`, `circle`, `interval[]` and `point[]`, which node-postgres hands over as objects that `String` refuses; a MySQL or MariaDB `BIT` or `BIT(1)` through mysql2, a Buffer that `Int` refuses; a MySQL or MariaDB `BIT(2)` to `BIT(64)`, a `BigInt` that mysql2 and the mariadb connector hand over as a Buffer, which the default serializer writes as `{"type":"Buffer","data":[5]}` and graphql-scalars' `BigInt` refuses; a MySQL or MariaDB spatial type, a `String` that mysql2 hands over as an object or a list of them (`{ x: 1, y: 2 }` for a point; MariaDB's `MULTIPOINT` as null) and the mariadb connector as a GeoJSON object, which `String` refuses; SQL Server's `geography`, `geometry` and `hierarchyid`, which tedious hands over as a Buffer that `String` refuses; Oracle's `INTERVAL` types, `CLOB`, `NCLOB` and `VECTOR`, which oracledb hands over by default as an IntervalDS or IntervalYM, a Lob and a Float32Array, all of which `String` refuses, and its `BLOB`, a Lob that `Byte`'s default serializer passes on but no response can write as JSON; SQL Server's `time` through tedious, a `Date` on 1970-01-01 that `String` serves as its epoch milliseconds (`45296000` for 12:34:56); a `date` through node-postgres or mysql2, a local-midnight `Date` that serves as the day before east of UTC unless the driver's date parser is replaced; a PostgreSQL numeric array (`numeric[]`, `dec[]`, `decimal[]`), whose items node-postgres parses with `parseFloat`, so `[Decimal]` serves them rounded to a double (`12345678901234567890.123456789012345678` as `12345678901234567000`) and `NaN` as null, where a numeric column's value stays exact text; through node:sqlite, a SQLite `JSON` column, handed over as its text, which `JSON` serves as a string (`"{\"a\":1}"`) and not an object, a `BLOB`, a Uint8Array that the default serializer writes as `{"0":1,"1":2}` and graphql-scalars' `Byte` refuses (`Value is not an instance of Buffer`), and a `DATETIME` written with a space for the `T`, as SQLite's `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` and `datetime()` write it (`2026-10-09 12:34:56`), which graphql-scalars' `DateTime` refuses (`DateTime cannot represent an invalid date-time-string`); a `Decimal`, which no stock scalar validates.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-10 with graphql-js 16.14.2 and 17.0.2 (`buildSchema` and `validateSchema`): the 274 names of the eight vendor tables and 155 other spellings (lengths, precision and scale, negative scales, UNSIGNED, ZEROFILL and SIGNED, arrays, ENUMs holding quotes and escapes, an empty and an unknown type), each on all eight databases as a primary key without the NN flag, a NOT NULL and a nullable column; 73 table and column names (every GraphQL keyword, built-in and custom scalar names, Hangul, emoji, spaces, a leading digit, the empty name, `__x`) under all 16 pairs of name cases, with relationships and comments holding CR, LF, CRLF, U+2028, U+2029, NEL, control characters, `"""`, a trailing quote or backslash and lone surrogates; and the five `data/*.sql` fixtures on all eight databases: 4640 whole documents and 9656 one-table views, none failing on either version. Every view parsed on both versions, matched its block in the whole text, declared the scalars its fields use, and built where it names no other table's type. Tables named `query`, `mutation` and `subscription` built as the three root types on both versions, `extend type Query { users: [User] }` added a field to the first, a second `type Query` failed, and of eleven tables named after root, Relay and Apollo Federation types the importer left out the six whose type names it reads as root or Relay types (`Query`, `Mutation`, `Subscription`, `PageInfo`, `UserConnection`, `UserEdge`) and brought back the other five. Every type document imported back through `schemaGraphQLParserToSchemaJson` and generated again with the field types the importer rules above give (3432 round trips); the 40 fixture round trips kept every relationship and the type of every field outside the key, and differed, per database, in 35 composite-key fields that came back `String!`, 8 left to their relationship and 16 relation fields renamed, as the importer rules above say. On MySQL 8.4.11 a `DECIMAL(10,2)` column went out as `Decimal!`, came back `DECIMAL(38,18)`, and the editor's DDL of it kept 12.5, `12345678901234567890.123456789012345678` and `0.000000000000000001`, where a bare `DECIMAL` (`decimal(10,0)`) stored 12.5 as 13. PostgreSQL 17.11 reads `dec(5,1)`, `dec` and `dec(5,1)[]` as `numeric(5,1)`, `numeric` and `numeric(5,1)[]`; SQLite 3.54.0 (and node:sqlite's 3.53.0) gives `DEC` the NUMERIC affinity `DECIMAL` has. Served through the generated SDL and the default serializers (TZ Asia/Seoul): pg 8.23.1 rows of `numeric`, `dec`, `dec[]` (whose items pg parses into numbers), `money` (`$1,234.56` as a `String`), `xid` (`"7"`), `bigint`, `jsonb`, `bytea`, `timestamptz`, `int[][]`, `bit(3)[]` and `xid[]` (each a `String` of its text) served; `interval`, `point`, `circle`, `interval[]` and `point[]` failed (`String cannot represent value: { days: 1, ... }`); a `date` served `2026-10-08T15:00:00.000Z` for 2026-10-09. mysql2 3.24.5, on MySQL 8.4.11 and MariaDB 11.4.13, hands `BIT(1)`, `BIT(5)` and `BIT(64)` over as Buffers, `POINT` and `GEOMETRY` as `{ x: 1, y: 2 }`, `LINESTRING`, `POLYGON`, `MULTIPOINT` and `GEOMETRYCOLLECTION` as lists of such objects (a MariaDB `MULTIPOINT` as null) and a `DATE` as the same local midnight; the mariadb connector 3.4.5 hands `BIT(1)` over as `true`, served as 1, the wider BITs as Buffers and those six spatial types as GeoJSON objects; graphql-scalars 2.0.0's `BigInt` refuses such a Buffer (`Cannot convert [object Object] to a BigInt`). tedious 20.3.3 on SQL Server 2022 (16.0.4295.3) hands `geography`, `geometry` and `hierarchyid` over as Buffers; oracledb 6.10.0 (thin) on Oracle Database 23.26 Free hands its intervals over as IntervalDS and IntervalYM, `CLOB`, `NCLOB` and `BLOB` as Lobs and `VECTOR` as a Float32Array; each failed through the SDL as the Written-as-is list says. That tedious hands SQL Server's `time` over as a `Date` was measured on 2026-10-09 (SQL Server 2022, tedious 20.3.3); graphql-js serves such a `Date` through `String` as `45296000` for 12:34:56. pg 8.23.1's own parsers (pg-types 2.2.0, no server) read `{12345678901234567890.123456789012345678,0.000000000000000001,NaN}` as a `numeric[]` into `[12345678901234567000, 1e-18, NaN]`, which `numeric(38,18)[]`, `dec[]` and `decimal[]`, all `[Decimal]`, served as `[12345678901234567000,1e-18,null]` on both versions, and the same value as a `numeric` into its exact text, served as is. node:sqlite (SQLite 3.53.0) rows of `JSON`, `DATETIME` (written and `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP`) and `BLOB` columns, served through the generated SDL by default on both versions and with graphql-scalars 2.0.0's `JSON`, `DateTime` and `Byte` serializers on 16, gave what Written as is says; better-sqlite3 was not run.
  - `csharp.ts` writes plain classes of auto-properties with no `using` line and no namespace, nullable reference types the way `dotnet ef dbcontext scaffold` writes them (`int?`, `string?`, `= null!;` on a NOT NULL reference type or array), so the whole text and one table compile in an SDK project with `Nullable` on and its default `ImplicitUsings`, whose `global using global::System;` supplies `Guid`, `DateTime`, `DateTimeOffset` and `TimeSpan`. A primary key or a NOT NULL column is `T`, any other `T?`; a primary key without the NOT NULL flag still reads as NOT NULL, as in every generator but DBML, AML and Mermaid (above). A comment is `//` lines, one a line at CR, LF, CRLF, U+0085, U+2028 and U+2029, the blank lines around it and each line's trailing spaces dropped.
    - **Types** are decided by name before the shared vendor tables, which other generators read and which file some types under a category C# tells apart, then by the width `columnTypes.ts` reads. Under every database the binary names (`BINARY_TYPES`) go to `byte[]`, `uuid` / `uniqueidentifier` to `Guid`, the four time-zone timestamps and PostgreSQL's `timetz` to `DateTimeOffset`, `money` / `smallmoney` to `decimal`. MySQL's and MariaDB's UNSIGNED, ZEROFILL and SERIAL types take the unsigned type of their width (`byte`, `ushort`, `uint` for MEDIUMINT and INT, `ulong`), and their `BIT(n)` past one bit `ulong` (`BIT` and `BIT(1)` stay `int`), which MySqlConnector reads at their maximum. PostgreSQL's `oid`, `cid`, `xid`, `regtype` and `regconfig` are `uint` and `xid8` and `pg_lsn` `ulong`, which Npgsql reads where it refuses a `long` (the object ids) or a `string` (`regtype`, `regconfig`, `pg_lsn`). Every integer type of Oracle, Snowflake and SQLite is `long`, `SMALLINT` included, since they store up to 64 bits whatever the name declares; elsewhere `smallint` alone is `short` (`int2`, `serial2`, `smallserial` and Databricks `SHORT` stay `int`, an owner decision). A 64-bit float is a `double` whatever the vendor table says, so MariaDB's bare `NUMBER`, which its Oracle mode stores as a DOUBLE, is one. Under SQL Server alone `tinyint` is `byte`, `timestamp` and `rowversion` (binary there, a date and time elsewhere) `byte[]` and `sql_variant` `object`. The rest follow the shared vendor tables' category, which reads `float(p)` by its precision, a `NUMBER(p,s>0)` as a decimal and PostgreSQL's bit strings as text. It keeps `DateTime` / `TimeSpan` over `DateOnly` / `TimeOnly` and `string` for the spatial types, `hierarchyid`, `vector`, `xml` and `json` (owner decisions), and `string` for MySQL's and MariaDB's `CHAR(36)` (an owner decision of 2026-10-10). A PostgreSQL array is one rank a dimension in one rectangular array, before the element's own brackets (`int[]`, `int[,]`, `byte[,][]`), never a jagged one, which Npgsql does not read; outside PostgreSQL `int[]` is no array.
    - **Names** follow the name cases, a property's first letter upper-cased by `toUpperCase`, whatever the locale (WebKit's `toLocaleUpperCase` turns `id` into `İd` under a Turkish or Azerbaijani system locale, which .NET's `OrdinalIgnoreCase` does not match to `id`). C#'s reserved words, Roslyn's `__arglist`, `__makeref`, `__reftype` and `__refvalue`, and the contextual keywords it refuses or warns against as a type name (`extension`, `file`, `record`, `required`, `scoped`) take `@` where the name stays one, the `@` before the keyword and any white space around it kept (`public class @class`). A property C# reads as its class's name, which it refuses (CS0542: sakila's `country.country`, `city.city`, `address.address`), is renamed as `dotnet ef dbcontext scaffold` renames it, to the first of `<Name>1`, `<Name>2`, … that no other property of the class holds, a property renamed before it included, and carries `[global::System.ComponentModel.DataAnnotations.Schema.Column("<column name>")]` with the stored name as a C# string literal (JSON's escapes, and U+0085, U+2028 and U+2029, where C# ends a line, as `\u` escapes), which EF Core maps it through (an owner decision of 2026-10-10). The names compare as C# reads and compares identifiers: an `@`, Unicode formatting characters (Cf) and the white space and line breaks around a name aside (a column `country` followed by a space, a no-break space, a tab, U+0085 or U+2028 is renamed in class `Country`), save U+001A, which Roslyn alone reads as white space and the comparison keeps (CS0542 where it is the one difference); the attribute is spelled from `global::` so it needs no `using` line and a class named `System` cannot hide its namespace; a class with no name, or with white space alone for one, renames nothing. Any other name that is no identifier, or a repeated one, is written as is, as in `php.ts`.
    - **Written as is, though a driver reads it otherwise.** MySQL and MariaDB: a `string` for a `CHAR(36)`, which MySqlConnector reads as a `Guid` by default, so `GetString` and `GetFieldValue<string>` fail with `InvalidCastException`, Dapper with a `DataException` around it, and a value that is no GUID with `FormatException`, until `GuidFormat=None` in the connection string reads it as text (a `string` parameter writes either way); an `int` for a `TINYINT(1)`, which MySqlConnector reads as a `Boolean` by default, so `GetInt32`, `GetFieldValue<int>` and Dapper return 1 for a stored 2 with no error, until `TreatTinyAsBoolean=false` reads the number; a `string` for the spatial types, which MySqlConnector reads as `byte[]`. SQL Server: a `string` for `geometry`, `geography` and `hierarchyid`, which Microsoft.Data.SqlClient reads only through `Microsoft.SqlServer.Types`, so `GetFieldValue<string>` and Dapper fail with `FileNotFoundException` for that assembly and `GetString` with `InvalidCastException`; a query that selects `STAsText()` or `ToString()` reads a `string`. Oracle: a `TimeSpan` for an `INTERVAL YEAR TO MONTH`, which ODP.NET hands over as an `Int64` of months, so `GetFieldValue<TimeSpan>`, `GetTimeSpan` (on `'0-0'` too) and Dapper fail with `InvalidCastException`, while an `INTERVAL DAY TO SECOND` reads; Databricks's year-month intervals are `TimeSpan` too, not measured. ODP.NET's `GetFieldValue<T>` casts the value ODP.NET picks by precision and scale without converting it, so it reads a `long` only from a `NUMBER(10)` to `NUMBER(18)` (an `Int16` under 5 digits, an `Int32` under 10, a `Decimal` from 19 digits and for `INTEGER`, `INT`, `SMALLINT` and a bare `NUMBER`), a `decimal` from no `NUMBER(p,s>0)` under 16 digits (a `Double`) and a `double` from no `FLOAT`, `REAL` or `DOUBLE PRECISION` (a `Decimal`); `GetInt64`, `GetDecimal`, `GetDouble` and Dapper convert each. SQLite: a `TimeSpan` for a `TIME`, which Microsoft.Data.Sqlite hands Dapper as text that it refuses (`Invalid cast from 'System.String' to 'System.TimeSpan'`), while `GetFieldValue<TimeSpan>` reads it. PostgreSQL, what Npgsql refuses to read as written: a `string` for `inet`, `cidr`, `macaddr`, `macaddr8`, the range and multirange types, `tsvector`, `tsquery`, `tid` and the geometric types, each read as its own Npgsql type; `regclass`, `regproc`, `regrole`, `regnamespace`, `regoper`, `regoperator`, `regprocedure`, `regcollation` and `regdictionary`, read neither as a `string` nor as a `uint`; `pg_snapshot` and `txid_snapshot`, read as no type at all; `pg_lsn[]` as a `ulong[]`; an interval holding months as a `TimeSpan`; an array holding a NULL as `int[]` (only `int?[]` reads); a two-dimension column holding a one-dimension value as `int[,]`, since PostgreSQL enforces no declared dimension. Dapper reads a `bit(8)` or `varbit` into no `string` (Npgsql hands it a `BitArray`) and an `int2[][]` into no `int[,]` (an `Int16[,]`), both of which Npgsql's own `GetFieldValue` reads. PostgreSQL, what Npgsql writes only with an `NpgsqlDbType`: a `string` into a `bit` column (`Bit`), a `DateTimeOffset` off UTC into a `timetz` (`TimeTz`), a `uint` into an `oid`, `cid`, `xid` or `regtype` (`Oid`, `Cid`, `Xid`, `Regtype`), a `ulong` into an `xid8` or `pg_lsn` (`Xid8`, `PgLsn`). A `DateTimeOffset` off UTC into a `timestamptz` fails with `ArgumentException` (only offset 0 is supported) with `NpgsqlDbType.TimestampTz` too; `ToUniversalTime()` writes it, and Npgsql reads every `timestamptz` back at offset 0. Every database: a `decimal` for a value past its range (±7.9 × 10^28, 28 or 29 significant digits), which a `numeric` of no precision, a `DECIMAL`, `NUMERIC` or `NUMBER` past 28 digits and Snowflake's `DECFLOAT` can hold: on 10^30 Npgsql 10.0.3 (PostgreSQL 17.11) throws `OverflowException` and MySqlConnector 2.6.2 (MySQL 8.4.11) `FormatException` in `GetFieldValue<decimal>` and `GetDecimal`, Dapper a `DataException` around either, while a small value in the same column reads; that SQL Server, Oracle, Snowflake and Databricks fail too is inferred, not measured. Dapper and a renamed property: Dapper maps by name and ignores `[Column]`, so the property stays unset unless the query aliases the column (`country AS Country1`) or a type map reads the attribute.
    - **Written as is, though C# refuses or warns:** a class named in lower-case ASCII alone (CS8981, an error under warnings as errors), a property named after an `object` member (`Equals`, `GetHashCode`, `GetType`, `MemberwiseClone`, `ToString`; CS0108), a class named after a System type the output names (`Guid`, `DateTime`, `DateTimeOffset`, `TimeSpan`), which its own properties then name instead (CS8618 on `public Guid Id` in `class Guid`), and a class named `System` in the global namespace, which hides the `System` namespace from the attributes the SDK generates and from `global using global::System;` (CS0138, CS0426, CS0437, and CS0246 on a `Guid` property) but compiles inside a namespace; a project without `ImplicitUsings`, which needs `using System;`.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-10 with .NET SDK 10.0.401 (`mcr.microsoft.com/dotnet/sdk:10.0`, `Nullable` and `ImplicitUsings` on, `TreatWarningsAsErrors`, `WarningLevel` 9999): every type of every vendor list with extra spellings (419 names, the current and `main`'s lists, with lengths, precisions, UNSIGNED, ZEROFILL, arrays and ENUM / SET values holding quotes and escapes, under each of the eight databases, each as a primary key with and without the NN flag, NOT NULL and nullable), comments with every line terminator, an empty table and the five `data/*.sql` fixtures under the eight databases, whole and per table, 4,973 outputs each in a file-scoped namespace, built with no warning and no error, and the 55 whole texts again each alone in the global namespace; every keyword above as a table and a column name under each name case, and names that clash with their class, numbered past taken names, holding characters to escape or naming a class `System`, built with nothing but CS8981 on lower-case class names, CS0102 on repeated names, CS0108 on the `object` member names, CS8618 in class `Guid` and syntax errors (CS9008 for `@@x` among them) on names that are no identifier; table and column names with each of 18 white space, line break and formatting characters before, after or around them under every pair of name cases (2,592 outputs, checked with Roslyn), with nothing more but CS0542 where U+001A is the one difference and on the two missing names of a class with no name. With Npgsql 10.0.3 on PostgreSQL 17.11, MySqlConnector 2.6.2 on MySQL 8.4.11 and MariaDB 11.4.13, Microsoft.Data.SqlClient 6.1.1 on SQL Server 2022 (16.00.4295), Oracle.ManagedDataAccess.Core 23.9.1 on Oracle Database Free 23.26.3, Microsoft.Data.Sqlite 10.0.12 (SQLite 3.53.3) and Dapper 2.1.66, besides the limits above: the MySQL and MariaDB unsigned types and `BIT(64)` read at their maximum, where `int` or `long` overflow; SQLite's rowid 3000000000 and a `SMALLINT` holding 40000 read as `long`, where `int` and `short` overflow, and so does Oracle's `SMALLINT` holding 40000 through `GetInt64` and Dapper; `oid`, `cid`, `xid`, `xid8`, `regtype`, `regconfig`, `pg_lsn` and `oid[]` read as written, PostgreSQL's `money` as a `decimal`, `timetz` as a `DateTimeOffset`, `int[][]` as `int[,]`, MariaDB's `NUMBER` as a `double`, where a `decimal` drops the digits past 15; SQL Server's `sql_variant` as an `object`, `smallmoney` as a `decimal`, `rowversion` as a `byte[]` and `xml` as a `string`; Oracle's `TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE` as a `DateTimeOffset` keeping its offset, `RAW` as a `byte[]`, `BINARY_FLOAT` as a `float`, `JSON` and `XMLType` as a `string`; EF Core 10.0.12 reads and writes a renamed property through its `[Column]`.
  - `go.ts` writes one plain struct per table, sorted by name, each field typed for its database's usual `database/sql` driver and tagged `json:"<column name>"`, with no package clause, no import block (PR #424's choice) and no `db` tag, so a project imports `time`, `encoding/json`, `github.com/shopspring/decimal` and `mssql "github.com/microsoft/go-mssqldb"` as its fields use them; the one-table view is `formatTable` as in the other plain generators. A primary key, with or without the NN flag (an owner decision of 2026-10-09, above), or a NOT NULL column is `T`, any other `*T`, but `[]byte` and an array, which scan NULL as nil, are never a pointer (`json.RawMessage`, which database/sql cannot set to NULL, is). Fields are aligned as gofmt aligns them, counting runes, a run ending at a field with a comment line above it.
    - **Types** come from the shared classifier (`columnTypes.ts`, an owner decision of 2026-10-09), its scalars mapped in `SCALAR_TYPES`, with a few database overrides (`elementType`) where the driver hands over another value. Integers take the classifier's width (an owner decision of 2026-10-09): `int8` for TINYINT, BYTE and INT1 (MySQL's `TINYINT(1)` too), `int16` for SMALLINT, INT2, the PostgreSQL small serials and Databricks SHORT, `int32` for INT, MEDIUMINT and serial, `int64` for BIGINT; MySQL and MariaDB UNSIGNED and ZEROFILL integers unsigned at their width (`uint8`, `uint16`, `uint32` for INT and MEDIUMINT, `uint64` for BIGINT and SERIAL), YEAR a `uint16`, SQL Server's `tinyint` a `uint8`, PostgreSQL's `oid`, `xid` and `cid` a `uint32` and `xid8` a `uint64`; every Oracle, Snowflake and SQLite integer type is an `int64`, those databases storing any integer in a NUMBER(38) or eight bytes, and so is an Oracle or Snowflake NUMBER with no scale above 0, bare included. `FLOAT(p)` is a `float32` up to 24 and a `float64` above on MySQL, MariaDB, PostgreSQL and SQL Server, where PostgreSQL's and SQL Server's `real`, PostgreSQL's `float4` and MySQL's and MariaDB's `FLOAT`, `FLOAT4` and `FLOAT(m,d)` are a `float32` and MySQL's and MariaDB's `REAL`, a DOUBLE, and PostgreSQL's and SQL Server's bare `float` a `float64`; every Oracle, Snowflake and SQLite float is a `float64` (`FLOAT(24)` and Oracle's `REAL` too) but Oracle's `BINARY_FLOAT`, a `float32`; every Databricks `FLOAT` and `REAL` is a `float32` (`FLOAT(53)` too), its `DOUBLE` a `float64`; MariaDB's bare `NUMBER`, a DOUBLE, is a `float64`. numeric, decimal, dec and fixed, SQL Server's money and smallmoney, Snowflake's DECFLOAT, an Oracle or Snowflake NUMBER with a scale above 0 (`NUMBER(*,2)` too) and MariaDB's NUMBER with arguments are a `decimal.Decimal`; PostgreSQL's `money` is a `string`, the session's currency text (`$1,234.56`) that neither a float nor a decimal scans. BOOL, BOOLEAN and SQL Server's `bit` are a `bool`, but MySQL's and MariaDB's `BIT` of any width is a `[]byte`, which go-sql-driver/mysql hands over even for `b'1'`. Every binary type, SQL Server's `rowversion` and `timestamp` included, is a `[]byte`, but Oracle's `BFILE` a `string`; SQL Server's `uniqueidentifier`, stored mixed-endian, is an `mssql.UniqueIdentifier`, which alone reads it back as written and marshals it as its string, and any other UUID a `string`. `json` and `jsonb` are a `json.RawMessage` on MySQL, MariaDB, PostgreSQL and Oracle, whose drivers hand JSON over as bytes, and a `string` on SQLite, SQL Server, Snowflake and Databricks, as are the semi-structured VARIANT, OBJECT, ARRAY, MAP and STRUCT. A date and every timestamp, zoned or not, Oracle's DATE among them, is a `time.Time`, and so is a time of day on SQL Server and Snowflake; on MySQL, MariaDB, PostgreSQL and SQLite, whose usual drivers hand a time of day over as text, it is a `string`, and `timetz` and every interval are a `string` everywhere. A PostgreSQL array is one slice a dimension of its element's type (`int[][]` a `[][]int32`, `jsonb[]` a `[]json.RawMessage`); bit strings, `pg_lsn`, ranges, network and geometric types, ENUM, SET, spatial types and any type no rule names are a `string`.
    - **Names.** A table or column name is composed (NFC) before the name case, which would split a word at a lone combining mark, so a decomposed `émile` (`e` then U+0301) cases as the precomposed one, `Émile`, under every name case; the cased name's first letter is upper-cased, and where its first rune is then no upper-case letter it gets `X` in front (`X회원`, `X_id` and `X1st` under NameCase none, `X` for an empty name), since Go exports, and encoding/json sees, only such a name. Go takes a combining mark in no identifier. camelCase, pascalCase and snakeCase drop a name's own marks as they split it into words (under camelCase `ชื่อ` gives `Xชอ`, `नाम` `Xनम`), and a mark their casing makes is recomposed or dropped too (`İlçe` stays `İlçe`, `KİLO` gives `Kilo` and `KİLO_ชื่อ` `Kiloชอ`, under snakeCase `Kilo_ช_อ`); NameCase none keeps a name's own marks (`नाम` is `Xनाम`), and with them any its first letter's upper-casing makes, but drops that one from a name holding no mark of its own (`ǰx` gives `Jx`). Keywords, predeclared names and the packages the types name need nothing, since an exported name is none of them (`Type`, `String`, `Time`, `Decimal`). Any other name Go refuses, or a repeated one, is written as is, as in `php.ts` and `rust.ts`. The tag keeps the column name: `"` and `\` escaped, and every control character but a tab, and U+FEFF, escaped as an interpreted Go string writes it (`\n`, `\r`, a four-digit `\u` escape for the rest), since Go drops a raw carriage return, refuses a raw line feed, NUL or byte order mark, and gofmt ends an alignment run at a form feed; the tag is an interpreted literal where the name holds a backtick; a column named `-` is tagged `-,`, which encoding/json reads as the key `-`, where `json:"-"` would leave the field out.
    - **Comments** are `//` lines, one per line of the comment (CR, LF, CRLF, U+2028, U+2029), the table's above its struct and a column's above its field. Each line and the whole comment lose the spaces gofmt trims (Go's Unicode spaces, NEL included), a run of empty lines is one `//`, as gofmt folds it in a doc comment, and NUL and U+FEFF, which Go refuses inside a file, are dropped. A line that reads as a `+build` constraint (Go's spaces around `+build`) is a block comment, `/* +build ignore */`, its form feeds made spaces, since gofmt moves a `// +build` line above the package clause, where it drops the file from the build.
    - **Written as is, though Go, gofmt or a driver refuses it:** a name Go refuses and a repeated one (`id` beside `ID`), which fail the build: under every name case one holding an emoji or another pictographic symbol the name cases keep (`©`, `®`, `™`, `‼`, `↔`, `〰`), under NameCase none, which keeps every character, one holding a character no identifier holds (a space, `-`, `,`, `;`, `.`, `:`, `//`, a quote, a backslash, a backtick, `²`, `€`, `Ⅻ`, `ⓐ`, U+00A0, U+00AD, U+200B, U+202E, U+2028, a private-use character, a control character) or a mark of its own (`नाम`, `ชื่อ`); under NameCase none a table or column name ending in a space or a tab, which builds but gofmt trims (`type Trail  struct`, `type Tab<tab> struct`), a name ending in a `/*…*/` comment, which builds as the part before it (`a/*x*/` the field `A`, `Users/*x*/` the struct `Users`) and which gofmt lists, and a column name with a comma, which Go reads as two fields (`A,b string`), go vet then reporting the second unexported; a comment line that reads as a `+build` constraint but holds `*/`, which no block comment can carry, so go vet reports `misplaced +build comment` and gofmt moves the line above the package clause, where the build then excludes the file; a table comment gofmt rewrites as a doc comment: a line opening with a space or a tab becomes a code block (`//` then `//<tab>…`), or a list item where it opens with a list marker (`  1. one` becomes `//  1. one`, a tab then `* a` becomes `//   - a`; `  - id` is gofmt's own form and stays), a line alone between paragraphs that reads as an old-style heading gets `# `, and a link definition (`[docs]: https://x`) moves to the comment's end after an empty `//` (a column's comment is no doc comment and stays). encoding/json's keys: the key is the tag's name before its first comma, so `a,b` reads as `a`, and `a, b` as `a` too, which go vet reports (`suspicious space in struct tag value`), and what follows the comma is read as options, which go vet does not report: `a,string` writes the value as a JSON string (`{"a":"1"}`), `a,omitempty` and `a,omitzero` leave a zero value out, and an option go1.27.1's default encoding/json refuses (`a,format:RFC3339` on an integer) makes `json.Marshal` of the whole struct fail (``unsupported `format` tag option``), where `GOEXPERIMENT=nojsonv2` ignores it; under go1.27.1's default encoding/json, built on json/v2, a name holding `"`, `\`, `'` or a backtick reads up to that character (`say"hi` as `say`, `we`ird` as `we`); a name that is only `,`, or empty, reads as the field name; and two fields that read to one key both leave the JSON, set values included, so `a` beside `a,omitempty`, `a,b` beside `a,c`, and by default `say` beside `say"hi`, `back` beside `back\slash` and `it` beside `it's`, go vet reporting only the comma-split pairs (`repeats json tag`). Under `GOEXPERIMENT=nojsonv2` and on Go 1.26, whose encoding/json is v1, the key is the Go field name for any name holding a character other than a letter (`unicode.IsLetter`), a digit (`unicode.IsDigit`), an ASCII space and ``!#$%&()*+-./:;<=>?@[]^_{|}~``: a quote, a backslash, an apostrophe or a backtick (`say"hi` as `SayHi`), and, though the default reads them as the column name, a combining mark (a decomposed `émile` as `Émile`, `नाम` as `Xनम`, `ชื่อ` as `Xชอ`), `²`, `¹`, `½`, `Ⅷ`, `€`, `°`, `·`, `×`, `–`, curly quotes, full-width brackets (`金額（円）` as `X金額円`), U+200B, U+FEFF, a tab and any other control character. Driver limits: lib/pq reads no array into a slice, and reads a `time` into a `string` as `0000-01-01T13:45:30Z`, which PostgreSQL refuses written back; pgx, natively and through `database/sql`, refuses a NULL element of an array (`'{1,NULL}'`) into its slice (`cannot scan NULL into *int32`; a `[]*int32` would take it), the slice taking only a NULL array as nil; pgx's native interface reads no interval or bit string into a `string`; GORM refuses a whole model that holds an array's slice when it parses the schema; such a slice reads through pgx's native interface, or through pgx v5.11.0's `database/sql` driver on Go 1.27 (`RowsColumnScanner`), not on Go 1.26; go-ora reads an Oracle `BFILE` into no plain type (it hands over a locator) and refuses to bind a `json.RawMessage` (`ORA-01484`), so an Oracle JSON column reads but is not written back; an Oracle bare `NUMBER` (and `NUMBER(*)`, the same type) holding a fraction fails its `int64` scan, kept to match `rust.ts`'s `i64`; an Oracle `INT`, `INTEGER` or `SMALLINT` (a NUMBER(38)), bare `NUMBER` or `NUMBER(p)` with p from 19 to 38 can hold a value past `int64`'s range, which go-ora refuses (`converting driver.Value type string ("100000000000000000000") to a int64: value out of range`), and a Snowflake integer, a NUMBER(38,0), can hold one too (from its documented range; what gosnowflake does with it was not run); go-sql-driver/mysql fills a `time.Time` only with `parseTime=true` in the DSN.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-10 with go1.27.1 (darwin/arm64), shopspring/decimal v1.5.0 and go-mssqldb v1.11.2, the generator bundled from the current sources: 3,439 packages, each a whole text or a one-table view with the four imports added, covering the eight vendor lists' 492 names, each bare and with `(10)`, `(10,2)`, ` UNSIGNED`, ` ZEROFILL`, `[]` and `[][]`, and 101 other spellings (3,545 types in all) as NOT NULL, nullable and key-only columns; 105 names (Go keywords and predeclared names, the output's package and type names, Hangul, Kana, Han, the Turkish İ, combining marks, a decomposed and a precomposed `émile`, Devanagari, Thai, emoji, punctuation, quotes, a backslash, a backtick, control characters, U+FEFF, U+200B, U+202E, a leading space, a trailing space or tab) as table and column names under all four name cases; 46 comments (CR, LF, CRLF, U+2028, U+2029, NEL, NUL, U+FEFF, `+build` lines with every Go space, `//go:build`, indented lines, a heading, a link definition, form feeds, bidirectional controls, a lone surrogate) on tables and columns of PostgreSQL and MySQL; empty and column-less tables; alignment with Hangul and characters beyond the BMP; and the five `data/*.sql` fixtures on all eight databases; all whole and per table. `go build` failed 119 packages, every one a name Go refuses or a repeated one; `go vet` failed 15 more, the 6 holding the `+build` line with `*/` and 9 names with a comma; `gofmt -l` listed 47, 36 for a table comment it rewrites (18 an indented line, 6 a heading, 6 a link definition, 6 the `+build` line with `*/`) and 11 for a name under NameCase none (a trailing space or tab, a lone space, a comma, a line feed); every type list, fixture, empty table and alignment package built, passed go vet and was left as it was by gofmt. In the 2,799 one-table packages that parse, the tags of 38,299 of the 38,301 fields read back through `reflect.StructTag.Get` as their column name (`-,` for `-`); the two others are a column `x` LF `y` under NameCase none, whose line break splits its field, in a package that does not build. A further 732 packages: 34 comments (indented numbered and bulleted lists, a code block after a paragraph, `+build` after other spaces, CR, U+2028, U+FEFF or NUL, C1, private-use and noncharacters) on PostgreSQL and SQL Server, 37 names under all four name cases, and ENUM, SET and argument spellings on all eight databases; `go build` failed 54, every one a name under NameCase none that Go refuses; `go vet` 8 more, the `+build` line with a form feed and `*/`; `gofmt -l` listed 40, 32 for a table comment (8 each a numbered list, a tab-indented bulleted list, a code block and the `+build` line with `*/`) and 8 for a name under NameCase none (`a/*x*/` and `Users/*x*/` 3 each, which build, and 2 that do not); 1,333 of the 1,336 fields read back, the others in two packages that do not build. 19 names mixing a mark of their own (Thai, Devanagari, Hebrew, Arabic, a decomposed `é`) with a letter whose case change makes one (İ, ǰ, ΐ, ΰ, ẖ, ẗ, ẘ), as table and column names under all four name cases: every package built under camelCase, pascalCase and snakeCase, and under NameCase none the 15 holding a mark of their own failed. `json.Marshal` of the comma, quote, backslash, apostrophe and backtick names, alone and beside the name they read as, and of 60 more column names under camelCase gave the keys above, by default, under `GOEXPERIMENT=nojsonv2` and on go1.26.9, the last two alike for every name. A stub `database/sql` driver handing over NULL: a `[]byte` and a `*json.RawMessage` took it, a `json.RawMessage` did not (`unsupported Scan, storing driver.Value type <nil>`). The driver limits, and the types they decided, were measured on 2026-10-09 against PostgreSQL 17.11, MySQL 8.4.11, MariaDB 11.4.13, SQLite, SQL Server 2022 (CU27) and Oracle Database 23.26 Free, with pgx v5.11.0 (`database/sql` and native), lib/pq v1.12.3, go-sql-driver/mysql v1.10.1, mattn/go-sqlite3 v1.14.52, go-mssqldb v1.11.2, go-ora v2.9.0 and GORM v1.31.2, a value row and a NULL row each read and written back; the NULL array element (`'{1,NULL}'::int[]`, `'{a,NULL}'::text[]`, `'{{1,2},{NULL,4}}'::int[]` through pgx v5.11.0, natively and through `database/sql`, on PostgreSQL 17.11) and the integers past `int64` (an `INTEGER` column holding 10^20, and casts to INTEGER, NUMBER(19) and NUMBER(38) past either end, through go-ora v2.9.0 on Oracle Database 23.26 Free, scanned into `int64`, `*int64` and `string`) on 2026-10-10; PostgreSQL's `dec` and SQLite's `DEC`, which that run did not cover, are stored as the `numeric` and NUMERIC-affinity columns `decimal` and `DECIMAL` are (the `graphql.ts` entry's measurement), and take their `decimal.Decimal`. Snowflake's and Databricks' values are inferred from their drivers' documentation, not run. The comma options and the pictographic symbols were measured on 2026-10-10 with go1.27.1: NOT NULL `INT` columns named `a,string`, `a,format:RFC3339`, `a,omitempty`, `a,omitzero`, `a,b` and `a,unknown` under the default name cases, marshalled holding 1 and 0, by default and under `GOEXPERIMENT=nojsonv2`, go vet reporting none; and a column name holding `™`, `®`, `©`, `‼`, `⁉`, `↔`, `↕`, `↩`, `⌚`, `⌨`, `◻`, `◼`, `〰`, `〽`, `⤴` or an emoji under the default name cases, camelCase, pascalCase and snakeCase, each of the 64 packages failing to build (`invalid character U+2122 '™' in identifier`).
  - `java.ts`, `kotlin.ts` and `scala.ts` write plain classes with no import or package line, as the other plain generators do, so the reader adds `lombok.Data`, `java.math.BigDecimal`, `java.util.UUID` and the `java.time` types the text names (Scala's `Duration` needs none, below); the one-table view is `formatTable`. Java writes a Lombok `@Data public class` of `private` boxed fields. Kotlin writes `data class X(val a: T, val b: T? = null)` (an owner decision of 2026-10-09): a NOT NULL or key column a required `val a: T,`, any other `val b: T? = null,`, one parameter a line in four spaces with a comma after each, as Kotlin's coding conventions lay out a class header, and a table without columns `class X`, since a data class needs a property. Scala writes a `case class X(` with no `@Data`, two spaces, `Option[T]` for a column that is neither a key nor NOT NULL, no comma after the last field and no `= None`. A primary key without the NN flag is NOT NULL in all three (an owner decision of 2026-10-09). A comment is `//` lines, one a line at CR, LF, CRLF, U+2028 and U+2029 (`formatLineComment`, which `jpa.ts` shares), its blank first and last lines dropped and a blank inner line a bare `//`; Java and JPA add a backslash to an odd run of them before a `u` (`escapeUnicodeEscapes`), since javac reads a Unicode escape even in a comment, failing on a bad one (`C:\users`) and ending the comment at `\u000a`, and Scala writes U+202A to U+202E and U+2066 to U+2069 as the text of their escape (`\u202B`), since Scala 2.13 refuses them even in a comment, and SUB (U+001A), which the importer reads from a Databricks `'\Z'`, as `\u001A`, since Scala 2.13 and 3 read it as the end of the file (`illegal character`).
    - **Types** come from the shared classifier (`columnTypes.ts`) through `getJvmType` in `java.ts`, which Kotlin and Scala spell their own way (`Int`, `ByteArray` / `Array[Byte]`): integers at their width, `Byte`, `Short`, `Integer` and `Long`, an unsigned one (UNSIGNED or ZEROFILL) the next wider type, but MEDIUMINT UNSIGNED an `Integer`, and BIGINT UNSIGNED and SERIAL a `Long` (an owner decision of 2026-10-09: a `Long` holds every key below 2^63, where `BigInteger` would retype every Laravel-style key); Oracle `INT`, `INTEGER` and `SMALLINT` (a `NUMBER(38)`), every Snowflake integer (a `NUMBER(38,0)`) and every SQLite integer a `Long`, as a bare `NUMBER` is, though a `Long` holds 19 of the 38 digits Oracle's and Snowflake's take; SQL Server `tinyint` a `Short`; MySQL and MariaDB `TINYINT(1)` a `Byte`, `BIT(1)` a `Boolean` and `BIT(n)` past one bit a `Long`; on MySQL, MariaDB, PostgreSQL and SQL Server `FLOAT(1)` to `FLOAT(24)` a `Float` and `FLOAT(25)` to `FLOAT(53)` a `Double`, on Oracle, SQLite and Snowflake every `FLOAT(n)` a `Double`, as Oracle `REAL` is, and on Databricks every `FLOAT(n)` a `Float`; decimals, SQL Server and PostgreSQL money and an Oracle or Snowflake `NUMBER(p,s)` with a scale a `BigDecimal` (in Scala `scala.math.BigDecimal`, which needs no import), a bare `NUMBER` a `Long` but MariaDB's, a DOUBLE, a `Double`; binary types and SQL Server `rowversion` and `timestamp` bytes (`byte[]`); `uuid`, `uniqueidentifier` and MariaDB's and Snowflake's `UUID` a `UUID`; PostgreSQL `oid`, `cid`, `xid` and `xid8` a `Long`; JSON, the semi-structured, network, bit-string, spatial and every other type a `String`; the offset timestamps (`timestamptz`, `datetimeoffset`, Oracle's and Snowflake's WITH TIME ZONE, `TIMESTAMP_TZ`) an `OffsetDateTime`, `timetz` an `OffsetTime`, the naive and local-zone timestamps (MySQL `TIMESTAMP`, WITH LOCAL TIME ZONE, `TIMESTAMP_LTZ`, Databricks `TIMESTAMP`) and Oracle `DATE` a `LocalDateTime`; a day-to-second interval and a bare one (PostgreSQL `interval`, Databricks `INTERVAL`), whose months it cannot hold, a `Duration`, which Scala alone writes with its package, `java.time.Duration`, since a bare one would resolve to, or clash with, the `scala.concurrent.duration.Duration` a Scala file often imports, while `UUID` and `OffsetDateTime` stay bare in all three; a year-month interval a `String`, since a `Duration` holds no months. A PostgreSQL array is one level a dimension: Java `T[]`, which Lombok's `equals` compares by content and `jpa.ts` writes too, Kotlin `List<T>` and Scala `List[T]`, since a data class and a case class compare an `Array` by reference.
    - **Names** follow the name cases. Java, which has no escape, puts `_` after a keyword or literal (`_` too) and after `var`, `yield`, `record`, `sealed` and `permits` as a class name, which a field may take, and writes a field `Class` as `Class_`, whose Lombok getter would be the final `getClass`, and a field `java` as `java_`, which would hide the package of the `java.util.Arrays` calls Lombok writes for a `byte[]` or array field (`cannot find symbol … variable util … location: variable java`); a table `Class` stays `public class Class` (`toJavaFieldName`, `toJavaClassName`, which `jpa.ts` shares). Kotlin writes a hard keyword and a name of underscores alone (`_`, `__`) in backticks. Scala writes a reserved word of Scala 2.13 or 3 in backticks, and a field ending in `_` too, which the lexer would read with the colon after it as one name (`` `order_`: Int ``). Any other name that is no identifier, and a repeated one, is written as is.
    - **Written as is:** an empty table or column name (`public class  {`, `val : Int,`, `: Int`) and a name the name case empties (`_`, `__`, `$` under camelCase, PascalCase or snake_case), one opening on a digit or holding a space or a hyphen (`non-sealed` too), one holding `$` in Kotlin and, under `-Werror`, in Scala 3, and a repeated field, which fail to compile; a class named after a type or package the text or the reader's imports use (`Data`, `List`, `UUID`, `BigDecimal`, `Option`, `Array`, `java`, `lombok`), which breaks the class or silently retypes the fields of that type (`Duration` in Java and Kotlin, `String`); in Scala, a field named after a member every case class has (`hashCode`, `toString`, `wait`, `notify`, `notifyAll`, `clone`, `finalize`, `productPrefix`, `productIterator`, `productElementNames`, `productArity` of any type but `Int`, and in Scala 2.13 `getClass`), a class `_` and in Scala 3 a field `_`, a class `` `package` `` in Scala 2.13, which makes it the package object, a field `_1` or `_2` out of its place in Scala 3, and a field named `java`, which hides the package of `java.time.Duration` from its own type and the fields after it; two Java fields that differ only in the case of their first letter (`name`, `Name`), which share one Lombok getter, written for the first alone, and in Kotlin two such fields (`name` and `Name`, `uRL` and `URL`, `` `class` `` and `Class`), whose getters clash (`platform declaration clash`) and fail to compile; a binary column, which a data class and a case class compare by reference; a table of more columns than a constructor takes, the JVM counting a `Long` or `Double` twice against its 255 slots, so YOUNGCART5's `g5_shop_default` on Oracle, SQLite and Snowflake, where every integer is a `Long`, compiles in Kotlin and is refused when the JVM loads it, and Scala refuses it on SQLite and Snowflake.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-10 with javac 25.0.4 and Lombok 1.18.48 (`-proc:full -Xlint:all`), kotlinc 2.4.21 (`-Werror`) and Scala 3.10.0 and 2.13.18 (`-Werror`), each text compiled in a package of its own with the imports a reader adds, then every class loaded on the JVM: every type of the eight vendor lists with seven argument variants and further spellings (3,593 in all), each NOT NULL, nullable, a key without NN and an auto-increment key; comments with CR, LF, CRLF, U+2028, U+2029, U+0085, backslash runs before `u`, bidirectional controls and every C0 control, SUB among them, SUB also as a Databricks `COMMENT 'a\Zb'` read by the importer; the five `data/*.sql` fixtures on every database; all whole and per table, which compiled with no warning and loaded but for the wide table under Written as is; and 164 names (the keywords of the three languages, the members a class has, the types and packages the output names, and names that are no identifier) under the four name cases as a class, key, NOT NULL, nullable and relation name, and under none and camelCase as a field beside a `bytea` and a `text[]` column and as the parent of a child holding a `bytea` column, a field `java` also beside each binary type of the eight lists, and nine pairs of names that differ only in letter case, which failed only as Written as is lists. Scala's `java.time.Duration` field compiled to that type with `scala.concurrent.duration.Duration` imported, alone or beside `java.time.Duration`, where a bare `Duration` resolved to the Scala one or was refused as ambiguous.
  - `jpa.ts` writes a Jakarta Persistence entity a table, Lombok's `@Data` on it and plain JPA annotations only (no Hibernate annotation, as the referential-action bullet records), a field a column in the table's order but for its foreign keys, which become relation fields (below), with `java.ts`'s names and types but where noted, and a composite key adds an `@IdClass` holder, `XId implements Serializable`, before its entity.
    - **Names.** `@Table(name = "...")` where the class name differs from the table name, `@Column(name = "...")` where the field name differs from the column name, key columns included, and `@JoinColumn(name = "...")` with the column as written, each name in a Java string with `"`, `\`, CR and LF escaped. That fits Hibernate's default physical naming (`PhysicalNamingStrategyStandardImpl`), which keeps every name as given. Spring Boot's default, `CamelCaseToUnderscoresNamingStrategy` (from Spring Boot's documentation), rewrites a name with a capital in it, a written one too, so `@Table(name = "userAccount")` looks for `user_account` and a field `createdAt` with no `@Column` name for `created_at`, while a name with no capital (`member_info`, `small_n`) passes under either; an application on Spring Boot sets `spring.jpa.hibernate.naming.physical-strategy` to `org.hibernate.boot.model.naming.PhysicalNamingStrategyStandardImpl`. A table name the shared reserved-word list holds (`DOCTRINE_RESERVED_WORDS`, in any letter case) is quoted inside the `@Table` name, `"\"user\""`, which Hibernate writes in the dialect's own quotes, only where `settings.bracketType` quotes names, as the editor's DDL does: under `BracketType.none` the DDL leaves the name bare, so the database folds its case and a quoted mapping finds no table (`missing table [Member]` on PostgreSQL, ORA-00942 on Oracle), and a name the database itself reserves cannot be created there at all. Class and field names are `java.ts`'s (`toJavaClassName`, `toJavaFieldName`): a keyword gets `_` after it and the field its `@Column` name (`class_`), a field `Class` is `Class_` with `@Column(name = "class")`, a field `java` is `java_` with `@Column(name = "java")`, and every relation field, `mappedBy` and `@IdClass` field goes through the same rule, so each side names the one field.
    - **Types** are `java.ts`'s with three differences: `timetz` a `LocalTime`, since Hibernate moves an `OffsetTime` by the JVM's offset on the way in and out; an Oracle interval a `String`, since Hibernate writes a `Duration` to an Oracle interval in no way, while a PostgreSQL one takes it under `hibernate.type.preferred_duration_jdbc_type=INTERVAL_SECOND`; and `@Lob` on Oracle `BLOB`, `CLOB` and `NCLOB` alone, which Hibernate reads as a `byte[]` or `String` only under it, and on no other type. `columnDefinition` is written only where Hibernate's schema validation refuses the field without it: PostgreSQL `money`; a fixed `BINARY(n)` on MySQL, MariaDB and SQL Server, and MySQL's and MariaDB's `CHAR(n) BYTE`, as `"BINARY(16)"`; MySQL and MariaDB `TINYINT`, `SMALLINT` and `INT` UNSIGNED or ZEROFILL, by the name the driver reports (`"INT UNSIGNED"`, which drops the display width and, on MySQL, ZEROFILL, which MariaDB keeps, so MariaDB adds ` ZEROFILL`), and never on YEAR, which stays a YEAR under either. SQL Server `rowversion` and `timestamp` take `@Column(insertable = false, updatable = false)`, the database writing them.
    - **Keys and relations.** `@Id` on a key column, with `@GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)` where it auto-increments, the identity or AUTO_INCREMENT column the editor's DDL creates, and no `nullable` on an `@Id`, a key taking no NULL; `nullable = false` on any other NOT NULL column. A foreign key column is no field of its own: the child holds a `@ManyToOne` or, for a one cardinality, `@OneToOne` field of the parent's class with `@JoinColumn`, or `@JoinColumns` for a composite key, `@Id` too where the foreign key is part of the key, and the parent `@OneToMany(mappedBy = "...")` on a `List<X> xList = new ArrayList<>()`, or `@OneToOne(mappedBy = "...")`.
    - **Written as is, though Hibernate cannot use it:** a keyless table, whose `@Entity` without an `@Id` stops the persistence unit at boot; a foreign key to part of a composite key (`data/Magento2-sales.sql`), which MySQL and PostgreSQL refuse in the editor's DDL; an auto-increment column in a composite key, which Hibernate 6.6 refuses on insert (`Identity generation isn't supported for composite ids`) and 7.4 inserts on MySQL and PostgreSQL but not on SQLite, whose DDL writes no AUTOINCREMENT there; two relationships of one table pair, a one-to-one relationship of a table to itself, whose owning and inverse fields both take the table's name, and a column named like the field a relationship writes (`city` beside a foreign key to `city`), which repeat a field name (`already defined` in Magento2-sales, OKKY and sakila); a PostgreSQL array of more than one dimension (`Integer[][]`), which Hibernate refuses at boot (`Nested arrays ... not supported`); a reserved column name, never quoted, so a PostgreSQL insert into a column `order` fails; under a quoting bracket type, every name but a reserved table name left bare, so a name with capitals the DDL created quoted is not found on PostgreSQL, which folds it down, or Oracle, which folds it up, unless `hibernate.globally_quoted_identifiers=true` is set; the names `java.ts` writes as is, and a class named after an annotation or type the reader imports (`Entity`, `Table`, `Column`, `Id`, `List`, `ArrayList`, `Serializable`). And these types, each with the field type above: PostgreSQL: `json`, `jsonb`, `xml`, the network types (`inet`, `cidr`, `macaddr`, `macaddr8`), the geometric types (`point`, `line`, `lseg`, `box`, `path`, `polygon`, `circle`), every range and multirange, the year-month intervals (`interval year`, `interval month`, `interval year to month`), the bit strings, `tsvector`, `tsquery`, `jsonpath`, `tid`, `pg_lsn`, `pg_snapshot`, `txid_snapshot` and the `reg*` types, which fail validation (`found [json (Types#OTHER)]`, `found [xml (Types#SQLXML)]`) and write only with `stringtype=unspecified` in the JDBC URL (`column "c" is of type inet but expression is of type character varying` without it), `regclass` alone writing without it; `cid`, `xid` and `xid8`, which fail validation and a write with or without it (`column "c" is of type xid but expression is of type bigint`); `bpchar`, `char` and `character`, which fail validation alone; a bare `interval` holding months, which Hibernate reads with the months dropped (`1 mon` as `PT0S`, `1 year 2 mons 3 days 04:05:06` as `PT76H5M6S`); a `money` of 1,000 or more either side of zero, which writes but which pgjdbc cannot read through its group separators (`Bad value for type BigDecimal : 1,234.56`); and the arrays `jsonb[]`, `interval[]` and `money[]`, which fail a write or a read even with `stringtype=unspecified` and `INTERVAL_SECOND`, `money[]` with `Bad value for type double : $12.34` whatever the value. MySQL and MariaDB: `TINYINT(1)` (`found [bit (Types#BIT)], but expecting [tinyint]` on MySQL, `found [boolean]` on MariaDB), `BIT(n)` past one bit (`found [bit], but expecting [bigint]`), `YEAR`, MariaDB's `INET4` and `INET6`, and on Hibernate 6.6 alone `CHAR` and MySQL's `ENUM` and `SET`, which fail validation alone; the spatial types (`GEOMETRY`, `POINT`, `LINESTRING`, `POLYGON`, the `MULTI` ones, `GEOMETRYCOLLECTION` and `GEOMCOLLECTION`), which fail validation and a write (`Cannot get geometry object from data you send to the GEOMETRY field`). Oracle: `CHAR`, `CHARACTER`, `NATIONAL CHAR`, `NATIONAL CHARACTER` and `NCHAR`, `NCLOB`, `JSON` (`found [json (Types#UNKNOWN(2016))]`), and on Hibernate 6.6 alone `BINARY_FLOAT` and `BINARY_DOUBLE`, which fail validation alone; `INTERVAL DAY TO SECOND` and `INTERVAL YEAR TO MONTH`, which fail validation and write but read back in ojdbc's form (`+01 02:03:04.000000` as `1 2:3:4.0`, `+01-02` as `1-2`), and `VECTOR`, which fails validation and reads `[1,2,3]` back as `[1.0E+000,2.0E+000,3.0E+000]`; `BFILE`, `ANYDATA` and `URIType`, which fail validation and a write (ORA-00932); `XMLType`, which fails validation and a round trip without Oracle's xdb library (`NoClassDefFoundError: oracle/xdb/XMLType`); `ROWID` and `UROWID`, which fail validation; a bare `NUMBER` holding a fraction, read with it dropped (`1.5` as `1`), and a bare `NUMBER` or an integer type holding a value past a `Long`, whose read fails (ORA-17026). SQL Server: `char`, `character`, `national char`, `national character`, `nchar` and `sql_variant`, which fail validation alone on 6.6 and 7.4; `geometry` and `geography`, which fail validation and read back as the hex of their binary form (`POINT(1 2)` as `E6100000010C…`); `hierarchyid`, which fails validation and reads `/1/` back as `58`; `rowversion` and `timestamp`, which fail validation. SQLite, under Hibernate's community dialect: every integer type but `BIGINT` (`found [integer (Types#INTEGER)], but expecting [bigint]`), a key included, and `BOOL`, `DATETIME`, `DEC` and `DECIMAL`, which fail validation alone.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-10: javac 25.0.4 with Lombok 1.18.48 and jakarta.persistence-api 3.2.0 compiled the whole texts of the corpus above, every one-table view being a slice of its whole text, but for the repeated relation fields under Written as is (the fixtures', a one-to-one relationship of `employee` to itself, a column `city` beside a foreign key to `city`) and the names under Written as is, and every class loaded; `-Xlint:all` warned only `[processing]`, since no processor claims the Jakarta annotations, and `[serial]` on each `@IdClass` holder, which declares no `serialVersionUID` and, where a foreign key is part of the key, holds the parent entity, which is not `Serializable`, in a field. Hibernate 6.6.58 and 7.4.12 (Hibernate's default naming) validated, persisted and read back, against the editor's DDL on PostgreSQL 17.11 (pgjdbc 42.7.8) and MySQL 8.4.11 (Connector/J 9.4.0), a table `class` with a column `class` (`Class` under the none case), the relation, `mappedBy` and `@IdClass` fields named after it, a table the database reserves (`user`, `order`) and one only the list holds (`key`, `user`), under every column name case: under `doubleQuote` on PostgreSQL and `backtick` on MySQL, and under `none` without the reserved table, whose DDL fails there, but for PostgreSQL `doubleQuote` with a name in capitals (Written as is); under `singleQuote`, `backtick` on PostgreSQL and `doubleQuote` on MySQL the editor's DDL fails before any mapping. The naming strategies ran the same way on PostgreSQL with a `userAccount` and a `member_info` table under the PascalCase and camelCase name cases. A table `java` with a column `java` beside `bytea` and `text[]` columns, and the relation, `mappedBy` and `@IdClass` fields named after it, all `java_` in classes holding a `byte[]`, validated, persisted and read back the same way on PostgreSQL 17.11. The type limits come from one table per type, an integer key and the column, in the editor's DDL under `BracketType.none`: every type of the PostgreSQL, MySQL, MariaDB, Oracle, SQL Server and SQLite lists whose table that DDL creates there, spellings with arguments (`TINYINT(1)`, `BIT(64)`, `CHAR(10)`, `INTERVAL DAY(2) TO SECOND(6)`) and seventeen PostgreSQL arrays, each entity validated alone, then a row persisted and found, on PostgreSQL 17.11 (pgjdbc 42.7.8, once as it comes and once with `stringtype=unspecified` and `INTERVAL_SECOND`), MySQL 8.4.11 (Connector/J 9.4.0), MariaDB 11.4.13 (MariaDB Connector/J 3.5.6), Oracle Database Free 23.26.3 (ojdbc11 23.9), SQL Server 2022 CU27 (mssql-jdbc 13.2) and SQLite (sqlite-jdbc 3.53.4.0, `hibernate-community-dialects` of the same Hibernate release); the PostgreSQL intervals with months and the Oracle `NUMBER` values were inserted in SQL and read by Hibernate, and the money values persisted and read by it. Snowflake and Databricks were not run.
  - `php.ts` writes plain classes of typed public properties (owner decisions of 2026-10-07): `<?php` and `declare(strict_types=1);` open the text with nothing before them, not the blank line the other languages open with, since PHP refuses that declare after any output, so `index.ts` hands the one-table view to `createTableCode` (Doctrine's, Swift's, Zod's and JSON Schema's too, each opening on a header a file of its own needs, and SeaORM's, a module file's body). A NOT NULL column or a primary key is `T $x;`, any other `?T $x = null;`. A decimal or money type stays a string, and so do an Oracle or Snowflake NUMBER with a scale (the shared tables file every NUMBER under long; a bare one stays an int), a time and an interval, PHP having no time of day and the shared tables filing a PostgreSQL interval under time, and PostgreSQL's arrays, bit strings and `pg_lsn`, which pdo_pgsql reads as text; a date is `\DateTimeImmutable`, SQL Server's `bit` a `bool` and its `numeric` a string. A comment is a doc block, never `//`, which a `?>` in the text would end, with `*/` written `*\/`. A name PHP refuses for a class in any letter case (`List`, `Match`, `Object`, `Readonly`, and `_`, which PHP 8.4 deprecates; `RESERVED_CLASS_NAMES`, measured on PHP 8.1 and 8.4) gets `_` after it, as nikic/PHP-Parser names its nodes; every other name, column names included, stays as the name cases give it, so a name that is no PHP identifier or a repeated one is written as is. An empty table is `class X {}`, PER Coding Style's form, where PSR-12 wants the braces on two lines. On 2026-10-07 PHP 8.1 and 8.4 linted and ran the output for the five `data/*.sql` fixtures, whole and per table, and php-cs-fixer's `@PER-CS2.0` and `@PER-CS3.0` left it as it was.
  - `doctrine.ts` writes Doctrine ORM 3 attribute entities with public typed properties and no accessors (owner decisions of 2026-10-07; ORM 3's lazy ghost proxies load a public property, which ORM 2's did not), for DBAL 4.2 or later (`Types::ENUM`, `Types::SMALLFLOAT`). Its rules come from two adversarial reviews, of 2026-10-07 and 2026-10-09, that ran every finding through ORM 3.7.4 and DBAL 4.2.5, the second against the real databases too:
    - **Types.** Every column writes `name:` and `type: Types::…`, its PHP type what DBAL 4 hydrates (`BLOB` `mixed`, `JSON` and `SIMPLE_ARRAY` `array`, `DECIMAL` a string, `BIGINT` an int, or `int|string` where it is unsigned and can pass PHP's range), with the options DBAL reads back, so a migration diff against the editor's DDL leaves the column as it is but for the shapes under Written as is: a length from a character or binary type's argument alone, in every spelling the vendor lists hold (`lengthTypes`; an interval's or a vector's argument is no length), an Oracle `BYTE` or `CHAR` unit dropped, `'fixed' => true` for `char`, `nchar`, `binary`, MySQL's `CHAR BYTE` (a `BINARY`) and a `bpchar` with a length, and length 1 for a bare CHAR and for a bare binary on MySQL, MariaDB and SQL Server, which make it one byte; `SMALLFLOAT` for a single precision float as each platform reads it back (MySQL `FLOAT` up to 24 or with two arguments, PostgreSQL `real`, `float4` and `float(1)` to `float(24)`, SQL Server `real` and `float(1)` to `float(24)`, Oracle `REAL` and `FLOAT(63)`, SQLite `real`; Oracle `BINARY_FLOAT` is `FLOAT`); MySQL's sized text and blob types with their lengths (255, 65535, 16777215; a LONG type is a MEDIUMTEXT or MEDIUMBLOB there, `TEXT(n)` takes the class n four-byte characters need, where `BLOB(n)` counts bytes, and a zero argument the TINY class on MySQL and the plain one on MariaDB), its national character types with `'charset' => 'utf8mb3'`, which MySQL stores them in, `ENUM` with its `'values'`, each read as MySQL reads a literal, `SET` as `SIMPLE_ARRAY`, `UNSIGNED`, `ZEROFILL` and `SERIAL` with `'unsigned' => true` on an integer, a DECIMAL or a float; PostgreSQL `jsonb` with `'jsonb' => true`; an Oracle or Snowflake NUMBER with a scale as a `DECIMAL`, and an Oracle NUMBER, DECIMAL, NUMERIC or DEC of no scale as DBAL reads it back (precision 5 a `SMALLINT`, any other up to 10 an `INTEGER`, a wider one a `BIGINT`), Oracle's `SMALLINT`, which it stores as such a NUMBER, an `INTEGER`, and Oracle's `DATE`, which the shared vendor tables now file as a date and time, a `DATE_IMMUTABLE`, the type DBAL reads such a column back as (`vendorTypes`; from DBAL's Oracle type mapping, not run). DBAL 4 writes no VARBINARY or DECIMAL without a size, so any other length-less binary type is a `BLOB` and a DECIMAL naming no precision takes `precision: 10, scale: 0` (DBAL 3's default), on SQL Server its own 18 and 0, on Oracle the `INTEGER` its NUMBER(*,0) reads back as, or a money type its vendor's digits; ORM writes a length-less string as VARCHAR(255), so `varchar(max)` and a length-less `text` or `string` (PostgreSQL, SQLite, Snowflake, Databricks) are a `TEXT`, while a length-less `varchar` or `bpchar` stays a `STRING`, a key one of length 255, ORM's default, which SchemaTool would not copy to a join column toward it. A time of day is `TIME_IMMUTABLE` though `php.ts` writes a string (an owner decision: as a string a migration would turn the column into VARCHAR), an interval `STRING`. A key Doctrine cannot hash, which it does as a string, takes another type: a date or time key a `STRING` of length 255 with a string property and a definition (an owner decision), the length being what SchemaTool copies to a join column toward it and DBAL's comparator needs, and a length-less binary key on PostgreSQL or SQLite (`bytea`, `BLOB`), whose `BLOB` hydrates a stream, a `BINARY` with a string property, which those platforms write as the same type.
    - **Column definitions** keep a column whose type DBAL cannot write, since DBAL writes a definition verbatim where it creates a column: SQL Server's `rowversion` and `timestamp` (`ROWVERSION`, eight bytes the database writes and never leaves NULL, not fixed, as DBAL reads it back, so `insertable: false, updatable: false, generated: 'ALWAYS'` and a property null until the insert), `nvarchar(max)`, `ntext` and `national text` (`length: -1` and `NVARCHAR(MAX)`, since DBAL writes a TEXT as VARCHAR(MAX), which keeps no Unicode) and a VARCHAR or CHAR past the 4000 characters of the NVARCHAR DBAL writes; PostgreSQL's arrays, bit strings and `pg_lsn` as a `TEXT` with a string property, pdo_pgsql reading them as text; and a date or time key, the definition repeated on a join column toward it. A definition holds the column's NOT NULL, but on an Oracle key DBAL reads back otherwise (of a type other than DATE, or with a default), whose primary key makes it NOT NULL and whose MODIFY refuses that again, and on MySQL and MariaDB its comment, which those platforms write nowhere else; the `'comment'` option stays beside it for DBAL's comparator.
    - **Defaults and indexes** (an owner decision). A default goes into `options` when it is a number in the grammar the editor's DDL writes (a sign, a point, an exponent), a quoted string (an `N` before it, a character set before it on MySQL and MariaDB, which read its backslash escapes too, `\%` and `\_` kept), a boolean on a BOOLEAN or, as 1 or 0, on an integer column of MySQL, MariaDB or SQLite, or the current time on a timestamp (`CURRENT_TIMESTAMP`, `NOW()`, `GETDATE()`, `now()`, `LOCALTIMESTAMP` and the like, as each database spells it, all written `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP`), never an expression, which DBAL would quote, nor on a generated key or a defined column. It is written as its database reads it back: as typed on Oracle and SQLite, which keep the text, and elsewhere with no exponent, no plus, no leading zero, which PHP reads as octal, and no trailing zero; an integer bare, but as a string past PHP's int or where the text is kept, any other number as a string, on MySQL and MariaDB rounded and padded to its column's scale, a DECIMAL's or a FLOAT(M,D)'s, an integer column's rounded to an integer; a quoted `true`, `false`, 1 or 0 on a BOOLEAN as a flag, on PostgreSQL its `t`, `yes`, `on` and the rest too, any other text there left out. MySQL takes a JSON default only in parentheses, which DBAL never writes, so a JSON column there writes none; a SQL Server `N'…'` holding characters beyond ASCII, which DBAL would write without the N, and a PostgreSQL `E'…'` are left out. A join column an association carries writes its own default and comment, or `'default' => null` and `'comment' => ''` over its parent's, which SchemaTool would copy to it. An index is `#[ORM\Index]` and a unique one `#[ORM\UniqueConstraint]`, after `#[ORM\Table]`, its removed columns dropped; a blank name takes the one the editor's DDL gives it (`IDX_<table>`, numbered with `autoNameIgnoreCase` in the document's index order, so the one-table view writes the same), and a name out of its delimiters is written only where DBAL and ORM take it as given: of `[A-Za-z0-9_]`, no number, which ORM reads as no name, not `primary`, the key's, and no repeat of an earlier name of the table but for case; any other is left out, then one unnamed index a column set and uniqueness, since DBAL names those after their table, columns and uniqueness and refuses a second of one name. ORM keeps one index for a column set a unique constraint covers, so it folds a UQ column's own unique index into a constraint on that column alone.
    - **Names in SQL.** Doctrine quotes a reserved word in the DDL it writes but in a query only a name the mapping wraps in backticks, so every table, column and join column name gets them where `settings.bracketType` quotes names, and otherwise a word `DOCTRINE_RESERVED_WORDS` lists in any letter case (`doctrineReservedWords.ts`: every word DBAL 4 reserves on MySQL, MariaDB, PostgreSQL, SQL Server, Oracle or SQLite), a name that is no ASCII identifier and one typed in delimiters (`"Users"`, and on MySQL, MariaDB, SQLite and Databricks a table name's part in backticks), which keeps its case (an owner decision). Under `BracketType.none` an undelimited name is written as its database stores it, on Oracle each letter whose capital is one character upper case (a Greek letter with an iota below takes the capital with the iota beside it, as Oracle does) and on PostgreSQL ASCII letters lower case, so a backticked one names what the editor's DDL created. Doctrine keeps one quoted flag for a join column's two names, so a column takes backticks wherever it or a key it references in any association needs them, its field and every join column on it alike. Doctrine reads a dot in a table name as the end of a schema before it reads a backtick, and keeps one schema, so a dotted name goes out as `name:` and `schema:`, its last two parts split and unquoted as `splitNameParts` and `unquoteNamePart` read them for the SQL generators, the backticks on the table alone, which quote the schema too: `sales.order` is `name: '`order`', schema: 'sales'` under every bracket type, and `shop.sales.users` drops `shop`; a one-part name the document quotes is written whole, delimiters and all, as its DDL writes it.
    - **PHP names** are repaired as in `typeorm.ts`: a class name made an identifier, suffixed as `php.ts` does (a table with no name is `__`) and numbered against the header's imports and the other classes in any letter case, a property name made an identifier and numbered, a second column of one name dropped; letters beyond ASCII stay (an owner decision).
    - **Associations.** A relationship maps only between tables the document lists and toward the parent's key columns, the one column Doctrine lets a join column reference, so one toward a UNIQUE column keeps its columns plain fields. It maps both sides: `ManyToOne` with a `OneToMany` `Collection` the constructor fills, or `OneToOne` on both for a one relationship or for an association that is the child's one key column (`#[ORM\Id]` on it, below), which holds one child a parent and which Doctrine's validator refuses as a many-to-one; a foreign key that is the child's whole key in another way (a composite one, or one toward a parent keyed by an association) stays a `ManyToOne` beside its `#[ORM\Id]` fields, which validates. `inversedBy` and `mappedBy` name each other. Each `JoinColumn` carries its own `nullable: false`, the UNIQUE (written once a column, and left to Doctrine on a single-column one-to-one) and the options of a column the association carries (above), and `onDelete` where `referentialActionSupport` takes it, Doctrine having no ON UPDATE.
