The foreign key columns an association carries get no field of their own, but a key column may: an association is the key itself (`#[ORM\Id]` on it) only toward a parent keyed by one column of its own, since Doctrine refuses one toward a composite key at metadata load and its validator one toward a parent whose key is an association; elsewhere the key column stays an `#[ORM\Id]` field beside the association on that column. Members stand in the table's column order, an association at its first join column, since Doctrine builds the primary key in the order its members are declared. `GeneratedValue` goes on a single column integer key flagged auto increment or of a serial type; ORM 3 picks IDENTITY, which DBAL reads back from PostgreSQL only as a BY DEFAULT identity, so against the editor's GENERATED ALWAYS key every schema update adds the identity again, which PostgreSQL refuses, though inserts work, and on Oracle a sequence named `<table>_<column>_seq`, which the editor's Oracle DDL (`SEQ_<table>` and a trigger) does not create.
    - **Written as is, though Doctrine cannot use it:** a keyless table (the link tables of `data/OKKY.sql`) and a foreign key to part of a composite key (`data/Magento2-sales.sql`), which Doctrine refuses; an optional association carrying a key field or a NOT NULL column, into which ORM writes NULL when the association is null; a column name with a dot, which DBAL splits, and a dotted table name where the bracket type quotes names, which the editor's DDL creates as one identifier and Doctrine maps as a schema and a table; a join column ORM's OneToMany loader cannot reach, since it writes the name unquoted, so a parent's collection fails to load when that column is reserved, holds other characters or is quoted in a case PostgreSQL or Oracle would fold otherwise (an owner decision); a class or property name with letters beyond ASCII, which DQL and the QueryBuilder cannot name (an owner decision); a column whose name, stripped to ASCII letters, digits and the underscore, opens on a digit (`2fa_enabled`, `주소1`), which ORM's unquoted result alias makes unreadable on PostgreSQL, SQLite, SQL Server and Oracle, and on Oracle any name not opening on an ASCII letter (`이름`, `имя`), whose alias opens on an underscore; a name holding a double quote, a bracket or a backtick (a one-part name the document quotes is written with its delimiters), which DBAL's schema tools strip, so SchemaTool on an empty database creates it without them and the entity misses it, and a diff drops and recreates a table whose name holds one after its first character; a value DBAL parses only at the precision it writes itself: SQL Server's default `time` and `datetimeoffset`, MySQL `TIME(n)`, a fractional PostgreSQL `time`, `timetz` or `timestamptz`, and an Oracle DATE holding a time of day, which fail to load, and SQL Server's `datetime` and `smalldatetime`, which load but take none of the six fractional digits DBAL binds, so an insert, an update and a date parameter fail.
    - **Migration diffs against the editor's DDL keep a statement for:** a column with a definition, which DBAL's comparator compares as its Doctrine type, so on PostgreSQL each diff retypes a date or time key as VARCHAR(255), and on Oracle a NOT NULL join column toward a TIMESTAMP key or a DATE key with a default gets a MODIFY whose NOT NULL Oracle refuses, both removed by hand. DBAL reads some types only once the application maps them (`mapping_types`): PostgreSQL's arrays, bit strings and `pg_lsn` to `text`, after which they compare equal, SQL Server's `timestamp` (a rowversion) to `binary`, and SQLite's `character`, `native character`, `nchar` and `varying character` to `string`, `int2` to `smallint`, `int8` and `unsigned big int` to `bigint`. The first diff also rewrites a default DBAL reads back other than it writes it: a PostgreSQL `now()`, `transaction_timestamp()` or `LOCALTIMESTAMP`, set to CURRENT_TIMESTAMP with the column retyped TIMESTAMP(0), which drops stored fractions of a second; a SQL Server `N'…'` and a quoted `'false'` on a BIT, rewritten as a plain literal and 0; and a MySQL JSON default, which the mapping cannot carry and the diff drops, so a NOT NULL JSON column then needs a value on every insert. Statements stay for: a join column the mapping backticks (every one under a quoting bracket type, and a reserved, non-ASCII or delimited one, or one beside such a key, under `BracketType.none`), whose implicit index DBAL names from the quoted name, so the update renames an index that does not exist and fails on PostgreSQL, MySQL, SQL Server and Oracle; MySQL and MariaDB `TINYINT`, a `SMALLINT` here, which DBAL reads back as a BOOLEAN, so the update widens the column and fails where a foreign key holds it, unless the application maps `tinyint` to `smallint` (`registerDoctrineTypeMapping`); an Oracle NUMBER DBAL reads back as another integer than the one written (a bare one, NUMBER(1), NUMBER(11) to NUMBER(19), one past 20), which a diff retypes; a MySQL FLOAT default past its precision, which MySQL rounds, and a ZEROFILL one, which it pads; a PostgreSQL default with a plus, which it keeps as the expression `(+ 5)`; a plain or second unique index on a column set a unique constraint covers, which ORM drops, and on PostgreSQL the DROP INDEX of a UQ column's constraint, which PostgreSQL refuses; a MySQL `SET` with a default, which DBAL's own CREATE TABLE writes as a LONGTEXT MySQL takes no default on, though a diff leaves the editor's column alone; on SQLite, where DBAL writes comments inline, the comment of a column with a definition; and an index the mapping leaves unnamed, the unique index of a UQ column and a foreign key's index, which DBAL names `UNIQ_` or `IDX_` and a hash, so the first diff renames them.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-07 with ORM 3.7.4 and DBAL 4.2.5 on PHP 8.1: the mapping for the five `data/*.sql` fixtures validated with no error but the two refused kinds and wrote its schema for SQLite, MySQL, MariaDB, PostgreSQL, SQL Server and Oracle, and php-cs-fixer's `@PER-CS3.0` left it as it was; on SQLite entities stored and read back a reserved table and reserved columns, a date key, a one-to-one key, a dotted table name and an `#[ORM\Id]` field beside its association. On 2026-10-09 the second review ran its findings and the fixes against MySQL 8.4, MariaDB 11.4, PostgreSQL 17, SQL Server 2022 and Oracle 23ai, and the fixtures, `sakila.sql` also under every other database setting and the backtick bracket type, passed PHP 8.1 and 8.4, the validator (the same two refused kinds), the schema on all six platforms and `@PER-CS3.0` again.
  - `rust.ts` writes plain structs (owner decisions of 2026-10-09): `#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]` and `pub` fields typed by full path (`chrono::NaiveDateTime`, `rust_decimal::Decimal`, `uuid::Uuid`, `serde_json::Value`) with no `use` line, so the whole text and one table both compile against those four crates with no feature; the one-table view is `formatTable`, as in the other plain generators but `php.ts` and `swift.ts`, whose one-table view opens on the header a file of its own needs (`createTableCode`). A primary key or a NOT NULL column is `T`, any other `Option<T>`. A comment is `//` lines, never `///`, which would turn a fenced or indented line into a doctest, its bidirectional controls written as `\u{…}`, which rustc refuses in any comment. Names follow the name cases, a name off Rust's own only warning (`non_snake_case` on a camelCase field, `non_camel_case_types` on a snake_case struct): a keyword of any edition from 2015 to 2024 is a raw identifier (`r#type`, `r#gen`), `_`, `crate`, `self`, `Self` and `super`, which take none, get `_` after them, and so does a struct named after a type or crate the output writes (`Option`, `String`, `Vec`, `bool`, the integer and float primitives it uses, `chrono`, `rust_decimal`, `uuid`, `serde_json`), which would break, or silently retype, the fields of that type; any other name that is no identifier, or a repeated one, is written as is, as in `php.ts`.
    - **Types** come from `rustTypes.ts`, the shared classifier's Rust layer (above), which `seaorm.ts` reads too, by name before the shared vendor tables: integers at their width (`i8`, `i16`; `u8` to `u64` for MySQL and MariaDB UNSIGNED, ZEROFILL and SERIAL), MySQL and MariaDB `BIT` a `bool` or a `u64` past one bit, `YEAR` a `u16` and `TIMESTAMP` a `chrono::DateTime<chrono::Utc>`, as are the local time zone timestamps and Databricks' `TIMESTAMP`; the time zone timestamps a `chrono::DateTime<chrono::FixedOffset>`; binary types `Vec<u8>`, `json` and `jsonb` a `serde_json::Value`, `uuid` and `uniqueidentifier` a `uuid::Uuid` but MariaDB's, which it sends as text, a `String`; money a `Decimal`, an interval, a PostgreSQL bit string, `timetz`, `pg_lsn` and `cid`, `oid`, `xid`, `xid8` a `String`; an Oracle DATE a `NaiveDateTime` and its REAL an `f64`; Snowflake's and Databricks' `VARIANT`, `OBJECT`, `ARRAY` and `MAP` and Databricks' `STRUCT` a `String`, as the shared vendor tables class them; a PostgreSQL array one `Vec` a dimension, at every depth. On PostgreSQL a `String` column of any type but its character types carries the type sqlx reads it through (`selectAs`, `text`, or `numeric` for money), and so does an array of more than one dimension (`text`), which `seaorm.ts` declares a `String` (`entityScalar`). PostgreSQL's and SQLite's `dec`, bare or with arguments, which their vendor lists gained beside `numeric`, reads as the decimal it is, a `rust_decimal::Decimal` where it was a `String` before (an owner decision of 2026-10-10): the one change to the output of `rust.ts` and `seaorm.ts` since the classifier moved, made by the lists, which `rustPrimitiveType` reads, gaining `dec` (`rust.test.ts` and `seaorm.test.ts` pin it).
    - **Written as is:** MySQL and MariaDB spatial types as a `String`, which sqlx reads into nothing; a PostgreSQL array of two or more dimensions as nested `Vec`s, which sqlx does not decode; a MySQL TIME past a day and a PostgreSQL numeric past 28 digits, which the chrono and rust_decimal types cannot hold; SQLite's `DEC`, `DECIMAL` and `NUMERIC`, with or without arguments, as a `rust_decimal::Decimal`, which sqlx-sqlite leaves out on purpose and neither reads nor binds; on SQLite a `String` or `serde_json::Value` in a column of numeric affinity (`JSON`, `TIMESTAMP`, `TIME`, a type imported from another vendor such as `bit(1)`), which sqlx cannot read back once SQLite stores text that looks like a number, a JSON number among it, as an INTEGER or a REAL; a UUID another client stored as text in SQLite, which `uuid::Uuid` cannot read; SQL Server's money, which tiberius reads as an `f64` where the field is a `Decimal`; an Oracle `INT`, `INTEGER` or `SMALLINT`, a Snowflake integer and a SQLite integer type at the width its name declares (`i32`; SQLite's `SMALLINT` and `INT2` an `i16`, its `TINYINT` an `i8`), though those databases store a NUMBER(38) or eight bytes, so a SQLite rowid past 2^31 − 1 is more than an `i32` holds: the classifier gives every other generator 64 bits there, while Rust and SeaORM keep the output they had, a gap reported to the owner on 2026-10-10.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-09 with rustc 1.98.0, editions 2021 and 2024, chrono 0.4.45, rust_decimal 1.43.0, uuid 1.27.0 and serde_json 1.0.151: every type of every vendor list, NOT NULL and optional, and every keyword as a struct and a field name compiled, with only the naming lints, and rustfmt changed only the opening blank line; the five `data/*.sql` fixtures compiled whole and per table with the naming lints alone; sqlx 0.9.0 read and wrote back each type of PostgreSQL 17, MySQL 8.4, MariaDB 11.4 and SQLite as its Rust type, through `selectAs` where it carries one, but for the MySQL and MariaDB spatial types and the SQLite decimals and numeric-affinity values under Written as is; PostgreSQL's `dec` and SQLite's `DEC`, listed since, write the `rust_decimal::Decimal` that `numeric` and `DECIMAL` write and were not compiled again.
  - `seaorm.ts` writes SeaORM 2.0 entities in its dense form (`#[sea_orm::model]`, relations as `BelongsTo` / `HasOne` / `HasMany` fields, no `Relation` enum), each table a `pub mod` so the whole text compiles as one file, and the one-table view that module's file body unwrapped, so `index.ts` hands it to `createTableCode` (owner decisions of 2026-10-09). It ignores the name cases: fields and modules are heck's snake_case, which DeriveEntityModel reads back (`heck.ts`, a port of heck 0.5), with `table_name` always and `column_name` where the stored name differs, PostgreSQL folding an undelimited name to lower case under `BracketType.none`, a dotted table name split into `schema_name` and `table_name`, at a dot inside backticks too, since `splitNameParts` knows no backticks (`` `a.b`.c `` writes `` schema_name = "b`" ``). A keyword column takes `_` (`type_`), since `r#` on a field breaks `unique`, `unique_key` and `from`, and a keyword module or unique key takes it too, which keeps one spelling; a keyword relation field takes `r#` (`r#match`), but `self`, `crate` and `super`, which rustc takes no `r#` on, take `_`, so an optional one warns `non_snake_case` on the `set_self__option` setter the macros derive. A character no identifier holds parts words as a space does, so it leaves no run of underscores for the derived setters to warn on (`a ½b` writes `a_b`); a name opening on a digit or on U+0345, which can continue an identifier only, takes `column_`, `table_` or `unique_` before it (`rustBase`), and one with nothing left that word alone; an unnamed composite UNIQUE is named from its fields joined, through the same repair (`type_name`, not `type__name`). A module named beyond ASCII (`주문`) compiles in the one file but needs `#[path]` once moved to a file of its own. Names are repaired and numbered: `a1_2` beside `a_1`, whose Column variants match; `self_2` on a unique `self`, whose `find_by_` would name `Column::Self`; `column_2` on a key column named `column`, or with no name to make one from, whose variant would make the derived `Self::Column` ambiguous; `parent_key_2` for a field named `parent_key`, `parent_key_for` or `parent_key_for_self_rev`, whose setter would shadow the `ActiveModelTrait` method the derived relation code calls; `writer_2` where a column holds `writer_option`; `id_2` for a composite UNIQUE named `id`, whose derived `find_by_id` would hide `EntityTrait::find_by_id`.
    - **Types** come from `rustTypes.ts` in the prelude's spelling (`Decimal`, `DateTimeWithTimeZone`, `Json`); a PostgreSQL array of more than one dimension is a `String` read as text. PostgreSQL's and SQLite's `dec` is a `Decimal`, as in `rust.ts` (an owner decision of 2026-10-10): `custom("dec")` bare and `Decimal(Some((p, s)))` with arguments on PostgreSQL, with no `select_as` or `save_as`, and `custom` with `select_as = "REAL"` on SQLite, where it was a `String` read through `text` or `TEXT` before. `column_type` is written only where SeaORM's schema builder would write another type than the editor's DDL, in a native spelling where `postgresColumnType` or `mysqlColumnType` has one for the type and its arguments, else `custom("<type>")`, which ZEROFILL, a non-integer UNSIGNED, a PostgreSQL array and an argument past the `u32` the native spellings hold also take. The native spellings are `String(StringLen::N(n))` for a varchar, `Char(Some(n))` and `Char(None)`, `Decimal(Some((p, s)))`, a bare `Text`, `Bit(Some(n))` and `Bit(None)` on PostgreSQL, MySQL and MariaDB; `Binary(n)`, `VarBinary(StringLen::N(n))`, a bare `Blob` and `Year` on MySQL and MariaDB; and on PostgreSQL `VarBit(n)` and, for the bare type, `JsonBinary`, `Money(None)`, `Interval(None, None)`, `Inet`, `Cidr` and `MacAddr`, so `ltree`, `interval(3)` and MySQL's `TINYTEXT` are `custom`. SQLite takes `custom` by type affinity, and SQL Server, Oracle, Snowflake and Databricks none, which SeaORM has no backend for. On PostgreSQL a type sqlx cannot read is read through `select_as` and written through `save_as`, the column's type as typed, so an `inet` host, in an array too, reads back with its mask (`10.0.0.1/32`). On SQLite, whose numeric affinities store a whole number or numeric text as a number, a `Decimal`, which SeaORM reads as an `f64`, takes `select_as = "REAL"`, which rounds one past 15 to 17 significant digits and fails with `TryIntoErr` near `Decimal`'s maximum, and a `Json` or `String` in such a column `select_as = "TEXT"`. `Eq` is dropped beside an `f32` or `f64`.
    - **Keys and relations.** `auto_increment = false` on a key column without the flag, and nothing for the flag outside the key, where the macros refuse `auto_increment`; `unique`, a table's only key column included but where PostgreSQL, or SQLite on a key it does not make its rowid, folds that UNIQUE into the primary key, and `unique_key` on a composite UNIQUE index, a second one sharing a column left out; comments as `comment`; no default and no plain index (owner decisions). Relations follow the editor's relationships, read pair by pair: `belongs_to` on the child, `has_one` or `has_many` on the parent by cardinality, `self_ref` with `relation_reverse` on a self relationship, `relation_enum` and `via_rel` where one table pair has several relationships written, named by their foreign key through `graphql.ts`'s `stripIdWord` (`author`, `posts_by_author_list`, the list word last, which keeps apart the `add_` setters the macros name from the singular); a one side there or on a self relationship is a `HasMany`, SeaORM taking no `HasOne` in either. A link table gets no `via`. Referential actions follow `referentialActionSupport`.
    - **Written as is, though SeaORM cannot use it:** a keyless table; a primary key on a PostgreSQL array, or of more than twelve columns, and a foreign key of more than twelve, past the keys and tuples SeaORM implements; an auto-increment column in a composite primary key, an insert leaving it to the database writing the row on MySQL and MariaDB and then failing with `UnpackInsertId`, and on SQLite, where the editor's DDL writes no AUTOINCREMENT on a composite key, failing NOT NULL; a PostgreSQL `serial`, `serial2`, `serial4`, `serial8`, `smallserial` or `bigserial` column without the NN flag, an `Option<T>` by the rule both generators share (a key or NN column `T`, any other `Option<T>`), though PostgreSQL makes it NOT NULL, so a `Set(None)` insert fails where `NotSet` passes; a unique column named `id` outside the key, whose derived `find_by_id` shadows `EntityTrait::find_by_id`; a foreign key to part of a composite key (`data/Magento2-sales.sql`), which MySQL refuses in the editor's DDL and again in a schema sync; a composite UNIQUE index left out for sharing a column with an earlier one, or whose columns run against the table's order, which schema sync drops or rebuilds in table order on MySQL and stops on with `constraint … does not exist` on PostgreSQL, and a DESC column in one, which `unique_key` cannot carry; MySQL and MariaDB spatial types, and a MySQL TIME past a day and a PostgreSQL numeric past 28 digits, as in `rust.ts`; a UUID another client stored as text in SQLite, where SeaORM writes and reads 16 bytes, so `find` fails with `expected 16 bytes, found 36`; a MariaDB server in `sql_mode=ORACLE`, a mode the editor does not know, which makes a DATE a DATETIME that a `Date` field fails to read; nested writes through the 2.0 builder into a pair with several relationships, which SeaORM skips; an Oracle, Snowflake or SQLite integer at the width its name declares, as in `rust.ts`.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-09 with sea-orm 2.0.4 on rustc 1.98 (editions 2021 and 2024): the output compiled, matched rustfmt but for the leading blank line and a line of Hangul, which rustfmt counts at double width, as it does Han and Kana, where `formatAttribute` counts a column a character, and ran insert, find, find_related, load and nested load on PostgreSQL 17, MySQL 8.4, MariaDB 11.4 and SQLite against the editor's DDL, SQLite with whole-number decimals and numeric JSON and text too, where schema sync changed nothing, though the DDL SeaORM writes from the entities differs from the editor's in what sync leaves alone (a PostgreSQL identity `BY DEFAULT`, not `ALWAYS`, constraint and index names, no PostgreSQL comments); the five `data/*.sql` fixtures compiled but for their keyless tables, and without them selected every entity on MySQL 8.4 and MariaDB 11.4, where schema sync changed nothing but for the foreign keys MySQL refuses.
  - `swift.ts` writes one plain struct per table, sorted by name (owner decisions of 2026-10-09): `nonisolated struct X: Codable, Hashable, Sendable` with internal `var` properties and no `Identifiable`, initializer or default, a primary key (its NN flag set or not, the owner decision above) or a NOT NULL column `T`, any other `T?`, so the memberwise init leaves an optional column out. `nonisolated` keeps the conformances usable off the main actor in a module whose default isolation is MainActor, as Xcode 26's new app targets set (Apple's release notes, not measured), and makes Swift 6.1 (Xcode 16.3) the floor, since Swift 6.0.3 refuses it on a struct. `import Foundation` (`Date`, `Data`, `Decimal`, `UUID`) follows the blank line the other languages open with, whatever the columns hold, since an unused import draws no warning, and opens the one-table view too, so `index.ts` hands that view to `createTableCode`, as for PHP, Doctrine and SeaORM, and a table pasted alone into a file compiles. A document with no table is `''`, an empty table `nonisolated struct X: Codable, Hashable, Sendable {}`. Codable reads and writes the column names: a nested `enum CodingKeys: String, CodingKey` is written only where some property's name differs from its column's, one `case` a property, with `= "<column name>"` where they differ, the literal giving `"` and `\` (which would open an interpolation) a backslash and writing every C0 control and DEL, which a Swift string literal refuses raw, as `\u{…}`. A comment is `///` lines above its struct or property, Quick Help's and DocC's form, from which Swift runs no doctest: split at CR, LF, CRLF, U+2028 and U+2029 (`splitLines`), since a lone CR ends a line comment, each line's end trimmed, blank lines at either end dropped and those inside written as a bare `///`, a NUL written `\u{0}`, which Swift warns about, and every other character left as is, the bidirectional controls rustc refuses included. The panel highlights it with Shiki's `swift` grammar and saves it as `.swift`.
    - **Names** follow the name cases; Swift is case sensitive (`date` does not shadow `Date`) and warns about no naming style. A struct named after a type or protocol the output writes unqualified (`TYPE_NAMES`: `Bool`, `Codable`, `CodingKey`, `Data`, `Date`, `Decimal`, `Double`, `Float`, `Hashable`, the eight integer types, `Sendable`, `String`, `UUID`) gets `_` after it (`Date_`), as in `rust.ts`, since one breaks the conformances or silently retypes every field of its type; `Foundation`, `Swift`, `Optional`, `Array` and `CodingKeys` keep their names, nothing being qualified, `T?` and `[T]` always naming the standard library's types and the nested enum shadowing a table `CodingKeys`. A word the Swift lexer reserves (`SWIFT_KEYWORDS`, the 54 of `TokenKinds.def`: `class`, `default`, `self`, `Self`, `Any`, `_`, `inout`, …) goes in backticks as a struct, property or case name, and so does a property `Type` or `Protocol`, which would clash with the metatype it names; a call site writes the memberwise init's `inout`, `var`, `let` and `_` labels in backticks too. A property named `CodingKeys` or `RawValue` (`RESERVED_KEYS`) gets `_` after it and is keyed to its column, since the first breaks the synthesized conformance and the second the `String` raw type of an explicit `CodingKeys`, backticks or not, so a `RawValue` column always writes the enum. Any other name that is no identifier (a space, a leading digit, a name a name case empties, as `_` under camelCase) or a repeated one is written as is, as in `rust.ts` and `php.ts` (an owner decision); Swift 6.2's raw identifiers in backticks would compile some of them, but 6.1 refuses them.
    - **Types** come from the shared classifier (`getColumnType`, `columnTypes.ts`, an owner decision) through one table (`SWIFT_SCALARS`), one `[…]` a PostgreSQL array dimension at every depth (`int[][]` gives `[[Int32]]`), which Codable decodes at any depth. Integers keep their exact width (an owner decision): `Int8` to `Int64` (`INT` an `Int32`), and `UInt8` to `UInt64` for MySQL and MariaDB UNSIGNED, ZEROFILL and SERIAL (`MEDIUMINT UNSIGNED` a `UInt32`, `BIGINT UNSIGNED` and `SERIAL` a `UInt64`), their `YEAR` (`UInt16`) and `BIT(n)` past one bit (`UInt64`), and SQL Server's `tinyint` (`UInt8`); but every integer type of Oracle and Snowflake, which store a NUMBER(38), and of SQLite, which stores eight bytes, is an `Int64`, and so are an Oracle or Snowflake `NUMBER` with no fraction and the bare one. Floats are `Float` or `Double`, `float(p)` by p on PostgreSQL, SQL Server, MySQL and MariaDB, Oracle's `FLOAT` and `REAL` a `Double` and its `BINARY_FLOAT` a `Float`, MariaDB's bare `NUMBER` (a DOUBLE in its Oracle mode) a `Double`. `Decimal` is `decimal`, `numeric`, `dec` and `fixed`, SQL Server's `money` and `smallmoney`, Snowflake's `DECFLOAT`, MariaDB's `NUMBER(p)` and `NUMBER(p,s)`, and an Oracle or Snowflake NUMBER with a scale above 0. `Bool` is a boolean, MySQL's and MariaDB's `BIT` and `BIT(1)` too, while their `TINYINT(1)` stays an `Int8`; `Data` the binary types, SQL Server's `rowversion` and `timestamp` among them; `UUID` `uuid` and `uniqueidentifier`; `Date` a `date` and every timestamp and datetime type, Oracle's `DATE`, MySQL's `TIMESTAMP` and SQL Server's `datetimeoffset` among them. `String` is a time of day, `timetz` and an interval, which Foundation has no type for, JSON (an owner decision: Swift has no Codable any-value type), Snowflake's and Databricks' `VARIANT`, `OBJECT`, `ARRAY`, `MAP` and `STRUCT`, Databricks' `VOID`, the character, `ENUM`, `SET`, spatial and network types, a PostgreSQL bit string and `pg_lsn`, and any type no rule names. Where a Swift reader gets text, three choices override the classifier: MariaDB's `UUID` is a `String`, since MariaDB sends 36 characters and MySQLNIO reads a UUID only from 16 bytes (its source read, not run); PostgreSQL's `money` is a `String`, SQL Server's staying a `Decimal`, and so are PostgreSQL's `oid`, `xid`, `cid` and `xid8`, since PostgreSQL's JSON (`row_to_json`) writes them as text (`"$12.34"`, `"42"`), which `Decimal`, `UInt32` and `UInt64` refuse, and PostgresNIO has no unsigned decoder (its source read). An imported MySQL or MariaDB schema keeps UNSIGNED and ZEROFILL (an owner decision), so sakila's `SMALLINT UNSIGNED` keys are `UInt16`.
    - **Written as is, though a Swift program has to adjust it:** a name that is no identifier or a repeated one (above); a bare Oracle `NUMBER` or `NUMBER(*)`, which keeps any scale, as an `Int64`, as `rust.ts`'s `i64`, so a fractional value (`1.5`) is refused, as is a value past 19 digits in any Oracle or Snowflake integer column; a decimal wider than `Decimal`'s 128-bit mantissa (38 or 39 digits), as PostgreSQL's `numeric` and MySQL's and MariaDB's `DECIMAL` (up to 65) can be, whose last digits `Decimal` drops with no error; a time zone offset, which `Date`, an instant, drops (`timestamptz`, `datetimeoffset`, `TIMESTAMP_TZ`). `JSONEncoder` and `JSONDecoder` take a `Date` as seconds since 2001 unless the coder sets a date strategy, and under `.iso8601` a naive timestamp or a date as PostgreSQL's `row_to_json` writes them (`2026-10-09T12:34:56`, `2026-10-09`) and the space-separated datetimes of MySQL's and MariaDB's `JSON_OBJECT` are refused, while a `timestamptz` with fractional seconds decodes on macOS and not on Linux Swift 6.1. A database's own JSON fails elsewhere too: a `jsonb` object or array written inline, which a `String` refuses (it reads the text a driver hands over), as it does the GeoJSON object MySQL writes for a spatial column; PostgreSQL's bytea (`"\\xdeadbeef"`) and Oracle's RAW in hex (`"0102FF"`), which `Data`'s default base64 refuses; a MySQL or MariaDB boolean written as `1`, which `Bool` refuses; and a NULL item in a PostgreSQL array (`[1,null]`), since an array's items are never optional (`[Int32]`). Databricks' `VOID`, which holds only NULL, is a `String`; an auto-increment key is a non-optional `T`, where GRDB's idiom is `var id: Int64?` filled after the insert; the memberwise init is internal, so another module needs a `public init` written by hand; a `CodingKeys` case past swift-format's 100 columns stays on one line.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-09, by the design research, with Apple Swift 6.4 (swiftlang-6.4.0.34.1, Command Line Tools) and the Linux `swift` images 6.4, 6.2.4, 6.1.3 and 6.0.3: the keyword sweep (249 words in six forms, Swift 5 and 6, 2,988 typechecks) behind `SWIFT_KEYWORDS`, the backticked `Type` and `Protocol` and the `CodingKeys` rename; the shadowing sweep (51 names) behind `TYPE_NAMES`; the MainActor isolation errors a plain `struct` gets and `nonisolated` removes; GRDB 7.11.1 writing and reading back every SQLite list type through the struct against the editor's DDL, which failed without the coding keys (`no column named c0Nn`); swift-format with 4-space indentation changing only the opening blank line and three Magento2-sales cases past 100 columns. On 2026-10-10, on the shared classifier: 5,782 files (every vendor list name and its other spellings on all eight databases, the five `data/*.sql` fixtures on all eight under two name-case settings, the keyword corpus, whole and per table) typechecked with no diagnostic under `-warnings-as-errors` on macOS swiftc 6.4 (`-swift-version 5` and `6`, each also with `-default-isolation MainActor`) and Linux Swift 6.1.3 (5 and 6), and Swift 6.0.3 still refused `nonisolated`; JSON round trips keyed every property by its column name and decoded back `==` on both, inside `Task.detached` in a MainActor-default module too; GRDB 7.11.1 round-tripped every SQLite list type with every optional set and with none, and read 3,000,000,000 from nine SQLite integer columns, which an `Int32` refuses; PostgreSQL 18.6's and 17's `row_to_json` wrote `money`, `oid`, `xid`, `cid` and `xid8` as text, and `JSONDecoder` refused `1.5` and `12345678901234567890` for an `Int64` on macOS 6.4 and Linux 6.1.3. An adversarial run over the vendor lists in both letter cases and 186 other spellings, 218 words under all 16 name-case pairs, 23 kinds of comment and the fixtures under three name cases, on macOS 6.4 in the four modes and Linux 6.1.3, failed only on the names written as is and on a `RawValue` column, the case behind `RESERVED_KEYS`' `RawValue`. A macOS swiftc 6.4 run on 2026-10-10: 520 files (the 492 vendor list names, PostgreSQL's `dec` and SQLite's `DEC` among them, and 67 other spellings as a key without the NN flag, a NOT NULL and an optional column, the names the adversarial run failed on under four name-case settings, the five fixtures on all eight databases whole and on MySQL and PostgreSQL per table) typechecked under `-warnings-as-errors` with `-swift-version 5`, with `6` and with `6` and `-default-isolation MainActor`, every one but the names written as is, and the decodes under Written as is were run on structs the generator wrote (`Decimal` dropping the last digit of a 40-digit integer, `Bool` refusing `1`, `.iso8601` refusing `2026-10-09 12:34:56.000000`, `Data` refusing `"0102FF"`, `[Int32]` refusing `[1,null]`).
  - `typescript.ts` writes one `export interface` per table, typed as the `z.infer` of the `zod.ts` schema of the same table (owner decisions of 2026-10-09), so it describes a row as an API sends it in JSON: a decimal as text, dates and times as ISO text, binary as base64, a JSON value as `unknown`. The whole text opens with a blank line and writes the tables in `orderByNameASC` order, each followed by a blank line, with no import; a document with no table writes nothing; the one-table view is `formatTable`, as in the other plain generators. A primary key, its NOT NULL flag set or not (an owner decision), or a NOT NULL column is `T`, any other `T | null` (`isNullableColumn`, `jsonShape.ts`, which `zod.ts` reads too), except `unknown` and `null`, which hold null already. A comment is `//` lines written by `zod.ts`'s `formatComment`, so the two generators stay alike: one line for each line at CR, LF, CRLF, U+2028 and U+2029 (`splitLines`, `utils.ts`), its trailing white space trimmed, the blank lines at either end dropped and each one inside written `//`, so a blank comment writes nothing, and a line TypeScript would read as a directive taking a backslash before its at sign (No TypeScript directive in a comment, above); a table's comment sits above its interface, a column's above its property, indented two spaces. U+0085, NUL and `*/` stay in their line, since TypeScript ends a line comment at none of them.
    - **Types** come from `getJsonShape` (`jsonShape.ts`), the JSON shape `zod.ts` and `jsonSchema.ts` read, which classes a type by its name through `columnTypes.ts`, not by the vendor lists' `primitiveType` alone: `boolean` for `BOOLEAN`, SQL Server's `bit` and MySQL's and MariaDB's `BIT` and `BIT(1)` (`TINYINT(1)` and a longer `BIT(n)` stay a `number`); `number` for every integer and float, a 64-bit one exact only to 2^53 − 1 included (`BIGINT`, `BIGINT UNSIGNED`, `SERIAL`, Oracle's bare `NUMBER` and every Snowflake and SQLite integer; an owner decision), PostgreSQL's `oid`, MariaDB's bare `NUMBER` (a DOUBLE) and SQLite's `DECIMAL`, `NUMERIC` and `DEC`; `string` for a decimal or money type on every other database (an owner decision), Oracle's and Snowflake's `NUMBER` with a scale, MariaDB's `NUMBER(p)` and Snowflake's `DECFLOAT` among them, and for a character type, a uuid (SQL Server's `uniqueidentifier` and MariaDB's `UUID` too), a date, a time and a timestamp with or without a zone, an interval, binary, a bit string, `pg_lsn`, PostgreSQL's `xid`, `cid` and `xid8`, which pg hands over as text, a network or spatial type, a SET and any type the classifier does not know; `unknown` for JSON on every database and for Snowflake's `VARIANT`, `OBJECT` and `ARRAY` and Databricks' `STRUCT`, `MAP`, `ARRAY` and `VARIANT`; `null` for Databricks' `VOID`; for an ENUM the union of its members as JSON string literals, each once, in their order (`"it's" | "say \"hi\""`), the spaces that end a member dropped on MySQL and MariaDB, which drop them, and kept on PostgreSQL; for a PostgreSQL array (`int[]`, `int[3]`, `integer ARRAY`) one `[]` a dimension around its element type, never null inside, and in parentheses where that is a union of two members or more (`number[][]`, `("a" | "b")[]`, `"only"[]`), and an array of a type no list names (`"mood"[]`) a `string[]`. The interface and `z.infer` differ in two places only: a JSON column is `unknown` where `z.infer` gives Zod's `JSONType`, which assigns to `unknown` but not the reverse, and a table with no column is `export interface X {` and `}`, which takes any object, where `z.infer` gives `Record<string, never>`. A table named `as` or `z` keeps that name here, where Zod's type gets `_`.
    - **Names** follow the name cases. An interface named with a word an ES module refuses for one (the 56 of `RESERVED_TYPE_NAMES`: the reserved words, the strict mode ones such as `let`, `yield`, `await` and `package`, and the predefined type names `any`, `unknown`, `never`, `number`, `bigint`, `boolean`, `string`, `symbol`, `object`, `undefined` and `void`) gets `_` after it (`class_`), which only a lower-case name meets, so none under PascalCase; contextual keywords (`type`, `as`, `async`, `declare`, `global`, `infer`) and global type names (`Number`, `Date`, `Record`) compile as they are, and shadow nothing the interfaces use, all of whose types are keywords. A property key is written bare where TypeScript reads an identifier (`class`, `__proto__`, `$x`, `주소1`) and as a JSON string where it does not (`"first name"`, `"2FaEnabled"`, `""`); TypeScript reads identifiers by Unicode 15.1, so a key holding a character Unicode 16.0 or 17.0 added is quoted too (`NEWER_IDENTIFIER_RUNS`), which `jsonShape.test.ts` checks against TypeScript's own scanner for every code point of planes 0 to 3 and 14. Any other name that is no identifier is written as is, as in `php.ts` and `rust.ts`, and so is a repeated one (an owner decision of 2026-10-09: a compile error shows the repeat), though a repeated interface name compiles (Written as is).
    - **Written as is:** an interface name that is no identifier, which the name cases can make (`3DModels`, PascalCase of `3d_models`; `3d models` under no name case) and TypeScript refuses (TS1351, TS2427); a key a name case writes twice in one table (TS2300); a table name a name case writes twice, whose interfaces merge into one without an error unless the same key has another type in each (TS2717); a key holding a character a Unicode later than 17.0 adds outside `NEWER_IDENTIFIER_RUNS`, which a runtime that knows it writes bare and TypeScript then refuses (TS1127), until `jsonShape.test.ts` fails on a Node that knows it. The interface stands for the JSON row, so a value that reaches code in another form does not fit it: drivers hand over a 64-bit integer as text (pg for PostgreSQL's `bigint`, tedious for SQL Server's) or as a `BigInt` (mariadb), SQL Server's decimal as a number (tedious), a date or timestamp as a `Date`, binary and mysql2's `BIT` as a `Buffer`, mysql2's `BOOLEAN` as `1`, PostgreSQL's `interval` and `point` as objects, its `numeric[]` as numbers and `int8[]` as text, an array of a bit string or of a user-defined type as one text (`"{sad,ok}"`) and a NULL array element as null; the servers' own JSON (PostgreSQL's `row_to_json`, which PostgREST and Supabase build on, MySQL's and MariaDB's `JSON_OBJECT`, SQL Server's `FOR JSON`, Oracle's `JSON_OBJECT`) writes a decimal as a number, which the owner kept a string, PostgreSQL's `oid` and a float's `NaN` and `±Infinity` as text, MySQL and MariaDB a `BOOLEAN` as `1`, MySQL a `BIT` as `"base64:type16:…"` text and MariaDB one unquoted, which is no JSON; MySQL's and MariaDB's DATETIME and TIMESTAMP with a space for the `T`, PostgreSQL's `infinity` and BC dates, and PostgreSQL's `bytea` and Oracle's `RAW` in hex pass as a `string`, though they are not the ISO and base64 text the interface stands for; MySQL and MariaDB store the ENUM error value `''` outside strict mode, which the union refuses.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-10 with Node 24.16.0: 168 whole texts (252 type spellings, every name of the eight vendor lists and forms with arguments, each as a NOT NULL, a nullable and a key column without the flag, under each database; the five `data/*.sql` fixtures under each database and four name-case pairs) and their 8,224 one-table views compiled with no error on TypeScript 5.9.3, 6.0.3 and 7.0.2 (`strict`, `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`, `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`, `isolatedModules`, `isolatedDeclarations`, lib ES2022), and each of the 8,224 interfaces was identical on all three, by a type identity check, to the `z.infer` of the `zod.ts` schema of the same table under zod 4.6.5, `unknown` read as `z.json()`'s type, where four altered interfaces each failed the check; each of the 56 reserved names failed as an interface name and compiled with its `_`, and contextual keywords and global type names compiled as they are; Prettier 3.9.9 changed only the opening blank line of the 168 whole texts, and elsewhere puts an empty interface's braces on one line, breaks a line past 80 columns (a long ENUM union) and single-quotes a string holding a double quote. The driver values under Written as is come from pg 8.23.1 on PostgreSQL 17.11, mysql2 3.24.5 on MySQL 8.4.11 and MariaDB 11.4.13, the mariadb connector 3.5.4, and tedious 20.3.3 on SQL Server 2022, run on 2026-10-09 and 2026-10-10, and the servers' own JSON from PostgreSQL 17.11, MySQL 8.4.11, MariaDB 11.4.13, SQL Server 2022 (16.0.4295.3) and Oracle AI Database 26ai Free (23.26.3) on 2026-10-10.
  - `zod.ts` writes one Zod 4 row schema a table, `export const <Name>Schema = z.object({…});` then `export type <Name> = z.infer<typeof <Name>Schema>;`, under `import * as z from "zod";`, the tables in `orderByNameASC` order and their columns in table order (owner decisions of 2026-10-09: a schema validates a row's JSON shape, as an API sends it, not what a driver returns; a row schema only, with no insert schema and no relation; comments as `//` lines). The text opens with the blank line the other languages open with, then the import line, each table after a blank line; a document with no table writes nothing; the one-table view carries the import line too, so its file compiles alone, and `index.ts` hands it to `createTableCode`. A table with no column is `z.object({})`. A primary key, its NN flag set or not, or a NOT NULL column takes its schema alone, any other adds `.nullable()`, `z.json()` and `z.null()` included though both take null already (`isNullableColumn`); `z.object` strips a key the table lacks, so a joined row parses. A comment is `//` lines (`formatComment`, which `typescript.ts` writes its comments through): split at CR, LF, CRLF, U+2028 and U+2029 (`splitLines`), each line's trailing white space dropped, the blank lines at either end dropped and one inside written `//`, a line TypeScript would read as a directive taking a backslash before its at sign (No TypeScript directive in a comment, above), a table's above `export const` and a column's above its key. The panel highlights it as TypeScript and saves it as `.ts`.
    - **Types** come from `getJsonShape` (`jsonShape.ts`), the JSON shape of a column's value read from `columnTypes.ts`, which `jsonSchema.ts` and `typescript.ts` read too, so the three agree column by column: a kind, an integer's bounds, a character type's length, an ENUM's members and a PostgreSQL array's depth. The owner decided on 2026-10-09 that a `date` is `z.iso.date()`, a `time` `z.iso.time()`, a naive timestamp `z.iso.datetime({ local: true })` (`timestamp`, `datetime`, `datetime2`, `smalldatetime`, `TIMESTAMP_NTZ`, Oracle's `DATE` and `TIMESTAMP`, SQLite's `TIMESTAMP`), a zoned or instant one `z.iso.datetime({ offset: true })` (`timestamptz`, `datetimeoffset`, the `WITH TIME ZONE`, `WITH LOCAL TIME ZONE`, `_TZ` and `_LTZ` types, MySQL's, MariaDB's and Databricks' `TIMESTAMP`), a decimal or money type `z.string()` but SQLite's `z.number()`, its NUMERIC affinity storing a number, a 64-bit integer `z.int()`, binary `z.base64()`, JSON `z.json()` and a boolean `z.boolean()`. Integers go by width: a 32-bit signed one is `z.int32()`, a 32-bit unsigned one `z.uint32()` (`INT UNSIGNED`, `INT ZEROFILL`, PostgreSQL's `oid`), a narrower one `z.int()` with its bounds (`TINYINT` −128 to 127, `SMALLINT` −32768 to 32767, MySQL's and MariaDB's `MEDIUMINT` −8388608 to 8388607 or, unsigned, 0 to 16777215, `YEAR` and `SMALLINT UNSIGNED` 0 to 65535, `TINYINT UNSIGNED` and SQL Server's `tinyint` 0 to 255) and a 64-bit unsigned one `z.int().min(0)` (`BIGINT UNSIGNED`, `SERIAL`, MySQL's and MariaDB's `BIT(n)` past one bit), Zod having no 8-, 16- or 64-bit integer that `z.toJSONSchema` converts; every Oracle, Snowflake and SQLite integer is `z.int()`, a `SMALLINT` and a `NUMBER`, `NUMBER(p)` or `NUMBER(p,0)` included, since those databases store 64 bits or more. A float is `z.number()`, and so is MariaDB's bare `NUMBER`, a DOUBLE there. A character type is `z.string().max(n)` from its declared length (`varchar(n)`, `nvarchar(n)`, `VARCHAR2(n BYTE)` or `VARCHAR2(n CHAR)`, Snowflake's `STRING(n)` and `TEXT(n)`, and `char(n)`, not `.length(n)`, since MySQL and MariaDB hand `char(n)` over unpadded); `varchar(max)` and the text types take no `.max`. `uuid` and MariaDB's and Snowflake's `UUID` are `z.uuid()`, SQL Server's `uniqueidentifier` `z.guid()`, which takes the GUIDs outside RFC 9562 that `z.uuid()` refuses, and MySQL's and MariaDB's `BIT` and `BIT(1)` `z.boolean()`. The Snowflake and Databricks semi-structured types, typed forms included, are `z.json()`, MariaDB's `INET4` and `INET6` `z.ipv4()` and `z.ipv6()`, Databricks' `VOID` `z.null()` and SQL Server's `timestamp` and `rowversion`, which are binary, `z.base64()`. An ENUM, `enum('a','b')` under any database, is `z.enum([…])` of its members, each read as MySQL reads a string literal and listed once, a member losing its trailing spaces on MySQL and MariaDB, which drop them as they create the table, and keeping its tabs and leading spaces; one whose list does not parse is `z.string()`. A PostgreSQL array is one `z.array(…)` a dimension (`int[][]`, `integer ARRAY`), its elements never null. A time with zone, an interval, PostgreSQL's bit strings, `pg_lsn`, `inet`, `cidr`, `macaddr`, ranges and geometric types, its `xid`, `cid` and `xid8`, which node-postgres hands over as text, a SET, a spatial type and any type the classifier does not know are `z.string()`. `z.infer` of a schema is the interface `typescript.ts` writes for the table (an owner decision) but in two places: a JSON column is `unknown` there and Zod's `JSONType` here, and a table with no column `{}` there and `Record<string, never>` here.
    - **Names** follow the name cases. A type name TypeScript refuses for a type alias gets `_` after it, in the type and in its schema alike (`class_` and `class_Schema`, `z_Schema`): a reserved or strict mode word, a predefined type name (`RESERVED_TYPE_NAMES` in `jsonShape.ts`: `class`, `let`, `await`, `string`, `any`, …), `as`, and `z`, the namespace the schemas are imported under, none of which PascalCase makes. Any other name is written as is, as in `typescript.ts`: one that is no identifier (`order items`, `2fa`, an empty name), a global's (`Date`, which the output never names), or a repeated one, an owner decision of 2026-10-09, since a compile error shows the repeat, as it does two keys a name case makes alike (`user_id` and `userId` under camelCase, TS1117). A property key is bare where TypeScript reads an identifier name, a reserved word included (`class`, `default`), else a JSON string (`"order-no"`, `""`, `"2fa_enabled"`); `__proto__` is written `["__proto__"]`, since written plainly or quoted an object literal would set its prototype; a key is quoted too where it holds a code point from one of the 31 runs around the identifier characters Unicode 16.0 and 17.0 added (`NEWER_IDENTIFIER_RUNS`), TypeScript reading identifiers by Unicode 15.1 where Node 22 and 24 read 17.0. `jsonShape.test.ts` checks every code point of planes 0 to 3 and 14 against the scanner of `@typescript/typescript6`, so it fails once the repository's Node reads a newer Unicode, and the runs then need the new characters.
    - **Written as is, though a row of real data fails the schema or the file fails to compile:** zod before 4.0.0 (imported from `"zod"`, zod 3.25 is the v3 API, with no `z.iso` or `z.int32`); zod 4.0.0 to 4.4 counting `.max(n)` in UTF-16 units, where 4.5.0 and later count code points as PostgreSQL and MySQL do, so five U+1F600 fail `.max(5)` on 4.0.0; a `__proto__` key, which zod 4.6.5 neither checks nor returns, so `{}` passes a NOT NULL one, while zod 4.0.0 checks the row's own `__proto__`, or `Object.prototype` where the row has none, so `{}`, `{"__proto__": "x"}` and `{"__proto__": null}` fail a NOT NULL integer key, and returns none either; with `skipLibCheck` off a program that needs the DOM lib or `@types/node`, zod's declarations naming `URL`, and under `--isolatedDeclarations` each `export const …Schema`, which fails TS9010, as any Zod code does, TypeScript 7.0.2 adding TS9013; a name that is no identifier and a repeated one, and on a runtime past Unicode 17.0 a bare key holding a character added outside those runs, which tsc refuses (TS1127); a length past 2^53 − 1, written as the double reads it, rounded (`.max(100000000000000000000)`) or `Infinity` past its range, which both compile and parse, zod 4.6.5's `z.toJSONSchema` then writing `"maxLength": null`; `.max(n)` in code points, where SQL Server's `varchar(n)` and Oracle's `BYTE` lengths count bytes, SQL Server's `nvarchar(n)` and `nchar(n)` and Oracle's `NVARCHAR2(n)` UTF-16 units, and SQLite enforces no length, so a longer stored value fails; `z.int()`'s ±(2^53 − 1), so a 64-bit value past it fails: pg hands `int8` over as text, which fails raw and in JSON, mysql2 rounds `BIGINT` 2^63 − 1 to `9223372036854776000`, better-sqlite3's `safeIntegers` and node:sqlite's `readBigInts` give a `bigint`, which `JSON.stringify` refuses, and Oracle's bare `NUMBER`, a `z.int()` though it holds fractions, fails the `12.5` its `JSON_OBJECT` writes. The drivers: pg 8.23.1 and mysql2 3.24.5 read a DATE as a JS `Date` at local midnight, whose JSON shows the day before east of UTC and fails `z.iso.date()` (pg's `types.setTypeParser(1082, v => v)` and mysql2's `dateStrings: ['DATE']` keep the text); a timestamp read as a `Date` fails raw and passes as JSON, a naive one moved to the UTC instant; binary, and mysql2's `BIT`, come as Buffers, whose JSON (`{"type":"Buffer",…}`) fails `z.base64()`; mysql2, better-sqlite3 and node:sqlite read MySQL's, MariaDB's and SQLite's booleans as `1` and `0`, which `z.boolean()` refuses; pg hands over `interval` (and the elements of an `interval[]`), `point` and `circle` as objects, an array of a user type (`mood[]`) or of `bit` or `varbit` as text (`{sad,ok}`, `{101}`), and an array holding NULL as `[1, null]`, which fails, an element never being null; mysql2 hands every MySQL and MariaDB spatial type over as `{x, y}` objects or arrays of them, which `z.string()` refuses; SQLite's `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` and mysql2's `dateStrings` write a space for the `T`, which every `z.iso.datetime` refuses; a MySQL `TIME` past a day (`838:59:59`) or below zero (`-01:00:00`) fails `z.iso.time()`; MySQL and MariaDB store the ENUM error value `''` under `INSERT IGNORE` or a non-strict `sql_mode`, which `z.enum` refuses; a snake_case row fails the default camelCase keys, so validating database rows directly needs the `none` column case. The servers' own JSON (PostgreSQL's `row_to_json`, which PostgREST and Supabase build on, MySQL's and MariaDB's `JSON_OBJECT`, SQL Server's `FOR JSON`, Oracle's `JSON_OBJECT`) writes a DECIMAL as a number, which the owner kept a string, SQL Server its money and smallmoney too and PostgreSQL a `numeric[]`'s elements; MySQL and MariaDB write a DATETIME or TIMESTAMP with a space and a BOOLEAN as `1`, MySQL a `BIT` as `"base64:type16:…"` and binary as `"base64:type15:…"`, and MariaDB binary as raw bytes inside a string, a `BIT` unquoted, which is no JSON, and a character past U+FFFF as a broken `\uD83DDE00` escape; PostgreSQL writes `bytea` in hex (`"\\x0102"`) and Oracle `RAW` and `BLOB` in hex (`"0102FF"`), which `z.base64()` refuses, or reads as other bytes where the hex is also base64 (`"01020304"`); PostgreSQL writes `oid` as a string (`"12345"`), float `NaN` and `±Infinity` as strings, and `infinity` dates and timestamps, `24:00:00`, BC years (`"0044-03-15 BC"`), five-digit years and offsets with seconds (`+08:27:52`, Seoul's before 1908), all of which fail. The rest of SQL Server's JSON passes: binary in base64, an upper-case GUID through `z.guid()`.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-10 with Node 24.16.0: 8,744 files compiled with no error under TypeScript 5.9.3, 6.0.3 and 7.0.2 (strict, `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`, `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`, `skipLibCheck: false`) against zod 4.6.5 and 4.0.0, holding 296 type spellings (every name of the eight vendor lists, and forms with arguments), each as a NOT NULL, a nullable and a key column without the NN flag under each database, and the five `data/*.sql` fixtures under each database and four name-case pairs, whole and per table; every one of the 17,152 schemas loaded, and `z.toJSONSchema` converted it under both zod versions. 295,159 keys compiled under the three TypeScript versions, 18,081 of them quoted: every code point of planes 0 to 3 and 14 that Node reads as an identifier character; the names those versions refuse for a type alias and an interface are exactly `RESERVED_TYPE_NAMES`; 132 keyword and global table names and 84 odd keys compiled under four name cases but for the names written as is. Rows from PostgreSQL 17.11 (pg 8.23.1, session zone Asia/Seoul), MySQL 8.4.11 and MariaDB 11.4.13 (mysql2 3.24.5) and SQLite 3.53.4 (better-sqlite3 13.0.3), parsed raw and after `JSON.stringify`, and the servers' own JSON from those three, SQL Server 2022 (16.0.4295.3) and Oracle AI Database 26ai Free (23.26.3) failed only as Written as is says, with the same verdicts under zod 4.6.5 and 4.0.0 but for `.max(n)` and `__proto__`; the driver behaviour was first measured in the design research of 2026-10-09. Prettier 3.9.9 changed only the opening blank line and the lines past 80 columns (a long `z.enum([…])`, a long `z.infer` line), and the TypeScript review's 6,514 tables compiled `z.infer` equal to `typescript.ts`'s interfaces but for the two differences under Types.
  - `jsonSchema.ts` writes JSON Schema draft 2020-12 (owner decisions of 2026-10-09). The whole text is one document, `$schema` then `$defs`, a row schema per table keyed by its name in `tableNameCase`, in `orderByNameASC` order; its root takes any value, so a row is validated against `#/$defs/<Key>` or the one-table view. The one-table view is that row schema standing alone with `$schema` first, which `index.ts` takes from `createTableCode`. A document with no table writes nothing, as `zod.ts` does. A row schema is `title` (its key), `description` (the table comment), `"type": "object"`, `properties` in column order, `required` listing every key and `"additionalProperties": false`, so a row carries every key and no other, and a table with no column takes only `{}`; a column comment is its property's `description`, written first, and a blank comment writes none. A primary key or a NOT NULL column takes its type alone; any other adds `"null"` to its `type` (`"type": ["string", "null"]`) and `null` to its `enum`, and `{}` and `{"type": "null"}`, which take null already, stay as they are. No `$comment`, `$id`, `default`, `readOnly` or `$ref`: a foreign key is a plain column and no relation is written. The text is a blank line, the JSON and a newline, written from `Map`s (`formatJson`), since a plain object loses a `__proto__` key and moves integer-like keys first: two spaces a level, an object one key a line, and an array (`type`, `required`, `enum`) on one line while that line fits in 80 columns, its comma counted only where another key follows, else one item a line. Columns are counted as Prettier counts them a code point at a time (`textWidth`): two for an East Asian Wide or Fullwidth character (`WIDE_RUNS`, get-east-asian-width 1.6.0 as Prettier 3.9.9 bundles it) and for the nine text-default emoji that take a skin tone (`WIDE_EMOJI`, which Prettier's emoji pattern matches alone), none for a control, U+0300 to U+036F or a variation selector U+FE00 to U+FE0F, one for any other, so a line of Hangul breaks where Prettier breaks it.
    - **Types** come from the JSON shape `zod.ts` and `typescript.ts` read, so the three agree column by column: `boolean`; `integer` with `minimum` and `maximum` at the column's width (TINYINT −128 to 127, MEDIUMINT −8388608 to 8388607, an unsigned type, YEAR, SQL Server `tinyint` and PostgreSQL `oid` from 0) and none for a 64-bit integer, whose range no JSON number holds exactly (a 64-bit unsigned one, SERIAL included, keeps `"minimum": 0`), which the classifier makes of Oracle's `INT`, `INTEGER` and `SMALLINT` (a `NUMBER(38)`), an Oracle or Snowflake `NUMBER` with no fraction (`NUMBER(p)`, a scale of 0 or below, Snowflake's bare `NUMBER`), every Snowflake integer (a `NUMBER(38,0)`) and every SQLite integer (stored in 64 bits), and of Oracle's bare `NUMBER` and `NUMBER(*)` too, though they keep any scale (Written as is); `number` for a float and MariaDB's bare `NUMBER` (a DOUBLE); a decimal or money type a `string`, and SQLite's `DECIMAL` and `NUMERIC` a `number` (owner decision); a character type `string` with `maxLength` from its declared length (`varchar(n)`, `nvarchar(n)`, `VARCHAR2(n BYTE)`, Snowflake `STRING(n)`), none past 2^53 − 1, where a double rounds the length or reads it as Infinity, which `JSON.stringify` writes as null; a uuid, SQL Server's `uniqueidentifier` and MariaDB's `UUID` `"format": "uuid"`; a date `"format": "date"`; a zoned or instant timestamp (MySQL, MariaDB and Databricks `TIMESTAMP` too) `"format": "date-time"`; a time and a naive timestamp (Oracle `DATE` too) a `pattern` (`TIME_PATTERN`, `NAIVE_DATE_TIME_PATTERN`), since 2020-12's `time` and `date-time` formats require an offset, written with `[0-9]`, since `\d` takes any script's digits in a Python or .NET validator, the naive one taking the `Z` a driver's `Date` adds after seconds; binary `"contentEncoding": "base64"`; JSON and the semi-structured types `{}`; `BOOLEAN`, SQL Server's `bit` and MySQL's and MariaDB's `BIT` and `BIT(1)` `boolean`, a longer MySQL or MariaDB `BIT(n)` an `integer` from 0; an ENUM an `enum` of its members, each once; MariaDB `INET4` and `INET6` `"format": "ipv4"` and `"ipv6"`; Databricks `VOID` `{"type": "null"}`; a PostgreSQL array one `"type": "array"` with `items` a dimension, its elements never null. A time with zone, an interval, a bit string, PostgreSQL's `xid`, `cid`, `xid8`, `inet`, `cidr` and `macaddr`, a SET, a spatial type and any type the classifier does not know are a plain `string`.
    - **Names** are the name cases' output, any string a valid key (`''`, `__proto__`, `$ref`, `a/b~c`, `회원`), `JSON.stringify` escaping each. A repeated table key is numbered from 2, past every key a table takes as is (`user`, `User`, `user2` give `User`, `User3`, `User2`), its `title` numbered too; of a repeated property key only the first column is kept (owner decisions of 2026-10-09: JSON keeps one value a key, and the metaschema refuses a `required` entry listed twice). The one-table view is never numbered, so its `title` can differ from its key in the whole text.
    - **Written as is:** a `format` is checked only where the validator is told to: ajv throws on one under its default or strict options without ajv-formats (`unknown format "uuid" ignored in schema`), @hyperjump checks none until `@hyperjump/json-schema/formats` is imported and `setShouldValidateFormat(true)` is called, and Python jsonschema none without a `format_checker`; ajv-formats and @cfworker take a date-time with `+0900`, `+09`, a space for the `T` or `23:59:60Z`, and a `urn:uuid:` uuid, which @hyperjump and Python refuse, and @cfworker takes the ipv4 `010.0.0.1`; Python's `re` lets a time or naive timestamp end in a newline; the naive pattern checks month and day ranges, not month lengths, so February 30 passes where Zod refuses it; `contentEncoding` is an annotation, so binary takes any string, PostgreSQL's hex `bytea`, Oracle's `RAW` and `BLOB`, which its `JSON_OBJECT` writes in hex (`"0102FF"`), and MySQL's `base64:type15:…` included; a 64-bit integer has no bound, so the 2^63 − 1 mysql2 rounds passes where Zod refuses it; Oracle's bare `NUMBER` and `NUMBER(*)`, which keep any scale, are an unbounded `integer`, so the `12.5` such a column holds and its `JSON_OBJECT` writes fails; `maxLength` counts code points, where SQL Server `varchar(n)` and Oracle `BYTE` lengths count bytes, SQL Server `nvarchar(n)` and `nchar(n)` and Oracle `NVARCHAR2(n)` UTF-16 units, and SQLite nothing; a key named after an `Object.prototype` member mis-validates in ajv and @cfworker, where @hyperjump and Python are right: ajv by default takes an inherited member for a missing key (a `{}` one, or `__proto__` of any type), which `ownProperties: true` stops, and refuses a row holding `__proto__` in a table of at most nine columns, either way, but in a larger one takes it whatever its value, since ajv never validates a `__proto__` property, and @cfworker throws (`Instances of "function" type are not supported`) on a row lacking a key named after one of its methods (`constructor`, `toString`, `valueOf`, `hasOwnProperty`, …), whatever the key's type, and takes a row lacking a `{}` `__proto__` key; a table key `$id` (a table named `$id` under `NameCase.none`) makes ajv throw (`id.replace is not a function`) resolving any `#/$defs/<key>` of the whole text, where @hyperjump, @cfworker and Python reach every def; a table key holding `#` (`NameCase.none` alone keeps one) is out of reach of `$ref` in @hyperjump, and in @cfworker but through a raw `#`, and one holding `?`, `$`, `&`, `+`, `,`, `:`, `;`, `=` or `@`, which @cfworker does not percent-decode, is reached there only through the raw character; a lone surrogate in a column name makes ajv, @cfworker and @hyperjump throw `URIError` compiling its schema, and in a table name leaves its def unaddressable, since no URI encodes it, and either makes @cfworker throw on every def of the whole text, where ajv and @hyperjump reach the others; drivers hand over PostgreSQL `bigint` as text, a DATE as a JS `Date` (a day early once east of UTC), binary and mysql2's `BIT` as Buffer JSON, mysql2's `BOOLEAN` as `1`, PostgreSQL's `interval`, `point` and `circle` and the elements of an `interval[]` as objects, a `bit` or `varbit` array as text (`"{101}"`), mysql2's spatial types (`POINT`) as objects, a NULL array element as null, which an array's items refuse, and a naive timestamp as the UTC instant, which passes though its value moved; SQLite (node:sqlite), raw and through its `json_object`, gives a BOOLEAN as `1` and a `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` or `datetime()` value with a space for the `T`; the servers' own JSON (PostgreSQL `row_to_json`, which PostgREST and Supabase build on, MySQL and MariaDB `JSON_OBJECT`, SQL Server `FOR JSON`, Oracle `JSON_OBJECT`) writes DECIMAL as a number, which the owner kept a string, PostgreSQL's `oid` as a string, float `NaN` and `±Infinity` as strings, `infinity` dates, `24:00:00`, BC years and offsets with seconds, which all fail, MySQL and MariaDB a DATETIME or TIMESTAMP with a space and a BOOLEAN as `1`, MySQL a `BIT` as `base64:type16:…`, and MariaDB a `BIT` unquoted, which is no JSON, and a character past U+FFFF as a broken `\uD83DDE00` escape; MySQL and MariaDB store the ENUM error value `''` outside strict mode, and a TIME below zero or past a day; a ZWJ, skin-tone or tag sequence, or U+FE0F after a text-default emoji, counts a column a code point, where Prettier counts the sequence two, so such a line can break where Prettier keeps it, or the reverse.
    - **Measured** on 2026-10-10 with Node 24.16.0: 11,107 files (297 type spellings, every name of the eight vendor lists and forms with arguments, under each database as NOT NULL, nullable and key columns; the five `data/*.sql` fixtures under each database and three name-case pairs; names, comments and line-width probes; whole and per table: 21,892 row schemas) passed the 2020-12 metaschema in ajv 8.20.0 (`Ajv2020`), @hyperjump/json-schema 1.17.8 and Python jsonschema 4.25.1, compiled under ajv's strict and default options with ajv-formats 3.0.1 and in @cfworker/json-schema 4.1.1, and reached each def through `#/$defs/<key>` (a reserved character left raw for @cfworker), but for a key holding `#` and, in ajv, every def of a text holding a table key `$id`, as Written as is says. Prettier 3.9.9 changed only the opening blank line but on 28 width probes holding one of the emoji sequences above, and `textWidth` equals its `getStringWidth` on all 1,112,064 Unicode scalar values. 5,085 labelled values over the 883 distinct column schemas and 9,792 whole rows got their expected verdict from ajv with ajv-formats, @hyperjump and @cfworker with formats on, and Python with its format checker. A row lacking a key named after one of ten `Object.prototype` members, over twelve types, NOT NULL and nullable (240 schemas), and lone surrogates in a table or column name gave the verdicts Written as is names in ajv, @cfworker and @hyperjump, and Python refused all 240 rows. Against zod 4.6.5's `z.toJSONSchema` of the `zod.ts` schema of the same 9,282 tables, 69,042 of 85,020 columns were identical (17,108 after the nullable form), and the rest differed only by Zod's ±(2^53 − 1) on a 64-bit integer, its patterns beside `uuid`, `date`, `date-time`, the IP formats and base64 (with `format: base64`), its leap-aware naive timestamp pattern, `\d` where this writes `[0-9]`, its recursive schema where this writes `{}`, and the 72 lengths past 2^53 − 1 that `zod.ts` writes rounded or infinite. json-schema-to-typescript 16.0.0 typed each of those 85,020 keys as `typescript.ts` does (a type identity check a key under TypeScript 5.9.3 and 7.0.2, strict). Rows of PostgreSQL 17.11 (pg 8.23.1), MySQL 8.4.11 and MariaDB 11.4.13 (mysql2 3.24.5), through the driver and the server's own JSON, of SQLite 3.53.0 (node:sqlite) raw and through `json_object`, and Oracle AI Database 26ai Free (23.26.3)'s `JSON_OBJECT` of `NUMBER`, `NUMBER(*)`, `RAW` and `BLOB`, failed only as Written as is says; SQL Server 2022's own JSON, Oracle's decimals and both servers' length units were measured on 2026-10-09 and not run again. On 2026-10-10 a table key `$id`, keys holding `#`, `?`, `$`, `&`, `+`, `,`, `:`, `;`, `=` or `@`, and a NOT NULL `__proto__` column beside 0 to 82 others gave the verdicts Written as is names in ajv (default and strict options, and `ownProperties: true`), @cfworker, @hyperjump and Python, and a second corpus of 9,399 files (every vendor list type under each database as NOT NULL, nullable and key columns, 83 names under all 16 name-case pairs, comments, empty tables and the five fixtures under five name-case pairs, whole and per table: 18,324 row schemas) passed the metaschema in all three, changed under Prettier only by its opening blank line, had ajv resolve every def but the 332 of the four texts holding a table key `$id` and three of the text holding lone surrogates in its names, and gave 187,019 rows of the types, fixtures, comments and empty tables their expected verdict in ajv, @hyperjump and @cfworker.
  - `dbml.ts` repairs, since DBML rejects what others render oddly: duplicate tables renamed, typeless or repeated columns dropped (and the tables they empty), a `Ref` kept only when both ends resolve, identifiers double-quoted, comments as `Note`, a table color as `headercolor`, which takes `#rgb` or `#rrggbb` alone, so an alpha is dropped, an `rgb()` or `hsl()` is written as its hex and a color that reads as neither is left out (`utils/tableColor.ts`, owner decisions). The importer reads a `headercolor` back as written when it is `#rgb` or `#rrggbb`, joining the mark, number and name the tokenizer cuts `#3498DB` into while each touches the last (`Token.offset`), and drops any other, `#3498 DB` too; a `TablePartial`'s is not inherited. A group holding a table the file writes becomes a `TableGroup` after the refs, its `color` by the `headercolor` rule, its members one a line in table order under the names their tables are written under; DBML refuses two groups of one name, so each is named in document order, a blank one `group_1`, `group_2` and on and a taken one with `_2`, `_3` and on, never a name another group holds, and the blocks are sorted by the name written, so the text an import of it exports again is the same. The importer reads a `TableGroup` in any letter case, its name quoted or bare, one table a line resolved as a `Ref` resolves one (an alias, any letter case, the schema optional), into a group of its name and a `#rgb` or `#rrggbb` color, a table named by two staying in the first and a group naming no declared table made not at all; its note, inner or a setting, and its other settings go to `skipped` as `tablegroup note` and `tablegroup <key>`. Its rect is the placement's, which wraps it round its members (`importSchema.test.ts`). `dbml.test.ts` parses with `@dbml/parse` and round-trips the groups through the importer.
  - `aml.ts` repairs likewise but keeps typeless attributes, writes `nullable`, always quotes types, and writes a table color as `{color: name}`, one of the 18 names AML takes (`AML_COLORS`, Tailwind's 500 shades): `gray` for a color whose OKLab chroma is under 0.04, however light or dark (owner decisions; a neutral's hue is rounding noise, so by hue alone `#ffffff` and `#cccccc` would be `yellow` and `#000000` `pink`), else, of the other 17, the one whose 500 shade is nearest in OKLCH hue (`atan2(b, a)`) around the circle, the first in AML's order on a tie, lightness and chroma aside (owner decisions; full OKLab distance, pulled by lightness, named `#fecaca` `yellow` and `#0000ff` `indigo`), so `#fecaca` is `rose`, `#0000ff` `blue` and a dark `#800000` `red`, pinned in `tableColor.test.ts`. The importer reads a name back as its hex in any letter case (an owner decision), bare or quoted as every property value is read, and a quoted `#rgb`, `#rrggbb` or `#rrggbbaa` as written, and drops any other color, as it does the property on an attribute or a relation. DBML and AML highlight as `sql` (`LanguageToLangMap`).
  - `mermaid.ts` writes the `erDiagram` GitHub renders, whose Mermaid is 11.17.2 (an owner decision): every table name double-quoted, the characters a quoted entity name cannot hold dropped and a blank name written `unnamed`, as a blank column name is in its attribute and in a relationship's label; a column name or type outside that release's attribute word, one starting with a whitespace character the word holds (U+3000, U+FEFF), which the lexer's whitespace rule would eat, or one its key rule reads first (`pk`, `pk-id`, `uk번호`), in backticks; the type trimmed, `type?` for a column that is not NOT NULL, `unknown` for no type; a line break, U+2028 and U+2029 included, written as a space in a comment, a label and a backtick word, since mermaid strips a `%%` comment from the start of any line its multiline pattern finds; every `~` of an attribute line written `～` (U+FF5E) when the lexer's generic type rule, which it tries ahead of a backtick and a comment, would read from a word holding one to the line's last (a comment `"1~5, 6~10"`), the line kept as it is otherwise; every `%%{` written `%%｛` (U+FF5B) in a comment, the table comment's line included, a label and a backtick word, since one a word follows is a directive wherever it stands, which mermaid applies before its lexer runs, failing the parse, emptying the diagram (an unclosed one in the table comment takes the rest of the text) or changing its config, and rewriting the rest as well keeps the rule simple; a `direction` that whitespace and `TB`, `BT`, `RL` or `LR` follow, in any case, inside a longer word (`sort direction tbl`) and with semicolons anywhere in it (`direc;tion LR`), which mermaid's entity encoding can drop before its lexer runs, written with U+200B after it in a table name and a relationship label, the strings the lexer reads outside an entity block, where its direction rule takes the rest of the line, so a table name would fail to parse and a label drop its relationship and turn the diagram without an error: U+200B because `\s` matches the no-break space and not it, while attribute words and comments keep the word, since the lexer does not try that rule inside an entity block and mermaid drops the table comment's line before the lexer runs; the table comment as a `%%` line; a relationship's label the child columns still in the child table, since a removed column keeps its entity; every column whatever View Option shows, the name cases inert as in DBML and AML; per table (`formatTable`) an `erDiagram` header and the one entity under its numbered name, no relationships. Two inputs still reach mermaid as written: a backtick word or a table comment holding an HTML-like tag (`<x y="z">`), whose `="…"` mermaid's text clean-up rewrites to `='…'` up to the next `>`, a later line's included, so an unclosed one can take the opening quote of a name or comment after it and fail the parse; and a semicolon on a line holding `style` or `classDef`, then `:`, a run of non-whitespace and `#`: mermaid's entity encoding drops the line's last semicolon before its lexer runs, once for each of the two words, and writes a `#`, a word and `;` (`#b;`) in other characters, so a backtick word or a relationship line's entity name a drop empties (`;`) fails the parse, and a table name it changes on one of its lines and not the other (parent `style`, child `a:#b;`) becomes two entities, the relationship going to the empty one. `mermaid.browser.test.ts` parses the output with `mermaid` 11.17.2, a devDependency no build ships, and reads back what it parsed. Shiki's `mermaid` grammar colours only unquoted names, so the panel colours `erDiagram` and the comments, and the `o` and `{` of a child `o{`, which it reads as an unquoted entity opening a block.
  - `schema-sql/Snowflake.ts`: `bracketType` decides whether to quote, since a quoted name is case sensitive.
  - `schema-sql/`: a comment, and a name SQL Server's `sp_addextendedproperty` takes, is written through `toStringLiteral` (`utils.ts`), quotes doubled as the importer reads them, the `MS_Description` call included (`packages/schema-sql-parser/AGENTS.md`); `Databricks.ts` escapes a quote and a backslash with a backslash instead, the form every Spark release reads (only the newest read a doubled quote). The importer reads that form back by Spark's rules when the document's database is Databricks: `toSchemaImportJson` (which `loadSchemaSQLAction$` and a placed import share) hands `settings.database` to `schemaSQLParser`, every backslash then escapes the character after it, and a `DEFAULT` literal or a STRUCT field's `COMMENT` inside the type goes back as `'it\'s'`, so import, Databricks export and import again agree. Under any other database it keeps the guess: an escaped quote inside a word comes back as a quote, an escaped backslash keeps both, and a `DEFAULT` doubles its quotes.
  - `schema-sql/MSSQL.ts` names an `MS_Description` call's schema at level 0 as `'schema'`, its table at level 1 and its column at level 2 (an owner decision of 2026-10-04: SQL Server refuses `'table', 'dbo.users'` for the table an unquoted `CREATE TABLE dbo.users` makes; SQL Server 2022 ran this output the same day for `sales.users` and `users`, unquoted and double-quoted, and `sys.extended_properties` kept every comment on its table and column). An unquoted name splits into parts, the last the table and the one before it the schema, `dbo` where it gives none (the default schema of the database owner, which the export assumes). Two owner decisions of 2026-10-05 follow: each part loses one enclosing pair of brackets or double quotes (`[sales].[users]` gives `'sales'` and `'users'`, `[Order]` gives `'Order'`), and the part before the schema names the database whose own procedure the calls run (`EXECUTE shop.sys.sp_addextendedproperty`), since the levels name no database and a call acts on the database of its procedure, so a script connected to any database finds `shop.sales.users`. The rest reads a part as SQL Server does: a dot splits only outside a pair (`[sales.v2].users` is `users` in schema `sales.v2`; split there, `[sales` before the procedure would leave a bracket open that swallows every later batch); a doubled closing character inside the pair is one (`[x]]y]` gives `'x]y'`); the database goes before the procedure as typed (`[my shop].sys.…`), and only as a regular identifier or one delimited name, else it is dropped (`o'shop.o'sales.users`, whose `CREATE TABLE` fails too, would open a quote); a fourth part, which `CREATE TABLE` refuses (Msg 117), is dropped. SQL Server 2022 ran the output on 2026-10-05, connected to another database, for `[sales].[users]`, `"sales"."orders"`, `shop.sales.users`, `[my shop].[sales].[users]`, `[my.db].sales.users`, `[sales.v2].users`, `"sales.v2".users`, `[x.y.z]`, `[x]]y]` and `"x""y"`: `sys.extended_properties` kept every comment on its table and column, and the table after it in the script kept its own, where the earlier output failed with Msg 15135. Those tables had no key. A key's name takes the table part by the same split (`tableNamePart`, below): on 2026-10-05 SQL Server 2022 ran the export of `sales.users`, `[sales].[users]`, `"sales"."users"`, `[Order]` and, connected to another database, `shop.sales.users`, each beside an orders table, with a primary key, a unique column, a foreign key, indexes and comments, every statement passing and `sys.extended_properties` holding each comment on its table and column, where the `PK_[users]` and `PK_[Order]` an earlier split wrote failed with Msg 102. A quoted name goes whole to level 1 in `dbo`, the one table `CREATE TABLE "dbo.users"` makes, which reads back as `dbo.users`. Either way level 1 holds the name the importer gives the table (`sales.users` quoted; `users` for `[sales].[users]`, `[sales.v2].users` or `shop.sales.users`, `x.y.z` for `[x.y.z]`), so the comments survive the round trip, except `[x]]y]`, which the importer reads as a table `x` with no columns. A column name goes to level 2 as typed, so a bracketed one in an unquoted export (`'[Order Id]'`) is refused by SQL Server (Msg 15135) and matches no column the importer reads (`Order Id`), a case the owner has not decided. `findCommentTable` reads a call naming an unquoted `dbo.users` whole at level 1 by its last part, so a file an older export wrote (`'user', dbo, 'table', 'dbo.users'`, which SQL Server refuses) keeps its comments, its `CREATE TABLE dbo.users` reading back as `users`.
  - `schema-sql/`: a column default goes out as stored, but in parentheses where the database reads that expression only inside them (`formatDefault`, `utils.ts`), since the importer keeps a default without the parentheses around it (`packages/schema-sql-parser/AGENTS.md`): MySQL wraps anything but a literal or the current time (with an `ON UPDATE` written into the default by hand, which stays bare), and, from the column's type it is handed, any default but `NULL` on a BLOB, TEXT, JSON or spatial column (a type that opens with `LONG`, which MySQL reads as MEDIUMTEXT or MEDIUMBLOB, among them) and the current time on any column but a TIMESTAMP or DATETIME, which MySQL reads only in parentheses; MariaDB wraps anything but a literal, the current time, a function call or a name, SQLite anything but a literal or a name, and PostgreSQL only an expression holding a word its `DEFAULT` refuses outside them (`AT TIME ZONE`, `AND`, `IS`, `COLLATE`, `= ANY`, …). SQL Server, Oracle, Snowflake and Databricks take an expression bare, so their output is the stored text, and so is a default its database takes bare.
  - `schema-sql/`: an unquoted table name with a dot is schema and table, so SQL Server, Oracle, MySQL, MariaDB and PostgreSQL build an automatic constraint or index name from its last part (`tableNamePart`, `utils.ts`): `sales.users` writes `PK_users`, `UQ_users_email`, `FK_users_TO_orders` and `IDX_users`, which each of them refused with the dot in it. SQLite names no constraint, writing its primary key, unique and foreign key clauses bare, so the only automatic name it builds from the table part is `IDX_users`, which it refused with the dot in it too.
