The name splits by the rule `MSSQL.ts` reads its `MS_Description` levels with (`splitNameParts` and `unquoteNamePart`, which both use): at a dot outside brackets or double quotes, the last part losing one enclosing pair and reading a doubled closing character inside as one, so `[sales].[users]` and `"sales"."users"` write `PK_users` too, `[Order]`, which has no dot, `PK_Order`, and `[x.y.z]` stays one part, `PK_x.y.z`, which no database reads unquoted (SQL Server refuses its `CREATE TABLE` with Msg 102, as it refused the `PK_z]` an earlier split wrote). The schema and the table a statement names keep their delimiters as typed (`splitTableName`): Oracle's and SQLite's index, sequence and trigger qualifier (`"sales".IDX_users`, a quoted Oracle schema being case sensitive) and SQLite's `ON` and `REFERENCES` table (`"users"`). On 2026-10-05 the `"sales"."users"` export ran clean on PostgreSQL 17, Oracle 23ai Free (in a user `"sales"`, the trigger filling the key on insert), SQLite 3.54 and, under `ANSI_QUOTES`, MySQL 8.4 and MariaDB 11.8, each of which refused the `IDX_"users"` or `PK_"users"` an earlier split wrote. `splitsTableName` names those six for the Indexes tab's key rows (`getColumnKeys`), which keep the whole name under Snowflake and Databricks, as their DDL does. Two schemas holding one table name repeat the primary key and unique names, kept in the table's schema by every database that names them, and number the foreign key and index names across the export as before; SQL Server 2022, Oracle 23ai Free, MySQL 8.4, MariaDB 11, PostgreSQL 17 and SQLite ran that script clean. Those six number an automatic index name, and the five that name foreign keys an automatic foreign key name, that repeats an earlier one but for case too, quoted or not (`autoNameIgnoreCase`, `utils.ts`), as the owner decided: `sales.users` and `hr.Users` referenced from `sales.orders` write `FK_users_TO_orders` and `FK_Users_TO_orders1`, where those five refused the unnumbered pair and ran the numbered script clean, and SQLite, which writes no foreign key name, ran either script; MySQL and MariaDB refused it for a quoted `users` and `Users` too, since a foreign key's index name folds; PostgreSQL and Oracle ran that pair either way, and SQL Server and SQLite refuse its second table. A name differing in more than case keeps its bytes; Snowflake, Databricks, Oracle's sequence names, the code generators and the importers number through `autoName`, which compares exactly. Oracle writes every index on a dotted table beside it, automatic or named (`sales.IDX_users`, `sales.idx_users_name`), since unqualified it lands in the current user's schema, where one name given in two schemas clashes, and creates an `autoIncrement` column's sequence and trigger there too, named from the table part (`sales.SEQ_users`, `sales.SEQ_TRG_users`, whose body reads `sales.SEQ_users.NEXTVAL`; Oracle ran it as SYSTEM and as each schema's owner), the trigger name numbered against the sequence names, never the earlier triggers; SQLite writes `CREATE INDEX sales.IDX_users ON users` and `REFERENCES users`, the only spelling it reads, a foreign key resolving in the child table's schema; in both an index name the user already qualified keeps its own schema. A quoted name is one identifier and keeps the whole name (`"PK_sales.users"`).
  - `schema-sql/`: the eight SQL generators read a relationship pair by pair (`toForeignKeyPairs`, `utils.ts`). A pair one of whose columns has left its table is dropped whole and the rest stay paired; pairs referencing the start table's whole primary key follow its declaration order, the table's column order the PRIMARY KEY clause writes, and any other the stored order; a relationship whose table is out of the document, or with no pair left, is skipped, writing no constraint, using up no constraint name and leaving no blank line. MySQL refuses a foreign key naming a key's columns in another order (error 6125) and MariaDB too (errno 150), and a removed table or column made DDL reference what it never creates. SQLite counts the foreign keys it writes for the comma after the PRIMARY KEY clause and after the last column of a table with no primary key. `createSchemaSQL` given table ids (`toSchemaEntities`, what `mcp-server`'s `erd_read` with `tableIds` and the Schema SQL tab's Tables write) writes those tables, their indexes and the foreign keys they hold, judging a parent it leaves out by the whole document, so a reference out of the selection stays where a removed parent's does not. `seaorm.ts` reads them too, for its `from` and `to`; the other ORM generators and DBML, AML and Mermaid do not yet.
  - `schema-sql/index.ts`'s `createSchemaSQL(state, database?, tableIds?, options?)` writes the header, the before script, the DROP block, the tables and the after script, in that order, each without blank lines at its edges and one blank line between two (owner decisions of 2026-10-07); the blank line an index with no column left leaves at the tables' end is dropped only before an after script, so an output without one stays byte for byte what it was. With no options it writes Create and no header, which `getSchemaSQL()` and `mcp-server`'s `erd_read` do by default; the scripts go into the whole document's DDL and into that of some tables (`tableIds`) only under `scripts: true` (`CreateSchemaSQLOptions`, which the Schema SQL tab passes and `getSchemaSQL()`, picking the statements and header alone, and `erd_read` never do), `scripts: false` leaving them out of either, and a document with neither tables nor scripts writes `''` under any option. `options.ts` resolves the statements and header asked for per database (`schemaSQLSupport`, `resolveSchemaSQLOptions`): IF NOT EXISTS on MySQL, MariaDB, PostgreSQL, SQLite and Databricks, Create elsewhere; `use` alone on Oracle, `createAndUse` alone on PostgreSQL, neither on SQLite, `createAndUse` falling back to `use` and `use` to none. A header, and with it the schema PostgreSQL's DROP names a table in, is written only while `settings.databaseName` is a plain identifier (`HEADER_NAME_PATTERN`). `formatScript` writes a script with LF line breaks, no blank line ahead and no trailing whitespace, adding `GO` on SQL Server after one whose last line is no `GO` (or `GO n`); the text is otherwise the user's, never checked, and on Oracle a PL/SQL block's `/` is the user's to write. MySQL's IF NOT EXISTS is one batch between `FOREIGN_KEY_CHECKS` turned off and restored, each table holding its unique constraints, indexes and foreign keys inside its CREATE TABLE, named as the Create batch names them, since MySQL has no IF NOT EXISTS for them; MariaDB writes IF NOT EXISTS on each statement of its own batch, and PostgreSQL and Databricks drop a foreign key IF EXISTS before they add it back. Drop and re-create drops every table written before creating them: MySQL and MariaDB with the foreign key checks off, PostgreSQL in one `DROP TABLE IF EXISTS` that refuses a table another outside it references, SQL Server in one transaction (2016 or later) that first drops each foreign key a written table holds, read from the `sys.foreign_keys` of the database its name gives (`shop.sys.foreign_keys` for `shop.sales.users`, and only once `OBJECT_ID` finds the table, since a missing database's catalog fails past the `CATCH`, the transaction left open), so a key held from a table outside them still refuses, SQLite after `PRAGMA foreign_keys=OFF`, which stays off for the connection, Oracle in PL/SQL blocks that pass over ORA-00942 and ORA-02289, Databricks table by table, and Snowflake by `CREATE OR REPLACE TABLE`. `__fixtures__/options/` holds the bytes each combination writes, run on each database but Snowflake and Databricks, which follow their documentation, unmeasured, and beside them, each file named for the rule it shows, the bytes of a few rules on their own: Oracle's SQL*Plus triggers and names past 30 bytes, SQLite's AUTOINCREMENT, IDENTITY left off a key type PostgreSQL or Databricks does not take it on and kept on Databricks' BIGINT, and Snowflake's referential actions.
  - `schema-sql/Oracle.ts` writes each auto-increment trigger for SQL*Plus and SQLcl, a `/` line after its `END;` and `:NEW` in its `INTO`, and `oracleLongNames` lists the names it writes, for the document or the tables given, past the 30 bytes Oracle 12.1 and older allow, which the options panel shows, the output unchanged (owner decisions of 2026-10-07). An auto-increment column keeps its clause only where the database takes it, else loses it with a comment line after its table naming it (owner decisions of 2026-10-07): `SQLite.ts` writes the lone primary key column of an integer type as `INTEGER`, the one type AUTOINCREMENT runs on, and drops the keyword from one of any other type, `PostgreSQL.ts` writes IDENTITY only on `smallint`, `integer` or `bigint` by any name PostgreSQL gives them, and `Databricks.ts` only on `BIGINT` or its alias `LONG`, from its documentation.
  - A relationship's `onDelete` / `onUpdate` (`ReferentialAction`, `none` by default) write no clause while unset, so the output stays byte for byte what it was. `referentialActionSupport` (`schema-sql/utils.ts`) names per database what its DDL writes and drops the rest, leaving the database default: MySQL and MariaDB refuse SET DEFAULT, SQL Server has no RESTRICT, Oracle writes only ON DELETE CASCADE or SET NULL, Databricks and Snowflake only NO ACTION. Snowflake takes every action, yet creates no foreign key on a standard table whose action is another (from its documentation, not measured), so its DDL names what it left out in a comment above the `ALTER TABLE`. The ORM generators follow the same table for `settings.database`, `drizzle.ts` for the dialect it writes (PostgreSQL's where it borrows pg-core, which drizzle-kit turns into PostgreSQL DDL), `typeorm.ts` also leaving SET DEFAULT out (TypeORM writes it `DEFAULT`), `doctrine.ts` its ON DELETE alone, Doctrine having no ON UPDATE, `seaorm.ts` both as `on_update` and `on_delete`, and `jpa.ts` writes none (JPA has no option, and the generator stays plain JPA, never Hibernate's `@OnDelete`); `dbml.ts` and `aml.ts` write every action, the documents being vendor-neutral, and `aml.ts` writes a relationship with actions as a `rel` statement, since AML hands an inline relation's properties to its attribute; its `formatTable`, which writes no `rel` statement, keeps the relation inline without them. The relationship context menu offers every action and notes one the current database would drop (`not in MySQL`), so the document stays vendor-neutral and the user sees why the DDL has no clause.

## Dependencies

### Internal

`@dineug/r-html`, `@dineug/erd-editor-schema`, `@dineug/schema-sql-parser` and `@dineug/uuid`, whose `uuid25` mints every id the editor makes (inlined into `dist/`); `@dineug/vite-plugin-r-html` (the JSX transform every build and test config runs, plus HMR).

### External

- `konva` `^10.3.2` via `konva/lib/*` only; a caret so a consumer dedupes to one copy, since `batchDraw.ts` patches the namespace. `@chenglou/pretext` lays out memo text. `imports.test.ts` keeps `html-to-image` out.
- `elkjs` only from the elk-layout worker, `shiki` + `@shikijs/langs` / `themes` only from the shiki worker (`createHighlighterCore`, JavaScript regex engine, so no `wasm-unsafe-eval`); `comlink` on all four worker boundaries; `d3-force` for Graph mode and automatic placement.
- `rxjs`, `graphql` (only in `schema-graphql-parser/parser.ts`, which sits in the `loadValue` chunk all three entries share, so `peer.js` carries it too), `fuse.js`, `es-hangul` (the quick search's Hangul matching, `disassembleToGroups` alone, from `quick-search/hangul.ts`, which the peer graph never reaches), `lucide`, `@radix-ui/colors`; `stylis` for the inlined r-html. Key bindings, platform detection, word highlighting, deep merge and date math are written here, not taken from a dependency.
- `es-toolkit`: `get`, `set`, `isEmpty`, `round` come from `es-toolkit/compat` on purpose — the main entry lacks the first three and rounds exact `.xx5` ties down into persisted LWW state. Neither entry has an integer guard, so `clock.ts` and `tag.ts` spell `isNumber(x) && Number.isInteger(x)`.
